Unit 1 · Foundations for Mental Health Nursing · Chapter 1

Basic Mental Health Nursing Concepts

Mental health nursing provides care based on standards from the American Nurses Association, American Psychiatric Nurses Association, and International Society of Psychiatric-Mental Health Nurses. Care is grounded in the nursing process and a holistic approach integrating biological, social, psychological, and spiritual dimensions. The foundation of all psychiatric care is accurate assessment — beginning with the Mental Status Examination (MSE), an age-appropriate psychosocial history, and use of standardized screening tools when warranted. Every encounter is an assessment opportunity, and the nurse must always screen for safety: self-harm, harm to others, and risk for harm from others.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Mental health = the state in which a person realizes their abilities, copes with normal stresses, works productively, and contributes to community. Psychiatric care is holistic (bio-psycho-social-spiritual) and uses the nursing process. The MSE is the psychiatric equivalent of vital signs — assess appearance, behavior, speech, mood/affect, thought process, thought content, cognition, insight, and judgment. Use the DSM-5 for medical diagnostic criteria and NANDA for nursing diagnoses. Modify assessment for the lifespan: HEADSSS for adolescents, Geriatric Depression Scale and MMSE for older adults. Always assess for safety: self-harm, harm to others, risk of harm.

Mental Status Exam — 8 domains

  • Appearance — hygiene, grooming, dress, posture, apparent age
  • Behavior — eye contact, motor activity, mannerisms, cooperation
  • Speech — rate, volume, fluency, articulation, quantity
  • Mood & affect — subjective (mood) vs observed (affect)
  • Thought process — flow, logic, organization
  • Thought content — delusions, obsessions, suicidal/homicidal ideation, hallucinations
  • Cognition — LOC, orientation ×4, memory (immediate/recent/remote), attention
  • Insight & judgment — illness awareness, decision-making capacity

ADOPIE — standards of nursing practice

  • Assessment
  • Diagnosis (nursing)
  • Outcomes identification
  • Planning
  • Implementation
  • Evaluation

HEADSSS — psychosocial assessment for adolescents

  • Home environment — relationships with guardians, who lives at home, conflicts
  • Education/Employment — school performance, attendance, work
  • Activities — sports, hobbies, peer interaction
  • Drug and substance use — alcohol, tobacco, illicit drugs
  • Sexuality — sexual activity, partners, protection
  • Suicide/depression — risk for self-injury or suicide, signs of depression
  • Safety — exposure to abuse, violence, weapons

Levels of consciousness — quick reference

  • Alert — fully responsive, eyes open spontaneously, answers questions appropriately
  • Lethargic — drowsy, opens eyes but falls asleep readily
  • Stuporous — requires vigorous/painful stimuli (pinching tendon, sternal rub) to elicit brief response; may not respond verbally
  • Comatose — unconscious; no response to verbal or painful stimuli

Abnormal posturing in comatose clients:

  • Decorticate — flexion and internal rotation of upper extremities + leg extension
  • Decerebrate — neck and elbow extension, wrist and finger flexion (worse prognosis)
Memory hook: "A Big Sailor Must Try To Catch It" — Appearance, Behavior, Speech, Mood/affect, Thought (process & content), Cognition, Insight/judgment. Every psych assessment hits all eight.

Assessment

Each encounter with a mental health client involves ongoing assessment. The nurse uses observation, interviewing, physical examination, and collaboration to gather data. Mental health conditions can present with — or be caused by — underlying physical illness, so a focused physical assessment is part of every psychiatric workup.

Physical assessment

Rules out organic causes

Perform a physical assessment as indicated by client condition or policy. Mental health symptoms can be the first sign of an underlying medical condition (e.g., delirium from infection, agitation from hypoxia, mood changes from thyroid disease).

Approach

  • Use touch to communicate caring as appropriate; respect personal space if the client does not wish to be touched
  • Include the family or significant others as appropriate
  • Obtain a detailed medication history — including OTC, herbal, and recreational substances
  • After the interview, summarize and ask for client feedback
  • Each encounter is an assessment opportunity

Always screen for

  • Difficulty sleeping
  • Incontinence
  • Falls or other injuries
  • Depression and loss of energy
  • Dizziness
  • Substance use

Pain assessment tools

  • Visual Analogue Scale — 0–10 mark on a line
  • Wong-Baker FACES Pain Rating Scale — pediatric/cognitively impaired
  • Faces Pain Scale-Revised (FPS-R)
  • McGill Pain Questionnaire (MPQ) — qualitative descriptors
  • PAINAD — Pain Assessment in Advanced Dementia (non-verbal clients)

Psychosocial history

Client's lived experience

Assess the client's perception of their own health, beliefs about illness and wellness, and how they typically function and cope. Use this to identify both strengths and vulnerabilities.

Functioning & coping

  • Perception of own health, beliefs about illness/wellness
  • Activity and leisure — how the client passes time
  • Use of substances and any history of substance use disorder
  • Stress level and coping abilities — usual strategies, support systems
  • Knowledge of stress-reduction techniques
  • Ability to maintain housing and employment
  • Participation in community resources

Cultural beliefs & practices

  • Assess client's cultural health care beliefs, practices, and values
  • Cultural diet — does the client follow culture-specific food practices?
  • Cultural view of mental illness — how is diagnosis viewed in the client's culture?
  • Nurse awareness alleviates stereotyping and stigmatizing
  • Use a trained interpreter when needed — never family members for clinical content

Spiritual & religious beliefs

  • Spirituality — client's internal values, sense of morality, view of life's purpose. Does not require religion.
  • Religion — client's beliefs according to an organized set of patterns of worship and rituals
  • Spiritual/religious beliefs affect how the client finds meaning, hope, purpose, and peace
  • Assess support systems and help the client identify resources

Mental Status Examination (MSE)

The MSE is to psychiatric care what vital signs are to medical care — a structured snapshot of the client's current functioning across eight domains. Document observed behaviors, not just impressions or labels.

Level of consciousness & orientation

First and fastest

Document level of consciousness using standard terms (above). Then assess orientation to person, place, time, and situation (oriented ×4).

Orientation (×4)

  • Person — knows their own name and identity
  • Place — knows where they are (hospital, city)
  • Time — knows date, day, time of day
  • Situation — understands why they are here

Documentation example

"Client is alert and oriented ×3 (person, place, time); unable to articulate the reason for admission."

Physical appearance & behavior

Observable from first contact

Physical appearance

  • Personal hygiene and grooming
  • Nutritional status
  • Clothing choice — appropriate to weather, status, setting
  • Whether the client looks older than their stated age
  • Expected finding: well-kempt, clean, dressed appropriately for environment

Behavior

  • Voluntary and involuntary body movements (tics, tremors, mannerisms)
  • Eye contact — appropriate, avoidant, intense, staring
  • Motor activity — calm, restless, agitated, retarded
  • Cooperation with the interview
  • Posturing — note any abnormal positions

Speech

  • Rate — slow, pressured, rapid
  • Volume — loud, soft, monotone
  • Fluency and articulation
  • Quantity — poverty of speech, hyperverbal
  • Quality of language — meaningful, articulate, appropriate

Mood & affect

Internal vs observed emotion

Mood is what the client says they feel; affect is what the nurse observes. The two should be congruent — when they aren't, that's clinically significant.

Mood (subjective)

  • Client's self-reported emotional state
  • Document in client's own words: "I feel hopeless"
  • Examples: euthymic, depressed, anxious, irritable, euphoric

Affect (objective)

  • Nurse's observation of expressed emotion
  • Flat — no facial expression
  • Blunted — markedly reduced
  • Restricted — narrow range
  • Full / appropriate — normal range
  • Labile — rapidly shifting
  • Congruent / incongruent — matches stated mood or not

Thought process & content

How thinking flows and what it contains

Thought process

  • Logical / goal-directed — normal flow
  • Tangential — drifts off topic, never returns to original
  • Circumstantial — eventually returns to point but with excessive detail
  • Loose associations — illogical shifts between ideas; no clear connection
  • Flight of ideas — rapid, fragmented topic-jumping (mania)
  • Word salad — incoherent jumble of words

Thought content

  • Delusions — false fixed beliefs (persecutory, grandiose, somatic, referential)
  • Obsessions and preoccupations
  • Phobias — intense irrational fears
  • Suicidal ideation — always asked directly
  • Homicidal ideation — always asked directly
  • Hallucinations — perceptual experiences without external stimulus (auditory, visual, tactile, olfactory, gustatory)

Cognitive function

Thinking abilities

Assess memory across three timeframes and test specific cognitive abilities with practical tasks.

Memory

  • Immediate — repeat a series of numbers or list of objects right after presentation
  • Recent — recall events from the current day or purpose of the visit
  • Remote — verifiable facts from the past (birth date, mother's maiden name)

Calculation & attention

  • Count backward from 100 by 7s (serial 7s)
  • Spell WORLD backward
  • Repeat numbers forward and backward

Abstract thinking

  • Interpret a cliché or proverb: "A bird in the hand is worth two in the bush"
  • Identify similarities (apple/orange, car/airplane)
  • Concrete interpretation may indicate cognitive impairment or psychosis

Knowledge & fund of information

  • Knowledge of the client's own illness
  • General fund (recent presidents, current events)
  • Vocabulary and reading level appropriate to education

Insight & judgment

Awareness & decision-making

Insight

  • Awareness of own illness and need for treatment
  • Objective assessment of client's perception of their illness
  • Poor insight — denies illness, refuses treatment
  • Partial insight — acknowledges some aspects
  • Good insight — full awareness and engagement

Judgment

  • Capacity for sound decision-making
  • Test with hypothetical: "What would you do if there were a fire in your room?"
  • Logical, safe response = intact judgment
  • Impaired judgment = safety risk; may need supervised care

Standardized Screening Tools

Standardized rating scales add objectivity to evaluation and allow tracking of change over time. Choose tools based on the client's age and the specific concern.

Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE)

Cognitive status — all ages, especially older adults

A 30-point cognitive screen used to evaluate the following domains. A score < 24 typically suggests cognitive impairment, though cutoffs vary by education level.

Domains evaluated

  • Orientation — to time and place
  • Attention & calculation — count backward by 7s
  • Registration — repeat three objects
  • Recall — recall the same three objects after delay
  • Language — naming objects, following commands, ability to write a sentence, copying a design

Clinical use

  • Track cognitive decline over time
  • Differentiate dementia from depression (depression often improves with treatment; dementia progressive)
  • Establish baseline before initiating treatment
  • Cannot diagnose specific disorders alone — clinical context required

Geriatric Depression Scale (GDS)

Depression in older adults

A self-report yes/no questionnaire validated for depression screening in older adults. Available in 30-item and 15-item (short form) versions. Avoids somatic items that overlap with normal aging.

Michigan Alcoholism Screening Test — Geriatric Version (MAST-G)

Substance use in older adults

24-item yes/no screen designed for older adults; sensitive to drinking patterns that may not be flagged by general adult tools. Score ≥ 5 indicates probable alcohol problem.

Considerations Across the Lifespan

Mental health assessment must be adapted to the client's developmental stage. Children and adolescents need developmentally appropriate language and may rely on caregivers for collateral information. Older adults need sensory accommodations and lifespan-appropriate screening tools.

Children and adolescents

HEADSSS framework

Assessment includes temperament, social and environmental factors, cultural and religious concerns, and developmental level. The client should be the primary source of information, but caregivers can provide valuable collateral data.

Mental health in children

  • Mentally healthy children/adolescents trust others, view the world as safe, accurately interpret their environment, master developmental tasks, and use appropriate coping skills
  • Children experience many of the same mental health problems as adults
  • Disorders are not always easily diagnosed — children may lack ability to describe internal experience; "normal" varies widely by developmental stage
  • Always assess for mood, anxiety, developmental, behavioral, and eating disorders, plus risk for self-injury or suicide

HEADSSS — psychosocial assessment

  • Home — relationships with guardians, who lives at home
  • Education/Employment — school, performance, work
  • Activities — sports, hobbies, peer interaction
  • Drugs — alcohol, tobacco, illicit drugs
  • Sexuality — sexual activity, encounters
  • Suicide/depression — risk for self-injury, signs of depression
  • Safety — exposure to abuse or violence

Older adults

Comprehensive geriatric assessment

In addition to all the standard assessments, older adult assessment includes specific functional and environmental elements. Interview technique itself must be adapted to support sensory and mobility differences.

Additional areas to assess

  • Functional ability — independence in ADLs (dressing, bathing) and IADLs (household tasks)
  • Economic and social status
  • Environmental factors — stairways at home, fall risks, isolation
  • Medication review (polypharmacy, anticholinergic burden)

Standardized tools

  • Geriatric Depression Scale (short form)
  • Michigan Alcoholism Screening Test — Geriatric Version
  • MMSE

Interview technique — for ALL clients

  • Use a private, quiet space with adequate lighting (for vision/hearing impairment)
  • Make an introduction; determine the client's name preference
  • Stand or sit at the client's level — not standing over them in bed
  • Allow extra time; do not rush

Mental Health Diagnoses

Mental health professionals diagnose disorders using the DSM-5. Nurses use NANDA nursing diagnoses to guide care planning. Both work together — the medical diagnosis names the condition; the nursing diagnosis names the response that nursing care will address.

DSM-5 vs NANDA — two complementary systems

DSM-5 (medical)

  • Published by American Psychiatric Association
  • Establishes diagnostic criteria for individual mental health disorders
  • Identifies expected assessment findings
  • Used by nurses to plan, implement, and evaluate care
  • Does NOT include client education handouts
  • Does NOT indicate pharmacological treatment

NANDA (nursing)

  • North American Nursing Diagnosis Association
  • Provides the basis for appropriate nursing interventions
  • Names the client's response to actual or potential health problems
  • Examples: Risk for self-directed violence, Ineffective coping, Disturbed thought processes

Serious mental illness (SMI)

Severe and persistent

SMI includes disorders classified as severe and persistent mental illnesses. Clients with SMI often have difficulty with ADLs, and these are typically lifelong disorders with remissions and exacerbations. Care planning addresses long-term functional support, not just acute stabilization.

Therapeutic Strategies in the Mental Health Setting

Mental health nursing interventions fall into recognized categories. NCLEX items typically target one type — identify which category is being asked about.

Counseling

  • Using therapeutic communication skills
  • Assisting with problem solving
  • Crisis intervention
  • Stress management

Milieu therapy

The setting is the intervention
  • Orienting the client to the physical setting
  • Identifying rules and boundaries of the setting
  • Ensuring a safe environment for the client
  • Assisting the client to participate in appropriate activities

Promotion of self-care activities

  • Offering assistance with self-care tasks
  • Allowing time for the client to complete self-care tasks
  • Setting incentives to promote client self-care

Psychobiological interventions

  • Administering prescribed medications
  • Providing teaching to the client/family about medications
  • Monitoring for adverse effects and effectiveness of pharmacological therapy

Cognitive and behavioral therapies

  • Modeling — demonstrating desired behavior
  • Operant conditioning — reinforcement-based behavior change
  • Systematic desensitization — gradual exposure to feared stimulus (anxiety/phobias)

Health teaching, promotion, and case management

Health teaching

  • Teaching social and coping skills
  • Education about the disorder and treatment

Health promotion & maintenance

  • Assisting with smoking cessation
  • Monitoring other health conditions (comorbidities)
  • Lifestyle and wellness counseling

Case management

  • Coordinating holistic care to include medical, mental health, and social services
  • Continuity across providers and settings

Role and Life Changes

Role transitions are common contributors to mental health concerns. Some changes are predicted (planned retirement); others arrive unexpectedly (sudden illness, becoming a caregiver, widowhood). Adaptation depends on the client's resources, support, and coping skills.

Common role transitions

  • Loss of employment
  • Divorce
  • Retirement
  • Grand-parenthood
  • Widowhood
  • Death of a guardian
  • Becoming a caregiver or recipient of care

Assessing ability to adapt and cope

Factors to assess

  • Health status and functional abilities
  • Living arrangements and employability
  • Personality factors (attitudes, resilience)
  • Client, caregiver, and family assessments
  • Levels of information (knowledge of community programs)
  • Medication use and supplemental services

Evidence of successful adaptation

  • States positive coping behaviors
  • Identifies maladaptive coping behaviors
  • Participates in community resources
  • Lists stress reduction techniques
  • Maintains housing and employment

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Older adult with depression — initial admission

Basic Concept · Comprehensive assessment

A nurse is admitting an older adult client who has depression to an acute mental health facility. Use the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept to address the following.

Underlying principles

Identify the standardized assessment tool for depression severity in this client:

  • Geriatric Depression Scale (GDS) — short form or 30-item; avoids somatic items that overlap with normal aging

Identify at least four assessment/communication techniques for this client:

  • Use a private, quiet space with adequate lighting to accommodate impaired vision and hearing
  • Make an introduction and determine the client's name preference
  • Stand or sit at the client's level rather than standing over them
  • Use touch to communicate caring as appropriate (respect personal space)
  • Include questions about sleep, incontinence, falls, depression, dizziness, and loss of energy
  • Include family and significant others as appropriate
  • Obtain a detailed medication history
  • Summarize and ask for feedback at the end of the interview

Nursing interventions — assess role and life changes

Identify at least three factors to assess for role-related contributors to depression:

  • Recent role transitions (predicted or unexpected) — retirement, widowhood, becoming a recipient of care
  • Client's knowledge and use of positive coping behaviors
  • Participation in community resources
  • Knowledge and use of stress reduction techniques
  • Ability to maintain housing or employment

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 1.1 — Mental Status Examination (Select All That Apply)

A charge nurse is discussing mental status examinations with a newly licensed nurse. Which of the following statements by the newly licensed nurse indicates an understanding of the teaching? Select all that apply.

  1. "To assess cognitive ability, I should ask the client to count backward by sevens."
  2. "To assess affect, I should observe the client's facial expression."
  3. "To assess language ability, I should instruct the client to write a sentence."
  4. "To assess remote memory, I should have the client repeat a list of objects."
  5. "To assess the client's abstract thinking, I should ask the client to identify our most recent presidents."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, B, C

  • A. Correct — Counting backward by 7s assesses cognitive ability (attention and calculation).
  • B. Correct — Affect is assessed by observing the client's facial expression and outward expression of emotion.
  • C. Correct — Writing a sentence is an indication of language ability.
  • D. Incorrect — Asking the client to repeat a list of objects assesses immediate memory, not remote memory. Remote memory is verifiable facts from the distant past.
  • E. Incorrect — Identifying recent presidents assesses general fund of knowledge, not abstract thinking. Abstract thinking is tested with proverb interpretation or identifying similarities.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 1.2 — Psychobiological intervention

A nurse is planning care for a client who has a mental health disorder. Which of the following actions should the nurse include as a psychobiological intervention?

  1. Assist the client with systematic desensitization therapy.
  2. Teach the client appropriate coping mechanisms.
  3. Assess the client for comorbid health conditions.
  4. Monitor the client for adverse effects of medications.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D — Monitoring adverse effects of psychotropic medications is a classic psychobiological intervention. Psychobiological interventions address the biological underpinnings of mental health and the medications/somatic treatments affecting them.

  • A. Systematic desensitization is a cognitive and behavioral intervention.
  • B. Teaching coping mechanisms is counseling / health teaching.
  • C. Assessing for comorbidities is health promotion and maintenance.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 1.3 — Priority assessment

A nurse in an outpatient mental health clinic is preparing to conduct an initial client interview. When conducting the interview, which of the following actions should the nurse identify as the priority?

  1. Coordinate holistic care with social services.
  2. Identify the client's perception of their mental health status.
  3. Include the client's family in the interview.
  4. Teach the client about their current mental health disorder.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — Assessment is the priority when using the nursing process. Identifying the client's perception of their mental health status provides important psychosocial information that guides all subsequent care decisions.

  • A. Case management coordination is appropriate but not the priority at initial interview.
  • C. Family involvement may be appropriate but is secondary to direct client assessment.
  • D. Client teaching is part of implementation — it follows assessment, diagnosis, and planning.

NCLEX® Connection: Health Promotion and Maintenance — Health Screening

Exercise 1.4 — DSM-5 use (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is planning a peer group discussion about the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, 5th Edition (DSM-5). Which of the following information is appropriate to include in the discussion? Select all that apply.

  1. The DSM-5 includes client education handouts for mental health disorders.
  2. The DSM-5 establishes diagnostic criteria for individual mental health disorders.
  3. The DSM-5 indicates recommended pharmacological treatment for mental health disorders.
  4. The DSM-5 assists nurses in planning care for clients who have mental health disorders.
  5. The DSM-5 indicates expected assessment findings of mental health disorders.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, D, E

  • A. Incorrect — The DSM-5 is a clinical reference, not a client education resource.
  • B. Correct — Establishing diagnostic criteria is the DSM-5's primary purpose.
  • C. Incorrect — The DSM-5 does not prescribe medications.
  • D. Correct — Nurses apply the nursing process using DSM-5 criteria to plan, implement, and evaluate care.
  • E. Correct — Expected findings (symptoms, presentation) are catalogued in the DSM-5.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 1.5 — Adolescent psychosocial assessment

A nurse in a primary care clinic is conducting a psychosocial assessment of a 16-year-old client using the HEADSSS framework. Which of the following questions assesses the "A" component?

  1. "Do you feel safe at home?"
  2. "Do you participate in any sports or clubs at school?"
  3. "How are your grades this semester?"
  4. "Have you ever thought about hurting yourself?"
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — The "A" in HEADSSS stands for Activities. The nurse assesses sports, hobbies, and peer interaction to understand the client's engagement, social connection, and outlets for stress relief.

  • A. Safety at home falls under the second "S" (Safety) — exposure to abuse or violence.
  • C. School performance falls under "E" (Education/Employment).
  • D. Thoughts of self-harm fall under the first "S" (Suicide/depression).

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Unit 1 · Foundations for Mental Health Nursing · Chapter 2

Legal and Ethical Issues

A nurse who works in the mental health setting is responsible for practicing ethically, competently, safely, and consistently with all local, state, and federal laws. Mental health clients are guaranteed the same civil rights as any other citizen plus specific rights tied to their treatment status. The nurse must understand the five bioethical principles (beneficence, autonomy, justice, fidelity, veracity), HIPAA confidentiality rules, the legal categories of admission, the strict rules governing seclusion and restraint, and tort law (intentional vs unintentional). State laws vary — the nurse is responsible for knowing the laws of the state(s) in which they practice.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Mental health clients keep all civil rights. Specific protected rights: informed consent, refusal of treatment, confidentiality, written plan of care, communication with the outside, interpretive services, dignity, freedom from harm, psychiatric advance directives, and care with the least restrictive interventions. The nurse must apply the five bioethical principles to every dilemma. HIPAA protects information; break confidentiality only for duty to warn or mandated abuse reporting. Five admission types range from voluntary to long-term involuntary. Restraints are a LAST resort — verbal > redirection > PRN meds > seclusion > mechanical restraint; provider order required; strict age-based time limits; documentation every 15–30 min.

Five bioethical principles

  • Beneficence — doing good
  • Autonomy — client's right to decide
  • Justice — fair, equal treatment
  • Fidelity — loyalty and faithfulness
  • Veracity — honesty with the client

Restraint time limits by age

  • ≥ 18 years — up to 4 hr
  • 9–17 years — up to 2 hr
  • ≤ 8 years — up to 1 hr
  • Provider must reassess and rewrite prescription every 24 hr
  • Documentation every 15–30 min

Five types of admission — least to most restrictive

  1. Informal — client free to leave anytime, even AMA; no danger to self/others
  2. Voluntary — client or guardian chooses admission; client retains right to refuse treatment
  3. Temporary emergency — emergent care, ≤ 15 days (varies by state); for inability to make decisions
  4. Involuntary — against client's will, indefinite; mental illness + danger + need for treatment; ≤ 60 days, then legal/psych review
  5. Long-term involuntary — court-imposed, 60–180 days or no set release date

Less restrictive interventions BEFORE restraint — try in order

  1. Verbal interventions (encourage client to calm down)
  2. Diversion or redirection
  3. Calm, quiet, reduced-stimulation environment
  4. Offer a PRN medication (chemical, but less restrictive than mechanical)
  5. Monitored seclusion room
  6. Mechanical restraints — only if all else fails
Memory hook: "BAJFV — Big Animals Just Fight Viciously" for the bioethical principles. For torts: intentional = "I meant to" (assault, battery, false imprisonment); unintentional = "I didn't mean to but I had a duty" (negligence, malpractice).

Legal Rights of Clients in the Mental Health Setting

Clients diagnosed with a mental health disorder or receiving acute mental health care are guaranteed the same civil rights as any other citizen. They also have additional specific rights tied to their care. State laws vary — the nurse is responsible for knowing applicable state laws.

Preserved civil rights

Same as any citizen
  • The right to humane treatment and care — medical and dental care
  • The right to vote
  • Rights related to granting, forfeiture, or denial of a driver's license
  • The right to due process of law, including pressing legal charges against another person

Specific mental health client rights

Tied to treatment status

Treatment-related

  • Informed consent and the right to refuse treatment
  • A written plan of care/treatment with discharge follow-up; participation in the plan and its review
  • Provision of care with the least restrictive interventions necessary to meet needs without allowing the client to be a threat to self or others
  • A psychiatric advance directive including treatment preferences in the event of involuntary admission

Communication & access

  • Communication with people outside the facility — family, attorneys, other health care professionals
  • Provision of adequate interpretive services if needed

Dignity & safety

  • Confidentiality
  • Care provided with respect, dignity, and without discrimination
  • Freedom from harm related to physical or pharmacological restraint, seclusion, and any physical or mental abuse or neglect

Tort law — overview

Civil wrongdoing

A tort is a wrongful act or injury committed by an entity or person against another person or another person's property. Torts decide liability issues and intentional issues that can involve criminal penalties (e.g., abuse of a client). Detailed below in Tort Law in the Mental Health Setting.

Ethical Principles in the Mental Health Setting

In contrast to laws (enacted by government), ethical issues are philosophical ideas regarding right and wrong. Nurses face ethical dilemmas — situations with conflict between two or more courses of action — and resolve them using the five bioethical principles. Because ethics are philosophical and involve values, there is rarely a clear-cut, simple resolution.

Beneficence

The quality of doing good

Beneficence can be described as charity. The nurse acts in ways that benefit the client.

Example

A nurse helps a newly admitted client who has a psychotic disorder to feel safe in the environment of the mental health facility.

Autonomy

The client's right to make their own decisions

The client must accept the consequences of their decisions and must respect the decisions of others.

Example

Rather than giving advice to a client who has difficulty making decisions, a nurse helps the client explore all alternatives and arrive at a choice.

Justice

Fair and equal treatment for all

Example

During a treatment team meeting, a nurse leads a discussion regarding whether two clients who broke the same facility rule were treated equally.

Fidelity

Loyalty and faithfulness to the client and one's duty

Example

A client asks a nurse to be present when they talk to their guardian for the first time in a year. The nurse remains with the client during this interaction.

Veracity

Honesty when dealing with a client

Example

A client states, "You and that other staff member were talking about me, weren't you?" The nurse truthfully replies, "We were discussing ways to help you relate to the other clients in a more positive way."

Confidentiality

The client's right to privacy is protected by the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) Privacy Rule of 2003. Mental health information is treated with especially high protection. The nurse must understand both federal and state laws applicable to their facility.

Routine confidentiality practice

Do

  • Share information (verbal or written) only with those responsible for implementing the client's treatment plan
  • Share with other persons not on the team only with client consent
  • Take immediate action to stop a privacy violation (e.g., overheard elevator conversation)

Don't

  • Discuss client information in public places (hallways, elevators, cafeteria)
  • Use social media to discuss clients or their information — ever
  • Share with family without consent

Exceptions — when to break confidentiality

Mandated disclosure

Health care professionals can — and in some cases must — break confidentiality in specific mental health situations.

  • Duty to warn and protect third parties — if the client has made a credible threat against an identifiable person, the nurse/provider must warn that person
  • Mandated reporting of child abuse
  • Mandated reporting of vulnerable adult abuse (older adults, dependent adults)

Resources for solving ethical client issues

  • Code of Ethics for Nurses — American Nurses Association website
  • Patient Care Partnership — American Hospital Association website
  • The Nurse Practice Act of the specific state
  • Legal advice from attorneys
  • Facility policies
  • Other members of the health care team, including the facility bioethics committee
  • Members of the clergy and other spiritual or ethical counselors

Types of Admission to a Mental Health Facility

Five admission types exist on a spectrum from least to most restrictive. The type used depends on the client's competence, risk, and willingness to receive treatment.

Informal admission

Least restrictive

Criteria

  • Client does not pose a substantial threat to self or others
  • Client is free to leave the hospital at any time, even against medical advice (AMA)

Voluntary admission

Client-initiated

Process

  • Client or client's guardian chooses admission to obtain treatment
  • Client is considered competent
  • Client retains the right to refuse medication and treatment
  • Before release, client can be evaluated; if necessary, the care provider can initiate an involuntary admission

Temporary emergency admission

Acute, time-limited

Criteria & process

  • Client admitted for emergent mental health care due to inability to make decisions regarding care
  • Medical health care provider can initiate; evaluated by a mental health care provider
  • Length varies by need and state laws — often not to exceed 15 days

Involuntary admission

Against client's will, indefinite

Based on the client's need for psychiatric treatment, the risk of harm to self or others, or the inability to provide self-care.

Criteria — all must apply

  • Presence of mental illness
  • Poses a danger to self or others
  • Demonstrates severe disability or inability to meet basic necessities (food, clothing, shelter)
  • Requires treatment but unable to seek it voluntarily related to the impact of the mental illness

Process

  • Number of physicians required to certify varies by state (usually two)
  • Can be imposed by family member, legal guardian, primary care provider, or mental health provider
  • Client can request legal review of the admission at any time
  • Limited to 60 days; psychiatric and legal review required at that point

Rights retained

  • Clients admitted under involuntary commitment are still considered competent
  • Retain the right to refuse treatment, including medication
  • Client judged incompetent has a temporary or permanent guardian (usually a family member) appointed by the court
  • The guardian signs informed consent and is expected to consider what the client would want if still competent

Long-term involuntary admission

Court-imposed
  • Similar to temporary commitment but must be imposed by the courts
  • Time of commitment varies but is usually 60 to 180 days
  • Sometimes no set release date

Client Rights Regarding Seclusion and Restraint

Restraints and seclusion are governed by strict federal/state/facility policies. The provider should prescribe seclusion and/or restraint for the shortest duration necessary, and only if less restrictive measures are insufficient. They exist for the physical protection of the client and/or other clients and staff — never for staff convenience or punishment.

Types of restraint

Physical (mechanical)

Devices that restrict movement — wrist/ankle straps, vests, etc.

Chemical

Neuroleptic medication used specifically to calm the client. Considered less restrictive than mechanical, though still a restraint.

Timeout (NOT restraint)

A voluntary, client-requested temporary timeout when the environment is too stimulating. Different from prescribed seclusion — initiated by client request.

Less restrictive measures — try FIRST in this order

Restraint is the last resort
  1. Verbal interventions — encourage the client to calm down
  2. Diversion or redirection
  3. Calm, quiet environment — reduce stimulation
  4. Offer a PRN medication — technically a chemical restraint, but less restrictive than mechanical
  5. Monitored seclusion room
  6. Mechanical restraint — only if all of the above fail

When NEVER to use seclusion or restraint

Absolute contraindications
  • Convenience of the staff
  • Punishment of the client
  • Clients who are extremely physically or mentally unstable
  • Clients who cannot tolerate the decreased stimulation of a seclusion room

Requirements when seclusion or restraint IS used

Prescription

  • The provider must prescribe in writing
  • Time limits are based on the client's age
  • Provider must reassess and rewrite the prescription, specifying the type of restraint, every 24 hr or as facility policy specifies

Time limits by age

  • ≥ 18 years — up to 4 hr
  • 9 to 17 years — up to 2 hr
  • ≤ 8 years — up to 1 hr

Emergency use

  • The nurse can initiate seclusion/restraint in an emergency without a written prescription
  • Written prescription must be obtained within 15–30 min
  • Discontinue when client exhibits safer, quieter behavior
  • If reapplication needed, obtain a new prescription

Nursing responsibilities during restraint

Ongoing monitoring

The facility protocol identifies the nursing responsibilities. The client should be:

Frequency-based care

  • Assessed for safety and physical needs (behavior documented)
  • Offered food and fluid
  • Toileted
  • Monitored for vital signs
  • Monitored for pain

Documentation every 15–30 min

Includes:

  • Precipitating events and behavior prior to seclusion or restraint
  • Alternative actions taken to avoid seclusion or restraint
  • Time treatment began
  • Current behavior; foods/fluids offered and taken; needs provided for; vital signs
  • Medication administration
  • Time released from restraints

Tort Law in the Mental Health Setting

A tort is a civil wrongdoing in which monetary damages can potentially be awarded to the plaintiff (injured party) and collected from the defendant (responsible party). Torts are particularly likely in mental health settings due to the increased likelihood of violence and challenging client behavior.

Intentional torts

Willful actions that violate rights

Intentional torts are willful actions that damage a client's property or violate client rights.

False imprisonment

Confining a client to a specific area (e.g., a seclusion room) physically, verbally, or using a chemical restraint when it is not part of the client's treatment (i.e., not to prevent harm to self/others).

Example: Putting a client in seclusion overnight because the unit is short-staffed.

Assault

Making a threat to a client's person.

Example: Approaching the client in a threatening manner with a syringe in hand.

Battery

Touching a client in a harmful or offensive way.

Example: The nurse threatening with a syringe actually grabs the client and gives an injection against the client's will.

Invasion of privacy

Sharing or obtaining the client's confidential information without consent.

Unintentional torts

Failure of duty

Actions or inactions that cause unintended harm as a result of failing to meet one's duty of care.

Negligence

Failing to provide adequate care in a personal or professional situation when one has an obligation to do so.

To be liable for negligence, all four elements must be proven:

  1. Duty — the professional had a duty to protect
  2. Breach — the duty was breached
  3. Causation — the action or failure to act caused injury (proximate cause + cause in fact)
  4. Damages — actual damages occurred

Malpractice

A type of professional negligence. Applies when the negligent party is acting in a professional capacity.

Documentation of Violent or Unusual Episodes

When violent or unusual incidents occur, documentation must be clear, objective, and specific. Document behaviors observed — not subjective interpretations.

Three documentation domains

1. Client behavior — objectively

Example: "The client suddenly began to run down the hall with both hands in the air, screaming obscenities." (NOT: "The client acted out.")

2. Staff response

Document response to disruptive, violent, or potentially harmful behavior (suicide threats, potential or actual harm to others) — including timelines and extent.

Example: "Client picked up a chair and held it over their head 3 ft from another client. Nurse said, 'Put down the chair, and back away.' Other client moved to safe area. Five staff responded within 30 seconds and stood several yards from client. Client put the chair down, walked to room, and sat on the bed."

3. Provider notification

Document the time the nurse notified the provider and any prescriptions received.

Objective vs subjective — examples

✓ Objective — do this

  • "Client at 70% of their breakfast"
  • "Client was offered 8 oz of water every hr"
  • "Client shouted obscenities at assistive personnel"
  • "Client received chlorpromazine 15 mg by mouth at 1000"

✗ Subjective — avoid this

  • "Client ate most of their breakfast" (vague)
  • "Client acted out after lunch" (interpretive, not observed)

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Violent client with bipolar disorder — escalation to restraints

Basic Concept · Least restrictive interventions

A nurse in a mental health facility is caring for an adult client who has bipolar disorder. The client becomes violent and begins throwing objects at other clients. After calling for assistance, what actions should the nurse take next? Use the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept to complete this item.

Nursing interventions — at least four actions before mechanical restraints (with rationales)

  1. Tell the client calmly to sit down.

    Verbal intervention is the least restrictive method when dealing with an aggressive client.

  2. Provide a decreased-stimulation environment and attempt diversion or redirection.

    These interventions are less restrictive than seclusion or restraint and should be attempted prior to more restrictive actions.

  3. Offer the client a PRN medication (e.g., diazepam).

    It can be necessary for the nurse to administer diazepam to calm the client; this is considered less restrictive than mechanical restraints.

  4. Place the client in a monitored seclusion room.

    It can become necessary to place the client in seclusion if the client persists in the behavior after attempting less restrictive interventions.

  5. If other interventions are unsuccessful, obtain a prescription for mechanical restraints.

    Follow facility policy for application, monitoring, and documentation.

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 2.1 — Temporary emergency admission criteria

A nurse in an emergency mental health facility is caring for a group of clients. The nurse should identify that which of the following clients requires a temporary emergency admission?

  1. A client who has schizophrenia with delusions of grandeur
  2. A client who has manifestations of depression and attempted suicide a year ago
  3. A client who has borderline personality disorder and assaulted a homeless man with a metal rod
  4. A client who has bipolar disorder and paces quickly around the room while talking to themselves
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — A client who is a current danger to self or others is a candidate for a temporary emergency admission. The recent assault meets this threshold.

  • A. Delusions alone don't constitute a clear reason unless they present a current danger.
  • B. A suicide attempt one year ago is historical; without current risk, doesn't justify emergency admission.
  • D. Pacing and self-talk are behaviors but don't meet the danger-to-self-or-others threshold.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Crisis Intervention

Exercise 2.2 — Tort identification

A nurse decides to put a client who has a psychotic disorder in seclusion overnight because the unit is very short-staffed, and the client frequently fights with other clients. The nurse's actions are an example of which of the following torts?

  1. Invasion of privacy
  2. False imprisonment
  3. Assault
  4. Battery
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — False imprisonment is confining a client to a specific area (a seclusion room) when the reason is for the convenience of staff, not for the client's treatment needs. Seclusion is never appropriate for staffing reasons.

  • A. Invasion of privacy is sharing/obtaining confidential information without consent.
  • C. Assault is making a threat to the client's person.
  • D. Battery involves causing intentional, physical harm.

NCLEX® Connection: Safety and Infection Control — Accident/Error/Injury Prevention

Exercise 2.3 — Confidentiality & veracity

A client tells a nurse, "Don't tell anyone, but I hid a sharp knife under my mattress in order to protect myself from my roommate, who is always threatening me." Which of the following actions should the nurse take?

  1. Keep the client's communication confidential, but talk to the client daily, using therapeutic communication to convince them to admit to hiding the knife.
  2. Keep the client's communication confidential, but watch the client and their roommate closely.
  3. Tell the client that this must be reported to the health care team because it concerns the health and safety of the client and others.
  4. Report the incident to the health care team, but do not inform the client of the intention to do so.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — The information is a serious safety issue the nurse must report. Using the ethical principle of veracity (honesty), the nurse truthfully tells the client what must be done regarding the issue.

  • A. Therapeutic communication is good, but the nurse cannot wait — the safety risk requires immediate reporting.
  • B. The nurse cannot keep this confidential because of the safety threat.
  • D. Failing to inform the client violates veracity. Tell the client the information will be reported.

NCLEX® Connection: Management of Care — Ethical Practice

Exercise 2.4 — Restraint documentation (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is caring for a client who is in mechanical restraints. Which of the following statements should the nurse include in the documentation? Select all that apply.

  1. "Client ate most of their breakfast."
  2. "Client was offered 8 oz of water every hr."
  3. "Client shouted obscenities at assistive personnel."
  4. "Client received chlorpromazine 15 mg by mouth at 1000."
  5. "Client acted out after lunch."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, C, D — Document objective information.

  • A. Incorrect — "most of their breakfast" is vague; document objectively (e.g., "70% of breakfast consumed").
  • B. Correct — Amount and frequency of fluids offered is objective.
  • C. Correct — Description of verbal communication (specific behavior) is objective.
  • D. Correct — Dosage and time of medication is objective and required.
  • E. Incorrect — "Acted out" is interpretive; document the specific observed behavior instead.

NCLEX® Connection: Safety and Infection Control — Use of Restraints/Safety Devices

Exercise 2.5 — Witnessed privacy violation

A nurse hears a newly licensed nurse discussing a client's hallucinations in the hallway with another nurse. Which of the following actions should the nurse take first?

  1. Notify the nurse manager.
  2. Tell the nurse to stop discussing the behavior.
  3. Provide an in-service program about confidentiality.
  4. Complete an incident report.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — The greatest immediate risk is the ongoing invasion of privacy in a public place. The first action is to stop the violation by telling the nurse to stop discussing the client's hallucinations in a public location.

  • A. Notify the manager — appropriate, but only after stopping the violation.
  • C. In-service training — appropriate longer-term action.
  • D. Incident report — required documentation but not first.

NCLEX® Connection: Management of Care — Confidentiality/Information Security

Unit 1 · Foundations for Mental Health Nursing · Chapter 3

Effective Communication

Communication is the dynamic, ongoing process of sending, receiving, and comprehending messages between two or more people. Foundational to the nurse-client relationship is therapeutic communication — the purposeful use of communication to build helping relationships with clients, families, and significant others. It is essential when caring for clients with mental health disorders because of the emotional as well as physical effects of the disorder. Therapeutic communication is client-centered, purposeful, planned, and goal-directed — not social or reciprocal. Effective use of the nursing process depends on therapeutic communication between the nurse, client, family, and interprofessional team.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Communication occurs at four levels: intrapersonal (self-talk), interpersonal (one-on-one), small group, and public. Messages travel through verbal (vocabulary, intonation, clarity, pacing, timing, denotative/connotative meaning) and nonverbal (appearance, posture, gait, facial expression, eye contact, gestures, sounds, territoriality, personal space, silence) channels. Nonverbal can have more impact than verbal. Therapeutic communication is client-centered and goal-directed. Master the key techniques: silence, active listening, open-ended questions, restating, reflecting, paraphrasing, clarifying, focusing, summarizing. Avoid the major barriers: giving advice, false reassurance, "why" questions, minimizing feelings, changing topic, value judgments.

Four levels of communication

  • Intrapersonal — self-talk; nurse's pre-care self-assessment of values
  • Interpersonal — one-on-one with client
  • Small group — team meetings, group therapy
  • Public — community education, classes

Top barriers — avoid these

  • Asking irrelevant personal questions
  • Offering personal opinions
  • Giving advice
  • Giving false reassurance
  • Minimizing feelings
  • Changing the topic
  • Asking "why" questions
  • Offering value judgments
  • Excessive questioning
  • Giving approval or disapproval

Therapeutic vs nontherapeutic — quick contrast

  • "I understand you're concerned. Let's discuss what concerns you specifically." → ✓ Therapeutic (reflects + accepts feelings)
  • "I'm sure everything will be okay. It just takes time to heal." → ✗ False reassurance
  • "Have you asked the doctor about your concerns?" → ✗ Avoidance / disinterest
  • "I think your child is getting better." → ✗ Nurse's opinion

Adapting communication by age

  • Children — simple straightforward language; eye-level; incorporate play; aware of own nonverbal
  • Adolescents — assess perception of diagnosis; risk of treatment refusal to be "normal"; impact on peer relationships
  • Older adults — amplification may be needed; minimize distractions; face the client; allow time; ask family/caregivers for input on deficits
Memory hook: If the response starts with "I think...", "I'm sure...", "Why...", or "You should..." — it's almost certainly nontherapeutic. Therapeutic responses keep the focus on the client and their feelings.

Basic Communication

Communication occurs at four levels — each with specific clinical applications in nursing.

Intrapersonal communication

Self-talk

Communication that occurs within an individual. The internal discussion that takes place when one is thinking thoughts and not outwardly verbalizing them.

Nursing application

Intrapersonal communication allows nurses to perform a self-assessment of their values or beliefs prior to caring for a client whose diagnosis can trigger an emotional response (e.g., before caring for someone with a substance use disorder when the nurse has personal family experience with addiction).

Interpersonal communication

One-on-one

Communication that occurs one-on-one with another individual.

Nursing application

Used when obtaining a psychosocial history from a client or listening to a client discuss their feelings.

Small-group communication

2+ in small group

Communication between two or more people in a small group.

Nursing application

Discussing a change in the client's behavior with the health care team, or discussing concerns with clients during a group therapy session.

Public communication

Large groups

Communication within large groups of people.

Nursing application

Educational endeavors where the nurse teaches a large group — for example, teaching about suicide prevention with high school students at a school assembly.

Verbal Communication

Verbal communication consists of the words and how they are delivered. Six components shape its effectiveness.

Six components of verbal communication

Vocabulary

  • The words used to communicate either a written or spoken message
  • Limited vocabulary or speaking a language other than English can make communication difficult
  • Use of medical jargon can decrease client understanding

Denotative/connotative meaning

  • Participants must share meanings
  • Words with multiple meanings can cause miscommunication if interpreted differently
  • Denotative = dictionary meaning; connotative = associated/emotional meaning

Clarity / brevity

  • The shortest, simplest communication is usually most effective
  • Clients can have difficulty understanding long, complex communication
  • Especially important with mental health clients (depression, psychosis can impair processing)

Timing / relevance

  • Knowing when to communicate allows the receiver to be more attentive
  • Communicating with a client in pain or distracted makes it difficult to convey the message

Pacing

  • The rate of speech communicates meaning to the receiver
  • Speaking rapidly can imply the nurse is in a rush and does not have time for the client

Intonation

  • The tone of voice communicates a variety of feelings
  • Through tone, the nurse can communicate acceptance, judgment, dislike
  • Intonation is a verbal component (not nonverbal) — common NCLEX distinction

Nonverbal Communication

Nurses must be aware of how they communicate nonverbally and assess clients' nonverbal communication for meaning. Culture impacts interpretation, and nonverbal can have more impact on the message than the verbal words.

Ten nonverbal channels to observe and use

Watch + match to verbal

Pay attention to these behaviors and compare them to the verbal message being conveyed. Incongruence between verbal and nonverbal is clinically significant.

Body presentation

  • Appearance — grooming, hygiene, dress
  • Posture — open, closed, slumped, rigid
  • Gait — pace, steadiness, purpose

Face & eyes

  • Facial expressions — congruent with content?
  • Eye contact — varies by situation and culture; conveys interest and respect
  • Gestures — emphatic, restless, absent

Sound & space

  • Sounds — non-word vocalizations
  • Territoriality — claim over physical space
  • Personal space — distance preference (cultural)
  • Silence — used purposefully; can communicate volumes

Therapeutic Communication — Overview

Therapeutic communication is the purposeful use of communication to build and maintain helping relationships with clients, families, and significant others. It is essential when caring for clients with mental health disorders because of the emotional as well as physical effects.

What therapeutic communication does

The nurse uses interactive, purposeful communication skills to:

  • Elicit and attend to the client's thoughts, feelings, concerns, and needs
  • Express empathy and genuine concern for the client's and family's issues
  • Obtain information and give feedback about the client's condition
  • Intervene to promote functional behavior and effective interpersonal relationships
  • Evaluate the client's progress toward goals and outcomes

Characteristics

  • Client centered — not social or reciprocal
  • Purposeful, planned, and goal-directed

This means: the nurse does not share personal stories ("That happened to me too…"), does not engage in reciprocal disclosure, and always serves a clinical purpose with the interaction.

Nursing Process for Communication

Effective therapeutic communication follows the nursing process. Each phase has specific tasks.

Assessment phase

General assessment

  • Assess verbal and nonverbal communication needs
  • Identify cultural considerations that can impact communication (eye contact, personal space, touch)
  • Assess for congruency between verbal and nonverbal message
  • Consider the client's developmental level and adapt accordingly

Children

  • Use simple, straightforward language
  • Be aware of own nonverbal messages — children are sensitive to them
  • Be at the child's eye level
  • Incorporate play in interactions
  • Adapt to child's level of development

Adolescents

  • Determine how the adolescent perceives the diagnosis
  • Is the client at risk for refusal of treatment due to a desire to be "normal"?
  • Identify if the diagnosis affects the client's relationship with peers

Older adult clients

  • Recognize that the client might require amplification
  • Minimize distractions and face the client when speaking
  • Allow plenty of time to respond
  • When impaired communication is assessed, ask for input from caregivers or family to determine the extent of deficits and how best to communicate

Planning phase

  • Minimize distractions
  • Provide for privacy
  • Identify mutually agreed-upon client outcomes
  • Set priorities according to the client's needs
  • Plan for adequate time for interventions

Implementation phase

  • Establish a trusting nurse-client relationship — the client feels more at ease once a helping relationship is established
  • Provide empathetic responses and explanations to the client by using observations and providing hope, humor, and information
  • Manipulate the environment to decrease distractions

Essential Components of Therapeutic Communication

Specific elements that make communication therapeutic. Each is a foundation; missing any one degrades the interaction.

Time

  • Plan for and allow adequate time to communicate
  • Clients with certain mental health disorders (major depressive disorder, schizophrenia) can require a longer period of time to respond to questions

Attending behaviors (active listening)

Nonverbal means of conveying interest in another.

  • Eye contact — typically conveys interest and respect; varies by situation and culture
  • Body language and posture — demonstrates level of comfort and ease
  • Vocal quality — enhances rapport and emphasizes particular topics or issues
  • Verbal tracking — provides feedback by restating or summarizing a client's statements

Personal qualities the nurse brings

Caring attitude

Show concern and facilitate an emotional connection with the client and the client's family.

Honesty

Be open, direct, truthful, and sincere.

Trust

Demonstrate reliability without doubt or question.

Empathy

Convey an objective awareness and understanding of the feelings, emotions, and behaviors of others — including trying to envision what it must be like to be in the position of the client and the client's family.

Nonjudgmental attitude

A display of acceptance that will encourage open, honest communication.

Effective Communication Skills and Techniques

The clinical "toolkit" of therapeutic techniques. The NCLEX frequently tests recognition of techniques — knowing the names and what each accomplishes is essential.

Silence and active listening

Silence

Silence allows time for meaningful reflection. Resist the urge to fill every pause.

Active listening

The nurse is able to hear, observe, and understand what the client communicates and to provide feedback.

Questions — four types

Questions allow the nurse to obtain specific or additional information from the client.

Open-ended questions

Facilitate spontaneous responses and interactive discussion.

Example: "How are you feeling today?"

Closed-ended questions

Helpful if used sparingly during the initial interaction to obtain specific data. Avoid repeated closed-ended questions which can block further communication.

Example: "Did you take your medication this morning?"

Projective questions

Uses "what if" or similar questions to assist clients in exploring feelings and to gain greater understanding of problems and possible solutions.

Example: "What if you decided to attend the support group?"

Presupposition questions

Explores the client's life goals or motivations by presenting a hypothetical situation in which the client no longer has the mental health disorder.

Example: "If you woke up tomorrow without depression, what would you do first?"

Clarifying techniques

Confirm understanding

Used to determine if the message received was accurate.

Restating

Uses the client's exact words.

Client: "I can't sleep. I stay up all night."
Nurse: "You are having difficulty sleeping?"

Reflecting

Directs the focus back to the client in order for the client to examine their feelings.

Client: "Should I tell my husband?"
Nurse: "What do you think you should do?"

Paraphrasing

Restates the client's feelings and thoughts for the client to confirm what has been communicated. (Nurse's own words.)

Exploring

Allows the nurse to gather more information regarding important topics mentioned by the client.

Example: "Tell me more about what happened at the party."

Other key techniques

Offering general leads / broad opening statements

Encourages the client to determine where the communication can start and to continue talking.

Example: "Tell me what brings you in today."

Showing acceptance and recognition

Acknowledges the nurse's interest and nonjudgmental attitude.

Example: "I notice you've been wearing your hair differently this week."

Focusing

Helps the client concentrate on what is important. Concentrates attention on one single point.

Example: "You mentioned feeling more anxious lately. Can we talk more about that?"

Giving information

Provides details the client might need for decision making.

Example: "Your medication can cause drowsiness for the first week."

Presenting reality

Used to help the client focus on what is actually happening and to dispel delusions, hallucinations, or faulty beliefs.

Example: "I don't see anyone in the hallway. I do see that you are very frightened."

Summarizing

Emphasizes important points and reviews what has been discussed.

Example: "So today we talked about your sleep, your medications, and your plan to call the hotline if you have intrusive thoughts again."

Offering self

Demonstrates a willingness to spend time with the client. Indicates the nurse has genuine concern.

Example: "I'll sit with you for a while."

Touch

If appropriate, therapeutic touch communicates caring and can provide comfort. Always assess client preference first — some clients (history of trauma, cultural practice, mental health symptoms) may find touch threatening.

Barriers to Effective Communication

Recognize these patterns — they consistently appear on the NCLEX as wrong-answer options. If a response demonstrates any of these, it's nontherapeutic.

Ten barriers to therapeutic communication

Personal injection

  • Asking irrelevant personal questions — diverts from client's concerns
  • Offering personal opinions — interjects nurse's views; can cause client to withhold
  • Giving advice — interferes with the client's ability to make personal decisions and choices
  • Offering value judgments — places nurse's standards over client's
  • Giving approval or disapproval — both can shut down honest expression

Dismissive responses

  • Giving false reassurance — "Everything will be okay" — denies the client's real concerns
  • Minimizing feelings — "It's not so bad" — invalidates emotion
  • Changing the topic — avoids engagement with what the client raised

Coercive question patterns

  • Asking "why" questions — feels accusatory; clients rarely know why and may become defensive
  • Excessive questioning — turns dialogue into interrogation; client withdraws

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Grief and loss class — older adult clients

Basic Concept · Adapted communication

A nurse in a mental health facility is preparing to conduct a class with older adult clients on grief and loss. Use the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept to complete this item.

Nursing interventions — at least four verbal/nonverbal interventions for older adults

  1. Recognize that the client might require amplification. Use a slower pace, lower pitch, and consider assistive listening devices.
  2. Minimize distractions and face the client when speaking. Reduce environmental noise; allow lip reading and observation of facial expression.
  3. Allow plenty of time for the client to respond. Older adults may take longer to formulate responses; resist the urge to fill silence.
  4. Ask for input from caregivers or family when impaired communication is assessed, to determine the extent of the deficits and how best to communicate.
  5. Provide for privacy. Confidential discussion of grief and loss requires a quiet, private setting.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Grief and Loss

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 3.1 — Verbal vs nonverbal classification

A charge nurse is conducting a class on therapeutic communication with a group of newly licensed nurses. Which of the following aspects of communication should the nurse identify as a component of verbal communication?

  1. Personal space
  2. Posture
  3. Eye contact
  4. Intonation
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D — Intonation is the tone of one's voice and can communicate a variety of feelings; it is a verbal component.

  • A. Personal space is a component of nonverbal communication.
  • B. Posture is a component of nonverbal communication.
  • C. Eye contact is a component of nonverbal communication.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Exercise 3.2 — Identify the technique

A nurse in an acute mental health facility is communicating with a client. The client states, "I can't sleep. I stay up all night." The nurse responds, "You are having difficulty sleeping?" Which of the following therapeutic communication techniques is the nurse demonstrating?

  1. Offering general leads
  2. Summarizing
  3. Focusing
  4. Restating
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D — Restating uses the client's exact words to repeat the main idea expressed. The nurse used the same words ("difficulty sleeping" = client's "can't sleep") to verify understanding.

  • A. Offering general leads allows the nurse to invite the direction of discussion (e.g., "Tell me more").
  • B. Summarizing brings together important points of discussion to enhance understanding.
  • C. Focusing concentrates the attention on one single point.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Exercise 3.3 — Identify the barrier

A nurse is communicating with a client who was admitted for treatment of a substance use disorder. Which of the following communication techniques should the nurse identify as a barrier to therapeutic communication?

  1. Offering advice
  2. Reflecting
  3. Listening attentively
  4. Giving information
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A — Offering advice is a barrier to therapeutic communication. Advice tends to interfere with the client's ability to make personal decisions and choices — particularly important in substance use recovery, where autonomy and self-direction are critical.

  • B. Reflection directs the focus back to the client to examine feelings — therapeutic.
  • C. Active listening is essential for understanding what the client is conveying.
  • D. Giving information provides details to assist treatment planning.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Exercise 3.4 — Level of communication

A nurse is caring for a client who has anorexia nervosa. Which of the following examples demonstrates the nurse's use of interpersonal communication?

  1. The nurse discusses the client's weight loss during a health care team meeting.
  2. The nurse examines their own personal feelings about clients who have anorexia nervosa.
  3. The nurse asks the client about personal body image perception.
  4. The nurse presents an educational session about anorexia nervosa to a large group of adolescents.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — One-on-one communication with the client is interpersonal communication.

  • A. Discussion with the team = small-group communication.
  • B. Self-assessment of feelings = intrapersonal communication.
  • D. Large-group educational presentation = public communication.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Exercise 3.5 — Best therapeutic response

A nurse is talking with the caregiver of a child who has demonstrated recent changes in behavior and mood. When the caregiver of the child asks the nurse for reassurance about their child's condition, which of the following responses should the nurse make?

  1. "I think your child is getting better. What have you noticed?"
  2. "I'm sure everything will be okay. It just takes time to heal."
  3. "I'm not sure what's wrong. Have you asked the doctor about your concerns?"
  4. "I understand you're concerned. Let's discuss what concerns you specifically."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D — This therapeutic response reflects upon and accepts the caregiver's feelings, and it allows them to clarify what they are feeling.

  • A. Interjects the nurse's opinion ("I think your child is getting better"); can cause the caregiver to withhold thoughts.
  • B. False reassurance — denies the caregiver's real concerns.
  • C. Avoids addressing the concerns directly and indicates disinterest in discussing with the parents.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Unit 1 · Foundations for Mental Health Nursing · Chapter 4

Stress and Defense Mechanisms

Stress results from a change in one's environment that is threatening, causes challenges, or is perceived as damaging to that person's well-being. Stress causes anxiety, and individuals use defense mechanisms to manage conflict in response to anxiety. Defense mechanisms are reversible and can be adaptive (achieve goals acceptably, reduce anxiety) or maladaptive (interfere with functioning, relationships, and reality testing when used in excess). The key clinical distinction is frequency, intensity, and duration of use — and using a variety of mechanisms rather than the same reaction to every stressful situation. Only altruism and sublimation are always healthy; all others can become maladaptive if overused or used inappropriately.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Anxiety lives on a continuum: mild → moderate → severe → panic. As anxiety rises, perception, learning, and problem-solving fall. Mild anxiety enhances perception; moderate hampers it but learning still possible; severe stops learning entirely; panic loses touch with reality. Nursing care is matched to level: mild/moderate = active listening, open-ended questions, explore coping; severe/panic = remain with client, low-stimulation environment, firm short statements, gross motor activity, meds/restraints only after less restrictive measures fail. Defense mechanisms manage anxiety — they are reversible and can be adaptive or maladaptive. Altruism and sublimation are the only two always-healthy mechanisms.

Four levels of anxiety — perception

  • Mild — ↑ perception, identifiable cause; fidgeting, restlessness
  • Moderate — slightly ↓ perception; selective inattention; learning still possible; benefits from direction of others
  • Severe — perceptual field greatly reduced; no learning; automatic behaviors; cannot take direction
  • Panic — markedly disturbed; loses touch with reality; hallucinations, delusions possible

Always-healthy mechanisms

  • Altruism — anxiety → reaching out to others
  • Sublimation — anxiety → substituting socially acceptable expression
  • All other defense mechanisms can become maladaptive

Three states of anxiety

  • Normal — healthy life force, motivates action (nurse defusing a violent unit situation)
  • Acute / immediate — imminent loss or change threatens security (sudden death of loved one)
  • Chronic / sustained trait — develops over time, often starting in childhood; physical manifestations (fatigue, frequent headaches)

Quick triage — anxiety level → intervention

  • Mild–moderate → open-ended questions, broad openings, exploring, clarifying; calm presence; review prior coping; explore alternatives; encourage exercise
  • Severe–panic → SAFE environment, stay with client, minimal stimulation, firm/short/simple statements (repeat as needed), low slow voice, gross motor activity (walking), present-moment focus, meds/restraint ONLY after less restrictive interventions fail
Memory hook: As anxiety climbs, the perceptual field shrinks. Mild = wide-angle lens; moderate = zoom in (selective inattention); severe = tunnel vision (no learning); panic = blackout (reality slips). Always ask: "Can this client still take direction?" If no → severe or panic.

Defense Mechanisms

Individuals use defense mechanisms (consciously and unconsciously) to manage conflict in response to anxiety. Defense mechanisms are reversible. Altruism and sublimation are always healthy. Other defense mechanisms can be used in a healthy manner but can become maladaptive if used inappropriately or repetitively. Consider frequency, intensity, and duration of use to determine adaptive vs maladaptive.

Altruism

Always healthy

Dealing with anxiety by reaching out to others.

Adaptive use

A nurse who lost a family member in a fire is a volunteer firefighter.

Maladaptive use

None — altruism is always healthy.

Sublimation

Always healthy

Dealing with unacceptable feelings or impulses by unconsciously substituting acceptable forms of expression.

Adaptive use

A person who has feelings of anger and hostility toward their work supervisor sublimates those feelings by working out vigorously at the gym during their lunch period.

Maladaptive use

None — sublimation is always healthy.

Suppression

Voluntary / conscious

Voluntarily denying unpleasant thoughts and feelings.

Adaptive use

A student puts off thinking about a fight they had with a friend so they can focus on a test.

Maladaptive use

A person who has lost their job states they will worry about paying bills next week.

Repression

Unconscious — vs suppression

Unconsciously putting unacceptable ideas, thoughts, and emotions out of awareness. Key contrast: suppression = voluntary; repression = involuntary/unconscious.

Adaptive use

A person preparing to give a speech unconsciously forgets about the time when they were young and kids laughed at them while on stage.

Maladaptive use

A person who has a fear of the dentist continually forgets to go to their dental appointments.

Regression

Childlike behaviors

Sudden use of childlike or primitive behaviors that do not correlate with the person's current developmental level.

Adaptive use

A young child temporarily wets the bed when they learn that their pet died.

Maladaptive use

A person who has a disagreement with a co-worker begins throwing things at their office.

Displacement

Shifting feelings

Shifting feelings related to an object, person, or situation to another less threatening object, person, or situation.

Adaptive use

An adolescent angrily punches a punching bag after losing a game.

Maladaptive use

A person who is angry about losing their job destroys their child's favorite toy.

Reaction Formation

Demonstrating the opposite

Unacceptable feelings or behaviors are controlled or kept out of awareness by overcompensating or demonstrating the opposite behavior of what is felt.

Adaptive use

A person who is trying to quit smoking repeatedly talks to adolescents about the dangers of nicotine.

Maladaptive use

A person who resents having to care for an aging parent becomes overprotective and restricts their freedoms.

Undoing

Making up for prior behavior

Performing an act to make up for prior behavior (most commonly seen in children).

Adaptive use

An adolescent completes their chores without being prompted after having an argument with their parent.

Maladaptive use

An individual buys their significant other flowers and gifts after an incident of partner abuse.

Rationalization

Reasonable-sounding excuse

Creating reasonable and acceptable explanations for unacceptable behavior.

Adaptive use
