An adolescent says, "They must already have a boyfriend" when rejected by another adolescent.

Maladaptive use

A young adult explains they had to drive home from a party after drinking alcohol because they had to feed the dog.

Identification

Assuming others' characteristics

Conscious or unconscious assumption of the characteristics of another individual or group.

Adaptive use

A child who has a chronic illness pretends to be a nurse for their dolls.

Maladaptive use

A child who observes their parent be abusive toward the other parent becomes a bully at school.

Intellectualization

Logic over emotion

Separation of emotions and logical facts when analyzing or coping with a situation or event.

Adaptive use

A law enforcement officer blocks out the emotional aspect of a crime so they can objectively focus on the investigation.

Maladaptive use

A person who learns they have a terminal illness focuses on creating a will and financial matters rather than acknowledging their grief.

Dissociation

Compartmentalization

A disruption in consciousness, memory, identity, or perception of the environment that results in compartmentalization of uncomfortable or unpleasant aspects of oneself.

Adaptive use

A parent blocks out the distracting noise of their children in order to focus while driving in traffic.

Maladaptive use

A person forgets who they are following a sexual assault.

Conversion

Stress → physical symptoms

Responding to stress through the unconscious development of physical manifestations not caused by a physical illness.

Adaptive use

None.

Maladaptive use

A person experiences deafness after their partner tells them that they want a divorce.

Splitting

All good / all bad

Demonstrating an inability to reconcile negative and positive attributes of self or others into a cohesive image. Common in borderline personality disorder.

Adaptive use

None.

Maladaptive use

A client tells a nurse that the nurse is the only one who cares about them, yet the following day, the client refuses to talk to that nurse.

Denial

Truth isn't real

Pretending the truth is not reality to manage unpleasant, anxiety-causing thoughts or feelings.

Adaptive use

A person initially says, "No, that can't be true" when told they have cancer.

Maladaptive use

A parent who is informed that their child was killed in combat tells everyone one month later that the child is coming home for the holidays.

Compensation

Strengths cover weaknesses

Emphasizing strengths to make up for weaknesses.

Adaptive use

An adolescent who is physically unable to play contact sports excels in academic competitions.

Maladaptive use

A person who is shy learns computer skills to avoid socialization.

Projection

Attributing onto others

Attributing one's unacceptable thoughts and feelings onto another who does not have them.

Adaptive use

None.

Maladaptive use

A married client who is attracted to another person accuses their partner of having an extramarital affair.

Anxiety — Continuum, States, and Levels

Anxiety is viewed on a continuum with increasing levels leading to decreasing ability to function. Three states (normal, acute, chronic) describe its origin; four levels (mild, moderate, severe, panic) describe its intensity.

Normal anxiety

A healthy life force

A healthy life force necessary for survival. Normal anxiety motivates people to take action.

Example

A potentially violent situation occurs on the mental health unit, and the nurse moves rapidly to defuse the situation. The anxiety experienced helped them perform quickly and efficiently.

Acute anxiety

Immediate state

Precipitated by an imminent loss or change that threatens one's sense of security.

Example

The sudden death of a loved one precipitates an acute state of anxiety.

Chronic anxiety

Sustained trait

Usually develops over time, often starting in childhood. The adult who experiences chronic anxiety might display it in physical manifestations (fatigue, frequent headaches).

Four Levels of Anxiety — Assessment

Assessment of a client's level of anxiety is basic to therapeutic intervention in any setting. Each level has specific perceptual, cognitive, and physical findings.

Mild anxiety

↑ perception

Occurs in the normal experience of everyday living.

Perception & cognition

  • Increases one's ability to perceive reality
  • There is an identifiable cause of the anxiety
  • Learning and problem-solving function well

Behavior & physical

  • Vague feeling of mild discomfort
  • Restlessness, irritability, impatience, apprehension
  • Mild tension-relieving behaviors — finger-/foot-tapping, fidgeting, lip-chewing

Moderate anxiety

Selective inattention

Occurs when mild anxiety escalates.

Perception & cognition

  • Slightly reduced perception and processing of information
  • Selective inattention can occur
  • Ability to think clearly is hampered, but learning and problem-solving can still occur

Behavior & physical

  • Concentration difficulties, tiredness, pacing
  • Change in voice pitch, voice tremors, shakiness
  • Increased heart rate and respiratory rate
  • Somatic — headaches, backache, urinary urgency/frequency, insomnia

Clinical pearl

The client at this level usually benefits from the direction of others. They can still take instruction and engage with calm guidance.

Severe anxiety

No learning possible

Perception & cognition

  • Perceptual field is greatly reduced with distorted perceptions
  • Learning and problem-solving do not occur
  • Functioning is effective; behaviors are automatic

Behavior & physical

  • Confusion, feelings of impending doom
  • Hyperventilation, tachycardia
  • Withdrawal, loud and rapid speech
  • Aimless activity

Clinical pearl

The client at this level usually is not able to take direction from others. The nurse must focus on safety and reducing stimulation, not education or processing.

Panic-level anxiety

Reality slips

Characterized by markedly disturbed behavior.

Perception & cognition

  • Client is not able to process what is occurring in the environment
  • Can lose touch with reality
  • Experiences extreme fright and horror

Behavior & physical

  • Severe hyperactivity, flight, or immobility
  • Dysfunction in speech
  • Dilated pupils
  • Severe shakiness, withdrawal
  • Inability to sleep
  • Delusions and hallucinations possible

Patient-Centered Care — Nursing Interventions by Level

Interventions are matched to the level of anxiety the client is experiencing. The therapeutic approaches that work at mild and moderate levels are contraindicated at severe and panic levels.

Mild to moderate anxiety

Engage, process, build coping

Use active listening & specific techniques

Open-ended questions, broad openings, exploring, seeking clarification.

Therapeutic intent: Encourage the client to express feelings, develop trust, and identify the source of the anxiety.

Provide a calm presence

Recognize the client's distress.

Therapeutic intent: Assists the client to focus and to begin to problem solve.

Evaluate past coping mechanisms

Therapeutic intent: Assists the client to identify adaptive and maladaptive coping mechanisms.

Explore alternatives to problem situations

Therapeutic intent: Offers options for problem-solving.

Encourage exercise/activity

Therapeutic intent: Provides an outlet for pent-up tension, promotes endorphin release, and improves mental well-being.

Severe to panic-level anxiety

Safety, simplicity, stay

The client cannot take direction or learn. The focus shifts to safety and reducing arousal.

Provide a safe environment; stay with the client

Provide an environment that meets the physical and safety needs of the client. Remain with the client and remain calm.

Therapeutic intent: Minimizes risk; the client might be unaware of the need for basic things (fluids, food, sleep).

Quiet environment, minimal stimulation

Therapeutic intent: Helps prevent intensification of the current level of anxiety.

Medications and restraint — LAST resort

Use medications and restraint, but only after less restrictive interventions have failed to decrease anxiety to safer levels.

Therapeutic intent: May be necessary to prevent harm to the client, other clients, and providers.

Gross motor activities

Encourage gross motor activities — walking and other forms of exercise.

Therapeutic intent: Provides an outlet for pent-up tension, promotes endorphin release, improves mental well-being.

Set limits with simple statements

Set limits by using firm, short, and simple statements. Repetition can be necessary. Speak slowly and in a low-pitched voice.

Therapeutic intent: Can minimize risk; clear, simple communication facilitates understanding.

Reality orientation

Direct the client to acknowledge reality and focus on what is present in the environment.

Therapeutic intent: Focusing on reality assists with reducing anxiety level.

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Client experiencing severe anxiety

Basic Concept · Severe anxiety interventions

A nurse is caring for a client who has severe anxiety. Use the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept to complete this item.

Nursing interventions — at least four for severe anxiety

  1. Provide an environment that meets the physical and safety needs of the client. Remain with the client.
  2. Provide a quiet environment with minimal stimulation.
  3. Use medications and restraint, but only after less restrictive interventions have failed to decrease anxiety to safer levels.
  4. Encourage gross motor activities, such as walking and other forms of exercise.
  5. Set limits by using firm, short, and simple statements. Repetition may be necessary.
  6. Direct the client to acknowledge reality and focus on what is present in the environment.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Behavioral Interventions

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 4.1 — Defense mechanism identification

A nurse is caring for a client who smokes and has lung cancer. The client reports, "I'm coughing because I have that cold that everyone has been getting." The nurse should identify that the client is using which of the following defense mechanisms?

  1. Reaction formation
  2. Denial
  3. Displacement
  4. Sublimation
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — Denial is pretending the truth is not reality to manage the anxiety of acknowledging what is real. The client attributes their cough to a cold rather than to lung cancer.

  • A. Reaction formation is overcompensating or demonstrating the opposite behavior of what is felt.
  • C. Displacement is shifting feelings to a less threatening object, person, or situation.
  • D. Sublimation is dealing with unacceptable feelings or impulses by unconsciously substituting acceptable forms of expression.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 4.2 — Level of anxiety

A nurse is providing preoperative teaching for a client who was informed of the need for emergency surgery. The client has a respiratory rate 30/min, and says, "This is difficult to comprehend. I feel shaky and nervous." The nurse should identify that the client is experiencing which of the following levels of anxiety?

  1. Mild
  2. Moderate
  3. Severe
  4. Panic
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — Moderate anxiety decreases problem-solving and may hamper the client's ability to understand information ("difficult to comprehend"). Vital signs may increase somewhat (RR 30/min), and the client is visibly anxious (shaky, nervous) but still able to communicate clearly about the experience.

  • A. In mild anxiety, the client's ability to understand information may actually increase.
  • C. Severe anxiety causes restlessness, greatly decreased perception, and an inability to take direction.
  • D. During a panic attack, the person is completely distracted, unable to function, and may lose touch with reality.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Behavioral Interventions

Exercise 4.3 — Moderate anxiety interventions (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is caring for a client who is experiencing moderate anxiety. Which of the following actions should the nurse take when trying to give necessary information to the client? Select all that apply.

  1. Reassure the client that everything will be okay.
  2. Discuss prior use of coping mechanisms with the client.
  3. Ignore the client's anxiety so that she will not be embarrassed.
  4. Demonstrate a calm manner while using simple and clear directions.
  5. Gather information from the client using closed-ended questions.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, D

  • A. Incorrect — False reassurance is nontherapeutic communication.
  • B. Correct — Discussing prior coping mechanisms assists the client in identifying ways of effectively coping with the current stressor.
  • C. Incorrect — Recognizing (not ignoring) the client's current level of anxiety assists the client to begin problem solving.
  • D. Correct — Providing a calm presence assists the client in feeling secure and promotes relaxation. Clients with moderate anxiety benefit from the direction of others.
  • E. Incorrect — Open-ended questions (not closed-ended) encourage the client to express feelings and identify the source of anxiety.

Exercise 4.4 — Defense mechanism contrast

A nurse is observing two clients in a mental health unit. Client A volunteers to lead an evening peer-support group after losing a sibling to suicide. Client B repeatedly explains they had to use cocaine because "everyone in the family does it." Which defense mechanisms are these clients demonstrating, respectively?

  1. Altruism and rationalization
  2. Sublimation and denial
  3. Identification and displacement
  4. Reaction formation and projection
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A — Client A reaches out to others (a peer-support group) following a personal loss → altruism (always healthy). Client B creates reasonable-sounding explanations for unacceptable substance use → rationalization.

  • B. Sublimation substitutes acceptable expression for unacceptable impulses; denial is pretending the truth isn't real.
  • C. Identification assumes others' characteristics; displacement shifts feelings to a less threatening target.
  • D. Reaction formation demonstrates the opposite of what is felt; projection attributes one's feelings to another.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 4.5 — Severe anxiety priority

A nurse is caring for a client experiencing severe anxiety on a mental health unit. The client is pacing rapidly, breathing 32/min, and repeating "I can't do this, I can't do this." Which of the following actions should the nurse take first?

  1. Teach the client deep-breathing exercises.
  2. Provide an environment with minimal stimulation and remain with the client.
  3. Document the client's behavior and vital signs.
  4. Encourage the client to verbalize feelings about the source of anxiety.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — At the severe level, perceptual field is greatly reduced and learning does not occur. The priority is safety and reduced stimulation: minimize stimulation and stay with the client.

  • A. Teaching deep-breathing requires learning, which does not occur at the severe level.
  • C. Documentation is needed but not the first action.
  • D. Verbalization techniques work at mild/moderate levels; at severe level the client cannot engage in this type of processing.

Unit 1 · Foundations for Mental Health Nursing · Chapter 5

Creating and Maintaining a Therapeutic and Safe Environment

Therapeutic encounters can occur in any nursing setting if a nurse is sensitive to a client's needs and uses effective communication skills. The therapeutic nurse-client relationship is foundational to mental health nursing care and differs fundamentally from social or intimate relationships. It is purposeful and goal-directed, well-defined with clear boundaries, structured to meet the client's needs, and characterized by an interpersonal process that is safe, confidential, reliable, and consistent. The relationship unfolds in three phases — orientation, working, and termination — each with specific tasks for both nurse and client. Milieu therapy creates and maintains the therapeutic environment that supports this work.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

The therapeutic nurse-client relationship is goal-directed, time-limited, and client-focused — not social or reciprocal. It has three phases: orientation (set contract, build trust, establish goals), working (problem-solve, build coping, address resistance), and termination (review progress, plan future, accept ending). Milieu therapy manages the total environment to minimize stress and maximize therapeutic benefit. Watch boundaries: transference = client views nurse as significant past figure; countertransference = nurse projects past feelings onto client. Empathy is therapeutic; sympathy is not. Physical safety requires no sharps access, monitored visitors, no alcohol/substances, no client sexual activity, prevent elopement, planned de-escalation.

Three phases of the relationship

  • Orientation — introduce, set contract (time/place/frequency/duration/termination date), discuss confidentiality, establish expectations and boundaries, set goals, enforce limits
  • Working — ongoing assessment, problem-solve, foster behavioral change, address resistance, recognize transference/countertransference, support new coping
  • Termination — discuss feelings about ending, review goals/achievements, plan for future, accept termination as final

Therapeutic vs social — key contrasts

  • Therapeutic — client-focused; goal-directed; emotional commitment absent; termination date set
  • Social — mutual needs; friendship; emotional commitment present; no termination
  • Empathy ≠ sympathy. Empathy = objectively understand client's feelings; sympathy = nurse feels what client feels (nontherapeutic)

Transference vs countertransference — quick distinction

  • Transference — client projects feelings/qualities of a significant past person onto the nurse. More likely with authority figures. Example: client accuses nurse of being controlling "just like my ex-partner."
  • Countertransference — nurse projects feelings from their past onto the client. Watch signs: over-identifying with client, competing with client, arguing with client. Example: nurse feels defensive with a client who reminds them of a friend who elicited those feelings.

Activities within the milieu

  • Individual therapy — scheduled with a provider for specific concerns (depression)
  • Group therapy — scheduled for common issues (substance use disorder)
  • Psychoeducational groups — by level of functioning and personal needs (medication side effects)
  • Recreational — games, community outings
  • Unstructured time — observe spontaneous interactions
  • Community meetings — clients and staff discuss common unit issues (NOT individual treatment plans)
Memory hook: If a question shows the nurse saying "I feel sorry for…" or "I know how difficult…" — that's sympathy (about the nurse). The therapeutic response keeps focus on the client: "That must be very upsetting for you." For phase identification: orientation = setting up, working = doing the work, termination = wrapping up.

The Therapeutic Nurse-Client Relationship

The therapeutic relationship is the foundation of mental health nursing care. It differs from social and intimate relationships in purpose, boundaries, and structure.

Defining characteristics

A therapeutic nurse-client relationship is:

  • Purposeful and goal-directed
  • Well-defined with clear boundaries
  • Structured to meet the client's needs (not the nurse's)
  • Characterized by an interpersonal process that is safe, confidential, reliable, and consistent
  • Has an established termination date

Roles of the nurse

  • Consistently focus on the client's ideas, experiences, and feelings
  • Identify and explore the client's needs and problems
  • Discuss problem-solving alternatives with the client
  • Help develop the client's strengths and new coping skills
  • Encourage positive behavior change in the client
  • Assist the client to develop a sense of autonomy and self-reliance
  • Portray genuineness, empathy, and a positive regard toward the client

Empathy vs sympathy

Critical distinction

The nurse practices empathy by remaining nonjudgmental and attempting to understand the client's actions and feelings.

Empathy (therapeutic)

Objective awareness and understanding of the feelings of others. The nurse understands what the client feels without taking it on.

Example: "Losing someone close to you must be very upsetting."

Sympathy (nontherapeutic)

The nurse allows themselves to feel the way the client does. This blurs the boundary and disables effective help.

Examples: "I feel very sorry for the loneliness you must be experiencing." / "I know how difficult it is to lose a loved one." (Both center the nurse's experience.)

Milieu Therapy

Milieu therapy creates an environment that is supportive, therapeutic, and safe. Also called therapeutic community or therapeutic environment. Management of the milieu refers to managing the total environment of the mental health unit to provide the least amount of stress while promoting the greatest benefit for all clients.

Goals and structure

  • The goal: while in this environment, the client will learn the tools necessary to cope adaptively, interact more effectively and appropriately, and strengthen relationship skills
  • The hope: the client will use these tools in all other aspects of their life
  • The nurse, as manager of care, is responsible for structuring and/or implementing aspects of the therapeutic milieu
  • One key structure: regular community meetings that include both clients and nursing staff

Characteristics of the therapeutic milieu

Physical setting

  • Unit should be clean and orderly
  • Comfortable furniture placed to promote interaction
  • Solitary spaces for reading and thinking alone
  • Comfortable places conducive to meals
  • Quiet areas for sleeping
  • Color scheme and overall design appropriate for the client's age
  • Materials (floors) attractive, easy to clean, and safe
  • Traffic flow conducive to client and staff movement

Health care team responsibilities

  • Promote independence for self-care and individual growth
  • Treat clients as individuals
  • Allow choices within daily routine and treatment plans
  • Apply rules of fair treatment for all clients
  • Model good social behavior — respect for others' rights
  • Work cooperatively as a team
  • Maintain boundaries with clients
  • Maintain professional appearance and demeanor
  • Promote safe, satisfying peer interactions
  • Practice open communication with team members and clients
  • Promote feelings of self-worth and hope for the future

Emotional climate

  • Clients should feel safe from harm (self-harm and harm from disruptive behaviors of other clients)
  • Clients should feel cared for and accepted by staff and others

Factors that Promote Therapeutic Relationship Development

Both nurse and client contribute to whether the relationship achieves its goals. Therapeutic relationships take time to establish, but even time-limited encounters can have positive outcomes. Supervision by peers or the clinical team enhances the nurse's ability to examine their own thoughts and feelings, maintain boundaries, and continue to learn from nurse-client relationships.

Nurse factors

  • Consistent approach to interaction
  • Adjustment of pace to client's needs
  • Attentive listening
  • Positive initial impressions
  • Comfort level during the relationship
  • Self-awareness of own thoughts and feelings
  • Consistent availability

Client factors

  • Trusting attitude
  • Willingness to talk
  • Active participation
  • Consistent availability

Boundaries of the Therapeutic Relationship

Boundaries must be established in order to maintain a safe and professional nurse-client relationship. The nurse must work to maintain a consistent level of involvement, reflect on boundary issues frequently, and maintain awareness of how behaviors can be perceived by others (clients, family members, other team members).

Blurred vs maintained boundaries

Blurred boundaries occur if the relationship begins to meet the needs of the nurse rather than those of the client, or if the relationship becomes social rather than therapeutic.

Social relationship

Primary purpose is socialization or friendship with a focus on the mutual needs of the individuals involved.

Therapeutic relationship

Primary purpose is to identify the client's problems or needs and then focus on assisting the client in meeting or resolving those issues.

Transference

Client → Nurse

Transference occurs when the client views a member of the health care team as having characteristics of another person who has been significant to the client's personal life.

Behaviors

  • Client expects exclusive services from the nurse, such as extra session time
  • Client demonstrates jealousy of the nurse's time or attention
  • Client compares the nurse to a former authority figure

Example

A client can see a nurse as being like their parent and thus can demonstrate some of the same behaviors with the nurse that they demonstrated with their parent. (Example: client accuses the nurse of "being controlling just like my ex-partner.")

Nursing implications

A nurse should be aware that transference by a client is more likely to occur with a person in authority.

Countertransference

Nurse → Client

Countertransference occurs when a health care team member displaces characteristics of people in their past onto a client.

Behaviors

  • Nurse overly identifies with client
  • Nurse competes with client
  • Nurse argues with client

Example

A nurse can feel defensive and angry with a client for no apparent reason if the client reminds them of a friend who often elicited those feelings.

Nursing implications

A nurse should be aware that clients who induce very strong personal feelings can become objects of countertransference. Regular peer supervision helps surface these patterns.

Physical Safety

The physical setup of a mental health unit is itself a safety intervention. Visibility, access control, and environmental design prevent harm.

Unit layout

  • The nurses' station and other areas should be placed to allow for easy observation of clients by staff and easy access to staff by clients
  • Special safety features (bathroom bars and wheelchair accessibility for clients who are disabled) should be addressed

Provisions to prevent self-harm or harm by others

Core safety protocol
  • No access to sharp or otherwise harmful objects
  • Restriction of client access to restricted or locked areas
  • Monitoring of visitors
  • Restriction of alcohol and illegal substance access or use
  • Restriction of sexual activity among clients
  • Deterrence of elopement from facility
  • Rapid de-escalation of disruptive and potentially violent behaviors through planned interventions by trained staff

Seclusion, restraint, and emergency planning

Seclusion & restraint

  • Set up for safety
  • Used only after all less restrictive measures have been exhausted
  • Facility policies and procedures must be followed when used
  • (See Chapter 2 for detailed restraint rules and time limits)

Safe access

Plan for safe access to recreational areas, occupational therapy, and meeting rooms.

Fire and evacuation

  • Teach fire, evacuation, and other safety rules to all staff
  • Clear plans for keeping clients and staff safe in emergencies
  • Maintain staff skills (cardiopulmonary resuscitation)

Room assignments — 24-hr care unit

  • Personalities of each roommate
  • Nighttime disruption risk if one client has sleep difficulty
  • Mental health and medical diagnoses (e.g., how two clients with severe paranoia might interact)

Activities Within the Therapeutic Milieu

Structured activities provide opportunities for skill-building, observation, and group support. Activities are structured and include time for both individual and group experiences.

Therapy formats

Individual therapy

Scheduled sessions with a mental health provider to address specific mental health concerns (e.g., depression).

Group therapy

Scheduled sessions for a group of clients to address common mental health issues (e.g., substance use disorder).

Psychoeducational groups

Based on clients' level of functioning and personal needs (e.g., adverse effects of medication).

Recreational activities

Include games and community outings.

Unstructured, flexible time

Opportunities for the nurse and other staff to observe clients as they interact spontaneously within the milieu.

Community meetings

Cornerstone of the milieu

Community meetings on the mental health unit enhance the emotional climate by promoting:

  • Interaction and communication between staff and clients
  • Decision-making skills of clients
  • A feeling of self-worth among clients
  • Discussions of common unit objectives — encouraging clients to meet treatment goals and plan for discharge
  • Discussion of issues of concern to all members — common problems, future activities, introduction of new clients

Meetings can be structured so that they are client-led with decisions made by the group as a whole.

Important: Community meetings address common issues. Individual treatment plans and personal mental health issues are discussed during individual therapy, not community meetings.

Phases and Tasks of the Therapeutic Relationship

The therapeutic relationship unfolds in three phases. Each has specific tasks for both nurse and client. Recognizing the phase tells the nurse what tasks belong now — and what doesn't.

Orientation phase

Setting up the relationship

Nurse tasks

  • Introduce self to the client and state purpose
  • Set the contract — meeting time, place, frequency, duration, date of termination
  • Discuss confidentiality
  • Build trust by establishing expectations and boundaries
  • Set goals with the client
  • Explore the client's ideas, issues, and needs
  • Explore the meaning of testing behaviors
  • Enforce limits on testing or other inappropriate behaviors

Client tasks

  • Meet with the nurse
  • Agree to the contract
  • Understand the limits of confidentiality
  • Understand expectations and limits of the relationship
  • Participate in setting goals
  • Begin to explore own thoughts, experiences, and feelings
  • Explore the meaning of own behaviors

Working phase

Doing the work

Nurse tasks

  • Maintain relationship according to the contract
  • Perform ongoing assessment to plan and evaluate therapeutic measures
  • Facilitate the client's expression of needs and issues
  • Encourage the client to problem-solve
  • Promote the client's self-esteem
  • Foster positive behavioral change
  • Explore and deal with resistance and other defense mechanisms
  • Recognize transference and countertransference issues
  • Reassess the client's problems and goals; revise plans as necessary
  • Support adaptive alternatives and use of new coping skills
  • Remind the client about the date of termination

Client tasks

  • Explore problematic areas of life
  • Reconsider usual coping behaviors
  • Examine own world view and self-concept
  • Describe major conflicts and various defenses
  • Experience intense feelings and learn to cope with anxiety reactions
  • Test new behaviors
  • Begin to develop awareness of transference situations
  • Try alternative solutions

Termination phase

Wrapping up

Nurse tasks

  • Provide opportunity for the client to discuss thoughts and feelings about termination and loss
  • Discuss the client's previous experiences with separations and loss
  • Elicit the client's feelings about the therapeutic work
  • Summarize goals and achievements
  • Review memories of work in the sessions
  • Express own feelings about sessions to validate the experience with the client
  • Discuss ways for the client to incorporate new healthy behaviors into life
  • Maintain limits of final termination

Client tasks

  • Discuss thoughts and feelings about termination
  • Examine previous separation and loss experiences
  • Explore the meaning of the therapeutic relationship
  • Review goals and achievements
  • Discuss plans to continue new behaviors
  • Express any feelings of loss related to termination
  • Make plans for the future
  • Accept termination as final

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Therapeutic milieu — orientation of new nurse

Basic Concept · Milieu management

A charge nurse is discussing the therapeutic milieu during the orientation of a newly hired nurse. Use the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept to complete this item.

Underlying principles — at least five health care team responsibilities

  1. Promote independence for self-care and individual growth.
  2. Treat clients as individuals.
  3. Allow choices for clients within the daily routine and treatment plan.
  4. Apply rules of fair treatment for all clients.
  5. Model good social behavior.
  6. Work cooperatively as a team to provide care.
  7. Maintain boundaries with clients.
  8. Maintain a professional appearance and demeanor.
  9. Promote safe and satisfying peer interactions among clients.
  10. Practice open communication with health team members and clients.
  11. Promote feelings of self-worth and hope for the future.

Nursing interventions — at least four to prevent client self-harm or harm by others

  1. Prevent access to sharp or harmful objects.
  2. Restrict client access to restricted or locked areas.
  3. Monitor visitors.
  4. Restrict alcohol and illegal substance access and use.
  5. Restrict sexual activity among clients.
  6. Deter elopement from facility.
  7. Provide rapid de-escalation of disruptive and potentially violent behaviors.
  8. Be aware of facility policies and procedures for seclusion or restraints.
  9. Provide safe access to recreational areas, therapy, and meeting rooms.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Environment

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 5.1 — Empathy vs sympathy

A nurse is talking with a client who is at risk for suicide following their partner's death. Which of the following statements should the nurse make?

  1. "I feel very sorry for the loneliness you must be experiencing."
  2. "Suicide is not the appropriate way to cope with loss."
  3. "Losing someone close to you must be very upsetting."
  4. "I know how difficult it is to lose a loved one."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — An empathetic response that attempts to understand the client's feelings. Keeps the focus on the client.

  • A. Focuses on the nurse's feelings ("I feel very sorry") — sympathetic rather than empathetic.
  • B. Implies judgment — neither empathetic nor therapeutic; also bypasses the underlying emotional pain.
  • D. Focuses on the nurse's experiences ("I know how difficult it is") rather than the client's — not therapeutic.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Exercise 5.2 — Characteristics of the therapeutic relationship (SATA)

A charge nurse is discussing the characteristics of a nurse-client relationship with a newly licensed nurse. Which of the following characteristics should the nurse include in the discussion? Select all that apply.

  1. The needs of both participants are met.
  2. An emotional commitment exists between the participants.
  3. It is goal-directed.
  4. Behavioral change is encouraged.
  5. A termination date is established.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C, D, E

  • A. Incorrect — Therapeutic nurse-client relationships focus on the needs of the client, not both participants.
  • B. Incorrect — Emotional commitment is characteristic of an intimate or social relationship, not therapeutic.
  • C. Correct — Goal-directed is a defining feature.
  • D. Correct — Encouraging positive behavioral change is part of the working phase.
  • E. Correct — A termination date is established in the orientation phase contract.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Environment

Exercise 5.3 — Transference identification

A nurse is in the working phase of a therapeutic relationship with a client who has methamphetamine use disorder. Which of the following actions indicates transference behavior?

  1. The client asks the nurse if they will go out to dinner together.
  2. The client accuses the nurse of being controlling just like an ex-partner.
  3. The client reminds the nurse of a friend who died from substance toxicity.
  4. The client becomes angry and threatens to engage in self harm.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — When a client views the nurse as having characteristics of another person significant to their personal life (an ex-partner), this is transference.

  • A. Indicates the need to discuss boundaries but is not transference.
  • C. The client reminding the nurse of someone from the nurse's past indicates countertransference, not transference.
  • D. Indicates a need for safety intervention but is not transference.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Environment

Exercise 5.4 — Termination phase tasks

A nurse is planning care for the termination phase of a nurse-client relationship. Which of the following actions should the nurse include in the plan of care?

  1. Discussing ways to use new behaviors
  2. Practicing new problem-solving skills
  3. Developing goals
  4. Establishing boundaries
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A — Discussing ways for the client to incorporate new healthy behaviors into life is a termination phase task — preparing the client to continue progress after the relationship ends.

  • B. Practicing new problem-solving skills is a working phase task.
  • C. Developing goals is an orientation phase task.
  • D. Establishing boundaries is an orientation phase task.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Behavioral Interventions

Exercise 5.5 — Community meetings — purpose

A nurse is orienting a new client to a mental health unit. When explaining the unit's community meetings, which of the following statements should the nurse make?

  1. "You and a group of other clients will meet to discuss your treatment plans."
  2. "Community meetings have a specific agenda that is established by staff."
  3. "You and the other clients will meet with staff to discuss common problems."
  4. "Community meetings are an excellent opportunity to explore your personal mental health issues."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — Community meetings are an opportunity for clients to discuss common problems or issues affecting all members of the unit.

  • A. Individual treatment plans are discussed during individual therapy — not community meetings.
  • B. Community meetings may be structured so they are client-led, with decisions made by the group as a whole.
  • D. Personal mental health issues are discussed during individual therapy — not in the community setting.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Environment

Unit 1 · Foundations for Mental Health Nursing · Chapter 6

Diverse Practice Settings

Mental health nursing occurs in acute care, community settings, and forensic nursing settings. In all settings, nurses are advocates for clients who have mental illness — referral to organizations like the National Alliance on Mental Illness (NAMI) provides significant support for clients and families. A continuum of facilities has developed since the mid-twentieth century deinstitutionalization movement, with acute care reserved for clients in crisis or who present a danger, and community settings providing primary, secondary, and tertiary prevention for the larger population. Forensic nursing combines biophysical education and forensic science, applying nursing to public or legal proceedings.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Mental health care happens on a continuum: acute inpatient (most restrictive) → partial hospitalization → community programs → outpatient/home care (least restrictive). Acute care is reserved for clients with clear danger to self or others, inability to meet basic needs, failed community treatment, or comorbid medical need. Community settings deliver three levels of prevention: primary (prevent — community education), secondary (early detection — screenings), tertiary (rehabilitation — support groups for clients already diagnosed). Assertive Community Treatment (ACT) is for clients with severe mental illness who are nonadherent with traditional therapy. Nurses are advocates and refer to organizations like NAMI.

Three levels of prevention

  • Primary — prevents mental health problems from occurring (community education on stress reduction; teaching coping)
  • Secondary — early detection of mental illness (depression screening in older adults at community programs)
  • Tertiary — rehabilitation: prevent further problems in clients with previous diagnoses (support group for clients who completed a substance use disorder program)

Criteria for ACUTE admission

  • Clear risk of danger to self or others
  • Inability to meet own basic needs
  • Failure to meet expected outcomes of community-based treatment
  • Dangerous decline in mental health status during long-term treatment
  • Medical need in addition to mental illness

Community-based program quick guide

  • Partial hospitalization — intense short-term; client home at night with responsible support person
  • Detoxification programs — specialized partial hospitalization for substance use (medical supervision + relapse prevention)
  • Assertive Community Treatment (ACT) — nontraditional case management for severe mental illness with nonadherence to traditional treatment
  • Community mental health centers — educational groups, medication dispensing, individual and family counseling, family therapy
  • Psychosocial rehabilitation programs — residential services, day programs for older adults
  • Home care — mental health in the home for homebound clients with psych diagnosis, needing mental health nursing skills, with a provider plan
Memory hook: For prevention level: Primary = stop it before it starts (PREVENT). Secondary = catch it early (SCREEN). Tertiary = treat what's there (REHAB). For settings: if the client cannot be safe outside, acute care; if they can be safe with structure, partial; if they can be safe with intermittent support, community/home.

Settings for Mental Health Care

Mental health nursing occurs across three broad setting categories. Each has a different scope, intensity, and population.

Acute care

Locked, intensive, short-term

Provides intensive treatment and supervision in locked units for clients who have severe mental illness and who present a danger to self or others. Care helps stabilize manifestations and promotes the client's rapid return to the community.

Staff & structure

  • Interprofessional team with management provided by nurses
  • Individualized plans that are client and family centered
  • Plans for discharge begin from time of admission

Ownership & payment

  • Privately owned or general hospitals — private funds or insurance
  • State-owned — funding often for indigent clients
  • State-run facilities also often provide full-time acute care for forensic clients (those in a correctional setting) who have severe mental illness

Transitions

Case management programs assist with client transition to a community setting after discharge.

Community settings

Primary care + prevention

Primary care is provided in community-based settings. Nurses help stabilize or improve clients' mental functioning within the community. They teach, support, and make referrals to promote positive social activities.

Types of community settings

  • Clinics
  • Schools and day-care centers
  • Partial hospitalization programs
  • Substance treatment facilities
  • Forensic settings
  • Psychosocial rehabilitation programs
  • Telephone crisis counseling centers
  • Home health care

Scope

Nursing interventions provide for primary treatment as well as primary, secondary, and tertiary prevention of mental illness.

Forensic nursing

Nursing science meets legal proceedings

A combination of biophysical education and forensic science. Utilization of nursing science in public or legal proceedings.

  • Scientific investigation
  • Collection of evidence
  • Analysis
  • Prevention and treatment of trauma and/or death of perpetrators and victims of violence, abuse, and traumatic accidents

History of Mental Health Care in the United States

Understanding how today's continuum of care developed contextualizes current practice. The shift from institutional to community care has shaped every aspect of the system.

Timeline of mental health care

Pre-1950s

Most clients with severe mental illness were treated solely in acute care facilities.

1946, 1955, 1963 — Congress acts

In response to appalling conditions of facilities for the mentally ill, Congress passed a series of acts beginning the deinstitutionalization trend.

Post-deinstitutionalization

Clients who had lived in acute care facilities for many years were discharged into the community — at a time when community mental health facilities were often unprepared for the influx.

~1970 — Case management introduced

Concept introduced to meet the individual needs of clients in a mental health setting.

~1980 — Managed care

HMOs (Health Maintenance Organizations) and PPOs (Preferred Provider Organizations) began limiting hospital stays.

1999 — Right to community living

Mental illness determined to be a disability. With the Americans with Disabilities Act, those with mental illnesses had a right to live in the community.

MBHOs developed

Managed Behavioral Healthcare Organizations developed to coordinate care and limit acute stays for clients needing mental health care.

Current

Continuum of acute care and community mental health facilities providing all levels of behavioral health care needs. Complete and accurate documentation of client needs and progress by nurses and other professionals is essential to ensure quality care.

Factors shaping the future

  • An increase in the aging population
  • An increase in cultural diversity within the United States
  • The expansion of technology, which might provide new settings for client care and new ways to treat mental illness more effectively (telehealth, digital therapeutics)

Acute Mental Health Care Settings

Acute care reserves its intensive resources for clients in crisis. Specific criteria justify admission, and specific goals drive treatment.

Criteria to justify admission to an acute care facility

  • A clear risk of the client's danger to self or others
  • An inability to meet own basic needs
  • Failure to meet expected outcomes of community-based treatment
  • A dangerous decline in the mental health status of a client undergoing long-term treatment
  • A client having a medical need in addition to a mental illness

Goals of acute mental health treatment

  • Prevention of the client harming self or others
  • Stabilizing mental health crises
  • Return of clients who are severely ill to some type of community care

Interprofessional team members

Team members in acute care include:

  • Nurses
  • Mental health technicians (similar to assistive personnel in other health care settings)
  • Psychologists
  • Psychiatrists
  • Other general health care providers
  • Social workers
  • Counselors
  • Occupational and other specialty therapists
  • Pharmacists

The interprofessional team's primary responsibility is planning and monitoring individualized treatment plans or clinical pathways, depending on facility philosophy and policy. Plans for discharge begin from admission and continue with implementation of the initial treatment plan.

Nursing role in acute care

Nurses use the nursing process and a holistic approach to provide care. Nursing roles include:

  • Overall management of the unit, including client activities and therapeutic milieu
  • Ensuring safe administration and monitoring of all client medications
  • Implementation of individual treatment plans, including client teaching
  • Documentation of the nursing process for each client
  • Managing crises as they arise

Community Health Settings & Programs

Nurses play a vital role in linking acute care facilities with community care facilities. Intensive outpatient programs promote community reintegration. Community-based mental health programs are a continuum of agencies with varying treatment intensity to allow clients to remain safe in the least restrictive environment possible.

Three levels of prevention

Public health framework

Three levels of prevention are used by nurses when implementing community care interventions and teaching.

Primary prevention

Promotes health and emphasizes efforts on preventing mental health problems from occurring.

Example: A nurse teaches a community education program on stress reduction techniques.

Secondary prevention

Focuses on early detection of mental illness.

Example: A nurse screens older adults in the community for depression.

Tertiary prevention

Focuses on rehabilitation and prevention of further problems in clients who have previous diagnoses. Mental illness is present; the goal is to prevent further deterioration or complications.

Example: A nurse leads a support group for clients who have completed a substance use disorder program. Or: establishing rehabilitation programs to decrease the effects of depression.

Partial hospitalization programs

Day program, night home
  • Provide intense short-term treatment for clients who are well enough to go home every night
  • Client must have a responsible person at home to provide support and a safe environment
  • Detoxification programs are a specialized form of partial hospitalization for clients who require medical supervision, stress management, substance use disorder counseling, and relapse prevention

Best fit: a client with severe mental illness who requires daytime supervision, whose partner is home late afternoon and overnight to provide support.

Assertive Community Treatment (ACT)

For nonadherence with traditional treatment
  • Includes nontraditional case management and treatment by an interprofessional team
  • For clients who have severe mental illness and are noncompliant with traditional treatment
  • ACT helps to reduce recurrences of hospitalizations
  • Provides crisis intervention, assistance with independent living, and information about resources for support services
  • ACT teams work with clients in their homes, agencies, hospitals, and clinics

Best fit: a client who lives at home and keeps "forgetting" to come in for a scheduled monthly antipsychotic injection for schizophrenia.

Community mental health centers

These facilities provide a variety of services for a wide range of community clients:

  • Educational groups
  • Medication dispensing programs
  • Individual and family counseling programs
  • Family therapy

Note: Detoxification programs are services of partial hospitalization, not community mental health centers.

Psychosocial rehabilitation programs

Provide a structured range of programs for clients in a mental health setting, including:

  • Residential services
  • Day programs for older adults

Home care

Mental health in the home

Provides mental health assessment, interventions, and family support in the client's home. Implemented most often for children, older adults, and clients with medical conditions.

Four criteria for psychiatric home care — all must be met

  1. The client must be homebound
  2. The client must have a psychiatric diagnosis
  3. The client must need the skills of the mental health nurse
  4. A plan of care developed by the health care provider must exist

Roles of Nurses in Diverse Mental Health Practice Settings

The scope of practice differs between the registered nurse and the advanced practice nurse in mental health settings.

Registered Nurse

Educational preparation

Diploma, associate degree, or baccalaureate degree in nursing, with additional on-the-job training and continuing education in mental health care.

Scope

  • Can work in either an acute care or community-based facility
  • Functions within a facility using the nursing process to provide care and treatment (including medication)
  • Manages care for a group of clients within a unit of the facility

Advanced Practice Nurse

Educational preparation

Advanced nursing degree in behavioral health — master's degree, doctorate, nurse practitioner, or clinical nurse specialist.

Scope

  • Can work independently, often supervising individuals or groups in either acute care or community-based settings
  • Can have prescription privileges and is able to independently recommend interventions
  • Can manage and administrate the care for an entire facility

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Acute mental health treatment — education program

Basic Concept · Admission criteria + nursing roles

A nurse is conducting an education program regarding acute mental health treatment for a group of newly licensed nurses. What should the nurse include in this presentation? Use the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept to complete this item.

Related content — at least two criteria for admitting a client to an acute mental health facility

  • Clear risk of the client's danger to self and others
  • Failure to meet expected outcomes of community-based treatment
  • A dangerous decline in the mental health status of a client undergoing long-term treatment
  • A client having a medical need in addition to a mental illness

Underlying principles — at least two concepts of mental health treatment

Goals of acute mental health treatment:

  • Prevention of the client harming self or others
  • Stabilizing mental health crises
  • Return of clients who are severely ill to some type of community care

Interprofessional team members: nurses, mental health technicians, psychologists, psychiatrists, other general health care providers, social workers, counselors, occupational and other specialty therapists, and pharmacists.

Nursing interventions — at least two for acute mental health care

  • Who: The interprofessional team member's primary responsibility is planning and monitoring individualized treatment plans or clinical pathways of care.
  • When: Plans for discharge to home or to a community facility begin from the time of admission.
  • How: Nursing roles include overall management of the unit, including client activities and therapeutic milieu; ensuring safe administration and monitoring of client medications; implementation of individual treatment plans, including client teaching; documentation of the nursing process for each client; managing crises as they arise.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 6.1 — Priority client in community programs

A nurse is planning care for several clients who are attending community-based mental health programs. Which of the following clients should the nurse visit first?

  1. A client who received a burn on the arm while using a hot iron at home
  2. A client who requests a change of antipsychotic medication due to some new adverse effects
  3. A client who reports hearing a voice saying that life is not worth living anymore
  4. A client who tells the nurse about experiencing manifestations of severe anxiety before and during a job interview
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — A client who hears a voice saying life is not worth living anymore is at the greatest risk for self-harm, so the nurse should visit this client first. This is a command auditory hallucination with suicidal content — an imminent safety priority.

  • A. The burn needs attention but is not immediately life-threatening.
  • B. Medication concerns are important but not urgent compared to suicidal ideation.
  • D. Anxiety surrounding a job interview is distressing but not an imminent safety priority.

NCLEX® Connection: Management of Care — Establishing Priorities

Exercise 6.2 — Tertiary prevention identification

A community mental health nurse is planning care to address the issue of depression among older adult clients in the community. Which of the following interventions should the nurse implement as a method of tertiary prevention?

  1. Educating clients on health promotion techniques to reduce the risk of depression
  2. Performing screenings for depression at community health programs
  3. Establishing rehabilitation programs to decrease the effects of depression
  4. Providing support groups for clients at risk for depression
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — Rehabilitation programs are an example of tertiary prevention. Tertiary prevention deals with preventing further problems in clients already diagnosed with mental illness.

  • A. Educating to reduce risk = primary prevention.
  • B. Screenings for depression = secondary prevention (early detection).
  • D. Support groups for clients at risk (not yet diagnosed) = primary prevention.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 6.3 — Community mental health services (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is working in a community mental health facility. Which of the following services does this type of program provide? Select all that apply.

  1. Educational groups
  2. Medication dispensing programs
  3. Individual counseling programs
  4. Detoxification programs
  5. Family therapy
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, B, C, E

  • A. Correct — Educational groups are core services at community mental health centers.
  • B. Correct — Medication dispensing programs are services provided in community mental health facilities.
  • C. Correct — Individual counseling programs are core services.
  • D. Incorrect — Detoxification programs are services provided in a partial hospitalization program, not community mental health centers.
  • E. Correct — Family therapy is a service provided in community mental health facilities.

NCLEX® Connection: Management of Care — Referrals

Exercise 6.4 — Discharge planning — best follow-up

A nurse in an acute mental health facility is assisting with discharge planning for a client who has a severe mental illness and requires supervision. The client's partner works all day but is home by late afternoon. Which of the following strategies should the nurse suggest for follow-up care?

  1. Receiving daily care from a home health aide
  2. Having a weekly visit from a nurse case worker
  3. Attending a partial hospitalization program
  4. Visiting a community mental health center on a daily basis
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C — A partial hospitalization program provides treatment during the day while allowing the client to spend nights at home, as long as a responsible family member is present. Here, the partner is home by late afternoon — perfect fit.

  • A. Daily home health aide care does not provide adequate supervision for severe mental illness.
  • B. Weekly case worker visits are insufficient supervision.
  • D. Daily visits to a community mental health center don't provide consistent supervision.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 6.5 — ACT referral candidate

A nurse is caring for a group of clients. Which of the following clients should a nurse consider for referral to an assertive community treatment (ACT) group?

  1. A client in an acute care mental health facility who has fallen several times while running down the hallway
  2. A client who lives at home and keeps "forgetting" to come in for a scheduled monthly antipsychotic injection for schizophrenia
  3. A client in a day treatment program who reports increasing anxiety during group therapy
  4. A client in a weekly grief support group who reports still missing a deceased partner who has been dead for 3 months
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B — An ACT group works with clients who are nonadherent with traditional therapy — exactly the case of the client in a home setting who keeps "forgetting" a scheduled injection for schizophrenia. ACT teams provide nontraditional case management and outreach.

  • A. A client in acute care running and falling should be helped by the treatment team on the unit.
  • C. A client with anxiety in a day treatment program might be referred to a counselor or provider for evaluation.
  • D. A client grieving for a deceased partner who died 3 months ago is currently involved in an appropriate intervention (grief support group).

NCLEX® Connection: Management of Care — Referrals

Unit 2 · Traditional Nonpharmacological Therapies · Chapter 7

Psychoanalysis, Psychotherapy, and Behavioral Therapies

The major nonpharmacological treatment modalities — psychoanalytic, cognitive, and behavioral — and the nurse's role in each.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Psychoanalysis (Freud) explores unconscious conflicts rooted in past/childhood experiences and relationships; it is long-term and uses free association (saying whatever comes to mind), dream analysis, and transference (the client unconsciously relates to the therapist as a significant figure from the past). Cognitive therapies change distorted thinking — cognitive reframing, monitoring thoughts, priority restructuring, and journal keeping. Behavioral therapies change behavior through conditioning — operant conditioning (reinforcement), aversion therapy (pairs an unwanted behavior with an unpleasant stimulus, e.g., disulfiram + alcohol), systematic desensitization (gradual exposure + relaxation), flooding (rapid full exposure), modeling, biofeedback, and relaxation training.

Cognitive techniques

  • Cognitive reframing — replace irrational/negative thoughts with realistic ones
  • Monitoring thoughts — identify automatic distortions
  • Priority restructuring — re-rank what matters
  • Journal keeping — track thoughts/triggers

Behavioral techniques

  • Operant conditioning — reinforce desired behavior
  • Aversion therapy — pair maladaptive behavior with an unpleasant stimulus
  • Systematic desensitization — graded exposure with relaxation
  • Flooding — intense, sustained exposure to the feared stimulus

Psychoanalysis

Classical psychoanalysis is a long-term process of surfacing unconscious thoughts and feelings and resolving conflict through talking with a psychoanalyst, over many sessions across months to years. Because of its length and insurance constraints, it is rarely the sole therapy used today.

Classical psychoanalysis

  • Developed by Sigmund Freud to resolve internal conflicts he believed originate in early childhood experiences
  • Past relationships are a common focus, used to uncover unconscious conflicts
  • Freud's original form is seldom used today; length and cost limit its use

Transference vs. countertransference

Transference

  • Feelings the client develops toward the therapist
  • Mirror feelings the client held toward significant people in early childhood

Countertransference

  • Unconscious feelings the health care worker has toward the client
  • The client may remind the worker of someone from their past, positively or negatively

Therapeutic tools

  • Free association — spontaneous, uncensored verbalization of whatever comes to mind
  • Dream analysis and interpretation — Freud viewed dreams as urges/impulses of the unconscious played out in sleep
  • Use of defense mechanisms

Psychotherapy & Its Variants

Psychotherapy involves more verbal therapist-to-client interaction than classical psychoanalysis, built on a trusting relationship to explore the client's problems. Several variants target different goals.

Talk-based therapies

Psychodynamic psychotherapy

  • Uses the same tools as psychoanalysis
  • Focuses more on the client's present state than early life
  • Tends to last longer than other approaches

Interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT)

  • Addresses specific problems; improves relationships, communication, role-relationships, and bereavement
  • Premise: many disorders are influenced by interpersonal interactions and social context
  • Goal: improve interpersonal/social functioning to reduce psychiatric manifestations

Cognitive therapy

  • Based on the cognitive model — thoughts come before feelings and actions
  • Focuses on individual thoughts/behaviors to solve current problems
  • Treats depression, anxiety, eating disorders, and more by changing attitude toward experiences

Behavioral & Combined Therapies

Behavioral theorists (Pavlov, Watson, Skinner) held that changing behavior, not insight, is the key to treating problems. Behavior is learned and has consequences; maladaptive behavior can be changed without insight into its underlying cause.

Behavioral therapy

  • Abnormal behavior is seen as an attempt to avoid painful feelings
  • Teaches clients ways to decrease anxiety or avoidant behavior and practice techniques (relaxation training, modeling)
  • Used successfully for phobias, substance use/addictive disorders, and other issues

Combined cognitive-behavioral approaches

Cognitive-behavioral therapy (CBT)

  • Uses both cognitive and behavioral approaches for anxiety management
  • Accounts for how thoughts influence feelings and behaviors

Dialectical behavior therapy (DBT)

  • A CBT for clients who have a personality disorder and exhibit self-injurious behavior
  • Focuses on gradual behavior change while providing acceptance and validation

Cognitive Therapy Techniques

Cognitive techniques target the distorted thoughts that produce anxiety, helping clients replace negative self-talk with supportive ideas.

Cognitive reframing

  • Changing cognitive distortions can decrease anxiety
  • Client identifies negative thoughts, examines their cause, and develops supportive ideas to replace negative self-talk
  • Example: a client who says they are "a bad person who never did anything good" learns to see that they may have made some bad choices but are not "a bad person"

Supporting techniques

  • Priority restructuring — identify what requires priority (devote energy to pleasurable activities)
  • Journal keeping — write down stressful thoughts; has a positive effect on well-being
  • Assertiveness training — express feelings and solve problems in a nonaggressive manner
  • Monitoring thoughts — build awareness of negative thinking

Behavioral Therapy Techniques

Behavioral techniques apply learning principles — reinforcement, exposure, and substitution — to reduce maladaptive behavior. Each has a defined use in mental health nursing.

Reinforcement & modeling

Modeling

  • A therapist or others serve as role models; the client imitates the behavior to improve
  • Use: in acute care milieu to improve interpersonal skills (therapist demonstrates appropriate behavior in a stressful situation)

Operant conditioning

  • Client receives positive rewards for positive behavior (positive reinforcement)
  • Use: a token economy — client earns tokens for good behavior to exchange for privileges or items

Exposure-based techniques

Systematic desensitization

  • Planned, graduated exposure to anxiety-provoking stimuli (real or imagined)
  • Begins with mastering relaxation techniques, then increasing exposure while using relaxation to overcome anxiety
  • Used for clients who have phobias

Flooding

  • Exposing a client (with a therapist present) to a great deal of an undesirable stimulus to turn off the anxiety response

Response prevention

  • Preventing a client from performing a compulsive behavior, with the intent that anxiety will diminish

Aversion, relaxation & other techniques

  • Aversion therapy — pairing a maladaptive behavior with a punishment/unpleasant stimulus (e.g., bitter taste, mild shock) for behaviors such as alcohol use disorder, violence, self-mutilation, thumb-sucking; requires ongoing supervision/evaluation
  • Relaxation techniques — meditation, guided imagery, diaphragmatic breathing, muscle relaxation, biofeedback control pain, tension, and anxiety (e.g., teach diaphragmatic breathing during a panic attack or labor)
  • Thought stopping — when negative thoughts/compulsions arise, the client says/shouts "stop" and substitutes a positive thought; over time uses the command silently
  • Validation therapy — for clients with neurocognitive disorders: communicate with a disoriented older adult by respecting and validating their feelings in a time/place that is real to them

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Therapeutic Procedure.

Operant conditioning for a withdrawn client

Therapeutic Procedure · Behavioral therapy

A nurse on an acute mental health unit is caring for a client who has a personality disorder. The client refuses to attend group meetings, will not speak to other clients or attend unit activities, but enjoys visiting with staff and requests a daily walk outside with a staff member. The provider prescribes behavioral therapy with operant conditioning.

Description of procedure

Behavioral therapy modifies behavior by manipulating its consequences. Operant conditioning increases a desired behavior by following it with a positive reinforcer (reward) and decreases an undesired behavior by withholding reinforcement.

Outcomes / evaluation

An appropriate outcome: the client will attend at least one group activity within a defined time frame.

Nursing interventions

Use the client's valued reward as the reinforcer — for example, allow the daily outdoor walk with staff only after the client attends a group session or unit activity, reinforcing the desired participation behavior.

Exercise 7.1 — Classical Psychoanalysis

A nurse is teaching a client who has an anxiety disorder and is scheduled to begin classical psychoanalysis. Which of the following client statements indicates an understanding of this form of therapy?

  1. "Even if my anxiety improves, I will need to continue this therapy for 6 weeks."
  2. "The therapist will focus on my past relationships during our sessions."
  3. "Psychoanalysis will help me reduce my anxiety by changing my behaviors."
  4. "This therapy will address my conscious feelings about stressful experiences."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Psychoanalysis is long-term and open-ended, not a fixed 6-week course.
  • B. Correct — Classical psychoanalysis places emphasis on past experiences and relationships and their influence on current behavior.
  • C. Changing behaviors is the focus of behavioral therapy, not psychoanalysis.
  • D. Psychoanalysis explores unconscious (not conscious) conflicts.

Exercise 7.2 — Free Association

A nurse is discussing free association as a therapeutic tool with a client who has major depressive disorder. Which of the following client statements indicates understanding of this technique?

  1. "I will write down my dreams as soon as I wake up."
  2. "I might begin to associate my therapist with important people in my life."
  3. "I can learn to express myself in a nonaggressive manner."
  4. "I should say the first thing that comes to my mind."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. Recording dreams describes dream analysis, a separate tool.
  • B. This describes transference, not free association.
  • C. This describes assertiveness training.
  • D. Correct — Free association is the spontaneous, uncensored verbalization of whatever comes to mind.

Exercise 7.4 — Aversion Therapy

A nurse is caring for a client who has a new prescription for disulfiram for treatment of alcohol use disorder. The nurse informs the client that this medication can cause nausea and vomiting when alcohol is consumed. This method is an example of which of the following types of treatment?

  1. Aversion therapy
  2. Flooding
  3. Biofeedback
  4. Dialectical behavior therapy
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Aversion therapy pairs a maladaptive behavior (drinking alcohol) with an unpleasant stimulus (nausea/vomiting from disulfiram).
  • B. Flooding is planned, intense exposure to a feared stimulus.
  • C. Biofeedback teaches control over physiologic responses.
  • D. Dialectical behavior therapy is a cognitive-behavioral approach for emotion regulation.

Exercise 7.5 — Systematic Desensitization

A nurse is assisting with systematic desensitization for a client who has an extreme fear of elevators. Which of the following actions should the nurse implement with this form of therapy?

  1. Demonstrate riding in an elevator, and then ask the client to imitate the behavior.
  2. Advise the client to say "stop" out loud every time they begin to feel an anxiety response related to an elevator.
  3. Gradually expose the client to an elevator while practicing relaxation techniques.
  4. Stay with the client in an elevator until the anxiety response diminishes.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Demonstration followed by imitation describes modeling.
  • B. Saying "stop" describes thought stopping.
  • C. Correct — Systematic desensitization is the gradual exposure to a feared stimulus while practicing relaxation, replacing the anxiety response.
  • D. Sustained full exposure until anxiety diminishes describes flooding.
