Unit 2 · Traditional Nonpharmacological Therapies · Chapter 8

Group and Family Therapy

Group leadership styles and phases, therapeutic member roles, and the family-as-system approach to therapy.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Group leadership styles: autocratic (leader controls direction), democratic (leader supports/encourages member input and discussion), and laissez-faire (leader allows the group to operate without interference). Group phases: the initial (orientation) phase defines purpose, sets confidentiality expectations, and discusses termination plans; the working phase pursues goals and surfaces informal roles; the termination phase summarizes and ends. Member roles: task roles move work forward, maintenance (group-building) roles support cohesion (encourager, follower), and individual (self-serving) roles disrupt (e.g., bragging, dominating). Family therapy treats the family as a system; watch for dysfunctional patterns — manipulation, blaming, placation, distraction, triangulation (pulling in a third party), subgroups, and hidden agendas.

Group phases — initial vs working

  • Initial: define purpose, set confidentiality, plan for termination
  • Working: pursue goals, identify informal member roles
  • Termination: review progress, end the group

Family dysfunctional behaviors

  • Manipulation — controlling others for one's own gain
  • Blaming — faulting others to avoid responsibility
  • Placation — over-taking responsibility to keep peace
  • Distraction — inserting irrelevant content to derail

Group Therapy Foundations

Group therapy offers each client opportunities for growth and a sense of belonging, in a more cost-effective setting than individual therapy. A leader guides the group using one of several leadership styles.

Leadership styles

Democratic

  • Supports group interaction and decision-making to solve problems

Laissez-faire

  • Progresses with no attempt by the leader to control direction

Autocratic

  • Leader completely controls direction and structure, with no group interaction or shared decision-making

Goals & examples

  • Share common feelings/concerns and stories/experiences; diminish isolation; create a community of healing
  • More cost-effective than individual therapy
  • Examples: stress management, substance use disorders, medication education, understanding mental illness, dual-diagnosis groups

Age groups

  • Children: in the form of play while discussing a common experience
  • Adolescents: especially valuable — this age group typically has strong peer relationships
  • Older adults: helps with socialization and sharing of memories

Group Process, Roles & Characteristics

Beyond its purpose, every group has a process and structure — how members communicate, the roles they take, and how the setting shapes the work.

Key group concepts

  • Group process — the verbal/nonverbal communication during sessions and how members interact
  • Group norm — the way the group behaves over time, providing structure (e.g., raising a hand before speaking)
  • Hidden agenda — members (or the leader) with goals different from the stated group goals, which can disrupt the process
  • Subgroup — a small number of people within the larger group who function separately
  • Open vs. closed — open groups admit new members as others leave; closed groups admit none after formation
  • Homogeneous (members share a chosen characteristic, e.g., diagnosis/gender) vs. heterogeneous (no shared chosen characteristic)

Member roles

Maintenance roles

  • Help maintain the purpose/process (e.g., the harmonizer prevents conflict)

Task roles

  • Take on tasks in the process (e.g., the recorder takes notes)

Individual roles

  • Prevent teamwork by promoting a personal agenda (e.g., the dominator, the recognition seeker)

Characteristics by setting

Acute mental health setting

  • Members vary daily; focus on relief; unit activities affect the group; leader provides a higher level of structure

Outpatient setting

  • Members consistent; focus on growth; external influences limited; leader allows members to help set direction

Phases of Group Development

Therapeutic groups move through three phases, with the leader's role shifting from active structuring toward facilitation as cohesiveness builds.

Orientation phase

  • Focus: define the purpose and goals of the group
  • Leader sets a tone of respect, trust, and confidentiality and is active in providing the group's purpose
  • Members get to know each other and the leader; termination is discussed

Working phase

  • Focus: promote problem-solving skills to facilitate behavioral change; power and control issues dominate
  • Leader uses therapeutic communication to encourage work toward goals
  • Members take informal roles (can help or hinder progress); cohesiveness is established and the leader's role gradually diminishes

Termination phase

  • Focus: marks the end of group sessions
  • Members discuss termination issues; leader summarizes the group's work and individual contributions
  • Feedback about the group therapy is elicited

Family Structures

A family is the first and most influential system to which a person belongs — a group with reciprocal relationships whose members are committed to each other. Structures vary widely and are often nontraditional.

Types of families

  • Nuclear — children with married parents
  • Single-parent — children with one adult (related or nonrelated)
  • Adoptive — children with parents who adopted them
  • Blended — children with one biological/adoptive parent and a nonrelated, married stepparent
  • Cohabitating — children with one biological parent and a nonrelated cohabitating adult
  • Extended — children with one biological/adoptive parent and a related adult who is not their parent (grandparent, aunt, uncle)
  • Other — children with related/nonrelated adults who are neither biological nor adoptive parents (grandparents, adult siblings, foster parents)

Family Functioning: Healthy vs. Dysfunctional

Families function across five areas — communication, management, boundaries, socialization, and emotional support. Dysfunction can occur in one or more areas.

Communication & boundaries

Healthy

  • Clear, understandable messages; each member encouraged to express feelings/thoughts
  • Boundaries distinguish roles, are clear and understood by all

Dysfunctional communication

  • Blaming, manipulating, placating, distracting, generalizing ("always"/"never")

Dysfunctional boundaries

  • Enmeshed — roles/feelings blend so individual roles are unclear
  • Rigid — inflexible rules/roles; members isolate, minimal communication

Management, socialization & emotional support

Healthy

  • Management: adults agree on important issues (rules, finances, future)
  • Socialization: members interact and adopt healthy coping; children learn to function in family and society
  • Emotional: needs met most of the time; members care about each other; conflict/anger don't dominate

Dysfunctional

  • Management: chaotic; a child may make management decisions
  • Socialization: children don't learn healthy skills; difficulty adapting to society
  • Emotional: negatives predominate; members isolated, afraid, unconcerned for each other

Other dysfunction concepts & discipline

  • Scapegoating — a low-power member is blamed for family problems
  • Triangulation — a third party is drawn into an unstable two-member relationship (e.g., a parent allies with a child against the other parent)
  • Multigenerational issues — emotional themes continuing ≥ three generations (substance use patterns, dysfunctional grief, triangulation, divorce)
  • Discipline — should be consistent, timely, age-appropriate, administered in private when calm, with caregivers in unison

Family Therapy

In family therapy the focus is on the family as a system, not on each person as an individual.

Nursing role

  • Family assessments use focused interviews and family assessment tools
  • Provide teaching (e.g., medication administration; helping a member manage a disorder)
  • Mobilize family resources, improve communication, and strengthen the family's ability to cope with one member's illness

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Family beginning therapy

Basic Concept · Family therapy

A nurse is creating a plan of care for a family that is planning to begin therapy to improve the emotional and supportive aspect of the family unit.

Related content — definition of a family

A family is a group with reciprocal relationships in which members are committed to each other.

Underlying principles

Family therapy focuses on the family as a system; a change in one member affects the whole. It aims to improve communication, role function, and the emotional/supportive functioning of the unit.

Nursing interventions

Outcomes: family members express feelings openly; members provide mutual support. Interventions: establish trust and ground rules; facilitate balanced participation so each member can speak without interruption or blame.

Exercise 8.1 — Democratic Leadership

A nurse wants to use democratic leadership with a group whose purpose is to learn appropriate conflict resolution techniques. The nurse is correct in implementing this form of group leadership when demonstrating which of the following actions?

  1. Observes group techniques without interfering with the group process
  2. Discusses a technique and then directs members to practice the technique
  3. Asks for group suggestions of techniques and then supports discussion
  4. Suggests techniques and asks group members to reflect on their use

Answer: C

  • A. Observing without interfering describes laissez-faire leadership.
  • B, D. Directing and suggesting techniques describe autocratic leadership.

Exercise 8.3 — Subgroup

A nurse on an acute mental health unit forms a group to focus on self-management of medications. At each of the meetings, two of the members conspire together to exclude the rest of the group. This is an example of which of the following concepts?

  1. Triangulation
  2. Group process
  3. Subgroup
  4. Hidden agenda
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • B. Group process is the verbal/nonverbal communication among members.

  • D. A hidden agenda is a concealed goal that conflicts with the group's stated purpose.

A nurse is conducting a family therapy session. The younger child tells the nurse about plans to make the older sibling look bad, believing this will earn more freedom and privileges. The nurse should identify this dysfunctional behavior as which of the following?

  1. Placation
  2. Manipulation
  3. Blaming
  4. Distraction

Answer: B

  • A. Placation is taking on excessive responsibility to keep peace.
  • B. Correct — Manipulation is influencing or controlling others for one's own gain (here, to gain privileges).
  • C. Blaming faults others to avoid personal responsibility.
  • D. Distraction inserts irrelevant content to derail communication.

Exercise 8.5 — Individual (Self-Serving) Role

A nurse is working with an established group and identifies various member roles. Which of the following should the nurse identify as an individual role?

  1. A member who praises input from other members
  2. A member who follows the direction of other members
  3. A member who brags about accomplishments
  4. A member who evaluates the group's performance toward a standard
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A, B. Praising input and following direction are maintenance (group-building) roles.
  • C. Correct — Bragging about accomplishments is an individual (self-serving) role that does not advance the group.
  • D. Evaluating performance toward a standard is a task role.

Unit 2 · Traditional Nonpharmacological Therapies · Chapter 9

Stress Management

The physiology of stress, acute vs. prolonged responses, and evidence-based stress-reduction techniques.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Stress is the body's response to any demand. Eustress is positive/motivating; distress results from excessive or negative stressors. Both positive and negative stressors produce a biological response. The general adaptation syndrome moves through alarm (fight-or-flight), resistance, and exhaustion. Acute stress findings include increased blood pressure and short-lived effects; prolonged/chronic stress produces chronic pain, panic attacks, and lasting immune effects. Stress-reduction techniques: cognitive reframing (change irrational thoughts), assertive communication (use "I" statements to change a situation), relaxation methods (progressive muscle relaxation, diaphragmatic breathing, meditation, mindfulness, guided imagery), biofeedback (mechanical feedback to gain control over physiologic responses), regular exercise (releases endorphins), and journaling.

Acute vs prolonged stress

  • Acute: increased BP, unhappiness, short-term effects
  • Prolonged: chronic pain, panic attacks, lasting immune changes

Technique → purpose

  • Cognitive reframing — reshape irrational thoughts
  • Assertiveness — change a stressful situation
  • Relaxation/mindfulness — decrease the stress response
  • Biofeedback — control physiologic responses via feedback

Stress & Resilience

Stress is the body's nonspecific response to any demand. Some stressors give life interest and purpose, but too much stress causes distress — and effects of stressors are cumulative. The body responds to a perceived/actual threat with the fight-or-flight response; prolonged stress leads to maladaptive responses.

Stress management & resilience factors

  • Stress management = experiencing appropriate emotions and coping; the healthy manager is flexible and uses varied techniques
  • Responses are affected by age, gender, culture, life experiences, and lifestyle
  • Protective/resilience factors: physical health, strong sense of self, religious/spiritual beliefs, optimism, hobbies/outside interests, satisfying relationships, strong social support, humor

Findings & Screening

Stress produces measurable findings that differ between the acute fight-or-flight response and prolonged, maladaptive stress.

Acute vs. prolonged stress

Acute stress (fight or flight)

  • Apprehension; unhappiness/sorrow; decreased appetite
  • Increased respiratory rate, heart rate, cardiac output, blood pressure
  • Increased metabolism/glucose use; depressed immune system

Prolonged stress (maladaptive)

  • Chronic anxiety/panic attacks; depression, chronic pain, sleep disturbances; weight gain or loss
  • Increased risk for MI and stroke; poor diabetes control; hypertension; fatigue; irritability; decreased concentration; increased infection risk

Standardized screening tools

  • Holmes and Rahe Stress Scale — life-changing-events questionnaire measuring Life Change Units
  • Perceived Stress Scale
  • Lazarus's Cognitive Appraisal

Cognitive Stress-Reduction Techniques

Most nursing care involves teaching stress-reduction strategies. Cognitive techniques restructure the thoughts that drive stress.

Cognitive techniques

  • Cognitive reframing — help the client view irrational thoughts more realistically and restructure them positively (e.g., "I'm a terrible parent" → "I've made mistakes but learned and improved")
  • Mindfulness — be mindful of surroundings using all senses; restructure negative interpretations into positive ones
  • Assertiveness training — communicate assertively to decrease psychological stressors (describe the situation, state feelings, request a change)

Behavioral Stress-Reduction Techniques

Behavioral techniques use relaxation, structure, and feedback to lower the physiologic stress response.

Relaxation techniques

  • Meditation (including prayer), guided imagery, breathing exercises, progressive muscle relaxation
  • Physical exercise (yoga, walking, biking) releases endorphins that lower anxiety, promote relaxation, and have antidepressant effects
  • Use nursing judgment about relaxation techniques for clients with acute psychotic manifestations

Other behavioral & individual techniques

  • Journal writing — therapeutic release; eases anxiety, worry, obsessional thinking; increases confidence/hope; helps identify stressors
  • Priority restructuring — re-prioritize to reduce stressors (e.g., delegate tasks rather than doing all alone)
  • Biofeedback — a device helps the client gain voluntary control of autonomic functions (heart rate, blood pressure)
  • Other: individual hobbies (fishing, scrapbooking), music therapy, pet therapy, sleep, massage, aerobic exercise

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Teaching stress-reduction strategies

Basic Concept · Stress management

A nurse is leading a peer group discussion about teaching stress-related strategies to clients.

Nursing interventions — three techniques and how to explain them

  • Diaphragmatic (deep) breathing — "Breathe in slowly through your nose, let your abdomen rise, and exhale slowly; this calms the body's stress response."
  • Progressive muscle relaxation — "Tense and then release each muscle group from feet to head to release physical tension."
  • Meditation / mindfulness — "Focus attention on the present moment or a single point to quiet racing thoughts and reduce arousal."

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 9.1 — Stress Concepts

A nurse is preparing an educational seminar on stress for other nursing staff. Which of the following information should the nurse include in the discussion?

  1. Excessive stressors cause the client to experience distress.
  2. The body's initial adaptive response to stress is denial.
  3. Absence of stressors results in homeostasis.
  4. Negative, rather than positive, stressors produce a biological response.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Distress is the result of excessive or negative stressors.
  • B. The body's initial adaptive response is the alarm (fight-or-flight) reaction; denial is part of the grief process.
  • C. Some stressors are needed; their complete absence does not equal homeostasis.
  • D. Both positive and negative stressors produce a biological response.

NCLEX® Connection: Health Promotion and Maintenance — Aging Process/Stress

Exercise 9.2 — Acute Stress Response (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is discussing acute vs. prolonged stress with a client. Which of the following effects should the nurse identify as an acute stress response? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Chronic pain
  2. Depressed immune system
  3. Increased blood pressure
  4. Panic attacks
  5. Unhappiness
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, C, E

  • A. Incorrect — Chronic pain indicates a prolonged/chronic stress response.
  • B. Correct — A depressed immune system is identified as an acute stress response.
  • C. Correct — Increased blood pressure is an acute stress response.
  • D. Incorrect — Panic attacks indicate a prolonged/chronic response.
  • E. Correct — Unhappiness is an indicator of acute stress.

NCLEX® Connection: Health Promotion and Maintenance — Aging Process/Stress

Exercise 9.3 — Stress-Reduction Techniques

A nurse is teaching a client about stress-reduction techniques. Which of the following client statements indicates understanding of the teaching?

  1. "Cognitive reframing will help me change my irrational thoughts to something positive."
  2. "Progressive muscle relaxation uses a mechanical device to help me gain control over my pulse rate."
  3. "Biofeedback causes my body to release endorphins so that I feel less stress and anxiety."
  4. "Mindfulness allows me to prioritize the stressors that I have in my life so that I have less anxiety."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Cognitive reframing helps the client view a situation differently by changing irrational thoughts to positive ones.
  • B. Using a mechanical device to gain control over physiologic responses describes biofeedback, not progressive muscle relaxation.
  • C. Physical exercise (not biofeedback) causes the release of endorphins.
  • D. Priority restructuring (not mindfulness) involves prioritizing stressors.

NCLEX® Connection: Health Promotion and Maintenance — Aging Process/Stress

Exercise 9.4 — Changing the Situation

A nurse is talking with a client who reports increased stress because a new partner is "pressuring me and my kids to go live with him. I love him, but I'm not ready to do that." Which of the following recommendations should the nurse make to promote a change in the client's situation?

  1. Learn to practice mindfulness.
  2. Use assertiveness techniques.
  3. Exercise regularly.
  4. Rely on the support of a close friend.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A, C, D. Mindfulness, exercise, and social support decrease the stress response but do not change the situation itself.
  • B. Correct — Assertive communication allows the client to express needs and set boundaries, changing the stressful situation.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Coping Mechanisms

Exercise 9.5 — Assertive Communication

A nurse is caring for a client who states, "I'm so stressed at work because of my coworker. I am expected to finish others' work because of their laziness!" When discussing effective communication, which of the following statements by the client to the coworker indicates client understanding?

  1. "You really should complete your own work. I don't think it's right to expect me to complete your responsibilities."
  2. "Why do you expect me to finish your work? You must realize that I have my own responsibilities."
  3. "It is not fair to expect me to complete your work. If you continue, then I will report your behavior to our supervisor."
  4. "When I have to pick up extra work, I feel very overwhelmed. I need to focus on my own responsibilities."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. This is disapproving/judgmental communication.
  • B. A "why" question puts the coworker on the defensive.
  • C. This is aggressive and threatening.
  • D. Correct — This is assertive communication using an "I" statement to express feelings and needs without attacking the coworker.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Unit 2 · Traditional Nonpharmacological Therapies · Chapter 10

Brain Stimulation Therapies

Electroconvulsive therapy, transcranial magnetic stimulation, and vagus nerve stimulation — indications, procedures, and nursing care.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) is indicated for major depressive disorder unresponsive to medication, and for bipolar disorder (including rapid cycling) and acute suicidality/mania. A typical course is 2-3 times per week for 6-12 treatments. ECT does not cure depression but reduces relapse severity. Pre-procedure: informed consent, NPO, baseline assessment; intra-procedure: a short-acting anesthetic (methohexital), a muscle relaxant (succinylcholine) to prevent injury, and atropine to reduce secretions; post-procedure: expect transient short-term memory loss and confusion. Transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS) is noninvasive, requires no anesthesia, lasts 30-40 min, is given 3-5 times per week for several weeks, and treats major depression. Vagus nerve stimulation (VNS) uses an implanted device; adverse effects include voice changes, cough, and neck pain.

ECT essentials

  • For drug-resistant major depression, bipolar (rapid cycling), acute suicidality
  • 2-3×/week, 6-12 treatments
  • Succinylcholine prevents injury; methohexital for anesthesia
  • Expect transient memory loss + confusion afterward

TMS vs VNS

  • TMS — noninvasive, no anesthesia, 30-40 min, 3-5×/week
  • VNS — implanted; voice changes, cough, neck pain
  • Seizure activity and disorientation relate to ECT, not VNS

Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT)

ECT uses electrical current to induce brief seizure activity while the client is anesthetized. The exact mechanism is unknown; one theory is that the seizure enhances the effects of neurotransmitters (serotonin, dopamine, norepinephrine).

Indications

Major depressive disorder

  • Not responsive to pharmacological treatment
  • Risks of other treatments outweigh ECT's risks
  • Suicidal/homicidal clients needing a rapid response; psychotic manifestations

Other indications

  • Schizophrenia with catatonic manifestations; schizoaffective disorder
  • Acute manic episodes — bipolar with rapid cycling (≥4 episodes/year); unresponsive to lithium + antipsychotics

ECT Procedure & Nursing Care

A typical course is 2-3 times/week for 6-12 treatments. The provider obtains informed consent (next of kin or court order if involuntary). Pre-ECT workup may include chest x-ray, blood work, and ECG; benzodiazepines are discontinued because they interfere with the seizure.

Medication management

  • 30 min prior: IM atropine sulfate or glycopyrrolate to decrease secretions (aspiration risk) and counter vagal bradycardia
  • At procedure: short-acting anesthetic (etomidate or propofol) IV bolus
  • Muscle relaxant (succinylcholine) paralyzes muscles to reduce injury — also paralyzes respiratory muscles, so the client needs assisted ventilation/oxygenation

Nursing actions (intra/post)

  • Assess client/family understanding and teach — correct media-driven misconceptions (modern ECT does not cause the old visible tonic-clonic seizure)
  • Maintain an IV line until full recovery; apply EEG electrodes; provide 100% oxygen until spontaneous respirations return
  • Provide ongoing cardiac monitoring (BP, ECG, oxygen saturation); control severe hypertension
  • Clients are expected to become alert about 15 min after ECT

ECT Contraindications & Complications

There are no absolute contraindications, but assess for conditions that raise the risk of adverse effects.

Higher-risk conditions & poor candidates

  • Cardiovascular (recent MI, hypertension, heart failure, arrhythmias) — ECT increases stress on the heart during the seizure
  • Cerebrovascular (stroke history, brain tumor, subdural hematoma) — ECT increases intracranial pressure and cerebral blood flow
  • Not found useful for substance use disorders, personality disorders, or dysphoric disorder

Complications

  • Memory loss/confusion — short-term loss, confusion, disorientation; retrograde amnesia (no memory of events leading to/during the procedure); can persist weeks; most clients fully recover. Nursing: frequent orientation, safe environment, assist with hygiene
  • Reactions to anesthesia — continuous monitoring during and immediately after
  • Cardiovascular changes — monitor vital signs and cardiac rhythm per protocol
  • Relapse — ECT is not a permanent cure; weekly/monthly maintenance ECT can decrease relapse

Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation (TMS)

TMS is a noninvasive therapy using MRI-strength magnetic pulsations to stimulate the cerebral cortex. It is FDA-approved for major depressive disorder unresponsive to medication and is similar to ECT but does not induce a seizure.

Considerations & complications

  • Commonly daily for 4-6 weeks; outpatient; each procedure 30-40 min; the client is alert throughout
  • A noninvasive electromagnet is placed on the scalp; clients may feel tapping/knocking, scalp contraction, and jaw tightening
  • Complications: mild discomfort/tingling at the site, headaches, lightheadedness; seizures are rare but possible
  • Contraindicated with cochlear implants, brain stimulators, or medication pumps (device metal interferes with treatment)

Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS)

VNS delivers electrical stimulation through the vagus nerve to the brain via a device surgically implanted under the skin of the chest (similar to a pacemaker). It is believed to increase neurotransmitter levels and enhance antidepressant medications.

Indications & considerations

  • Indications: depression resistant to pharmacological treatment and/or ECT (FDA-approved); research ongoing for anxiety, obesity, and pain
  • Outpatient surgical procedure; delivers around-the-clock programmed pulsations (typically every 5 min for 30 sec); therapeutic effect takes several weeks
  • The client can turn the device off at any time by placing a special external magnet over the implant

Complications

  • Voice changes (lead's proximity to the larynx/pharynx), hoarseness, throat/neck pain, coughing — commonly improve with time
  • Dyspnea, especially with exertion — the client may turn off the VNS during exercise or prolonged speaking

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Therapeutic Procedure.

Assisting with ECT

Therapeutic Procedure · ECT

A nurse is preparing to assist in providing electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) treatment for a client.

Description of procedure

ECT delivers a brief electrical current through the brain to induce a controlled generalized seizure, used primarily for major depression unresponsive to medication.

Nursing interventions — pre / intra / post

  • Pre: ensure informed consent and NPO status; manage medications (administer a short-acting anesthetic and a muscle relaxant such as succinylcholine; atropine reduces secretions).
  • Intra: maintain the airway, monitor oxygenation, and protect the client during the induced seizure.
  • Post: monitor vital signs and orientation; reorient the client and provide for safety because of expected transient confusion and short-term memory loss.

A nurse is providing teaching for a client who is scheduled to receive ECT for the treatment of major depressive disorder. Which of the following client statements indicates understanding of the teaching?

  2. "I can have my depression cured if I receive a series of ECT treatments."
  3. "I should receive ECT once a week for 6 weeks."
  4. "I will receive a muscle relaxant to protect me from injury during ECT."

Answer: D

  • A. ECT is used when depression is unresponsive to medication, not before trying medications.

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 11

Anxiety Disorders

Generalized anxiety, panic, phobias, OCD, and body dysmorphic disorder — assessment, safety, and therapeutic response.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Anxiety disorders share excessive fear/worry that impairs function. Generalized anxiety disorder (GAD): excessive worry on most days for ≥6 months with restlessness, fatigue, difficulty concentrating, irritability, muscle tension, and sleep disturbance. Panic disorder: recurrent panic attacks — during an attack, stay with the client, remain calm and quiet, and minimize stimuli (the client cannot learn new techniques mid-attack). OCD: compulsions (e.g., repetitive checking/grooming) are performed to reduce anxiety caused by obsessions. Phobias are persistent irrational fears. Body dysmorphic disorder: preoccupation with a perceived defect — the priority is assessing risk for self-harm. Use therapeutic communication: open-ended statements, avoid "why" questions and advice; postpone health teaching until acute anxiety subsides.

GAD hallmarks

  • Excessive worry, most days, ≥6 months
  • Restlessness, fatigue, irritability
  • Muscle tension, sleep disturbance

Acute panic — nursing priorities

  • Stay with the client; remain calm and quiet
  • Reduce environmental stimuli
  • Defer teaching/relaxation training until calm
  • Always assess safety/self-harm risk first

Types of Disorders

Normal anxiety is a healthy, survival response to stress; when it becomes elevated or persistent it impairs function. Anxiety progresses through levels — mild (restlessness, increased motivation), moderate (agitation, muscle tightness), severe (inability to function, ritualistic behavior), and panic (distorted perception, loss of rational thought, immobility).

Anxiety disorders (DSM-5)

  • Separation anxiety disorder — excessive fear/anxiety when separated from someone the client is emotionally attached to
  • Specific phobias — irrational fear of an object/situation (monophobia = being alone; zoophobia = animals; acrophobia = heights)
  • Agoraphobia — extreme fear of places where the client feels vulnerable or unsafe
  • Social anxiety disorder (social phobia) — excessive fear of social or performance situations
  • Panic disorder — recurrent panic attacks
  • Generalized anxiety disorder (GAD) — uncontrollable, excessive worry for at least 6 months

Obsessive-compulsive & related disorders

  • Not true anxiety disorders but have similar effects
  • OCD — intrusive obsessions controlled with compulsive behaviors (repetitive cleaning, handwashing)
  • Hoarding disorder — obsessive desire to save items regardless of value; higher prevalence in males
  • Body dysmorphic disorder — preoccupation with a perceived physical flaw/defect

Expected Findings

Findings vary by disorder but share physical and behavioral anxiety manifestations.

Phobias, separation & agoraphobia

  • Separation anxiety — excessive anxiety/concern when separated; fear of permanent separation; physical findings (headaches, nausea/vomiting, sleep disturbances)
  • Specific phobias — fear of objects/experiences; anxiety even when thinking of the trigger; may use alcohol/substances to decrease anxiety
  • Agoraphobia — avoids anxiety-causing places/situations; fear is out of proportion to actual danger

Social phobia, panic disorder & GAD

Social phobia

  • Difficulty performing/speaking in front of others; excessive fear of embarrassment; may report physical manifestations to avoid the situation

Panic disorder

  • Attacks typically last 15-30 min with ≥4 of: palpitations, shortness of breath, choking/smothering, chest pain, nausea, depersonalization, fear of dying/insanity, chills/hot flashes

GAD

  • Uncontrollable, excessive worry most days for ≥6 months; restlessness, muscle tension, avoidance, procrastination, sleep disturbance

OCD & related findings

  • OCD — suppress persistent thoughts/urges through compulsive behaviors; time-consuming, with impaired social/occupational functioning
  • Hoarding — extreme stress at discarding items; often leads to an unsafe living environment
  • Body dysmorphic — conceals a perceived flaw with repetitive behaviors (mirror checking, comparison); social/occupational impairment

Assessment & Screening

A medical cause must always be ruled out, since physical conditions and medications can mimic anxiety.

Risk factors

  • Most anxiety disorders are more common in females (hoarding is the exception — higher in males); genetic and neurobiological link
  • Acute medical conditions (hyperthyroidism, pulmonary embolism) can cause anxiety — assess to rule out a physical cause
  • Many medications' adverse effects mimic anxiety
  • Substance-induced anxiety relates to current use or withdrawal (e.g., alcohol)

Standardized screening tools

  • Hamilton Rating Scale for Anxiety
  • Fear Questionnaire (phobias)
  • Panic Disorder Severity Scale
  • Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale
  • Hoarding Scale Self-Report

Nursing Care

During the crisis period, the priority is safety and comfort — clients in severe or panic-level anxiety cannot problem-solve, focus, or learn.

Care during anxiety

  • Provide a structured interview to keep the client focused on the present; assess for comorbid substance use disorder
  • Provide a calm, quiet environment; remain with the client during the worst of the anxiety to provide reassurance
  • Perform a suicide risk assessment; provide a safe environment for clients and staff
  • Use milieu therapy, therapeutic communication (open-ended questions), and relaxation techniques
  • Instill hope but avoid false reassurance; enhance self-esteem; help identify defense mechanisms interfering with recovery
  • Postpone health teaching until acute anxiety subsides

Medications

Antidepressants are first-line; antianxiety agents help manage acute manifestations.

Medication classes

  • SSRIs (sertraline, paroxetine) — first-line for anxiety and OCD
  • SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine) — effective for anxiety disorders
  • Antianxiety agents: benzodiazepines (diazepam) for short-term use; buspirone for long-term management

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Therapeutic Procedure.

Systematic desensitization for elevator anxiety

Therapeutic Procedure · Behavioral therapy

A nurse in a mental health clinic is working with a client who experiences high levels of anxiety when riding in an elevator, and teaches the client about systematic desensitization as a form of behavioral therapy.

Description of procedure

Systematic desensitization gradually exposes the client to a hierarchy of feared situations while practicing relaxation, so the relaxation response replaces the anxiety response.

Indication

Useful for specific phobias and anxiety triggered by an identifiable stimulus (e.g., elevators).

Outcomes / evaluation

The client demonstrates relaxation techniques during exposure; the client rides in an elevator with manageable anxiety.

Exercise 11.1 — OCD Compulsions

A nurse observes a client who has OCD repeatedly applying, removing, and then reapplying makeup. The nurse identifies that repetitive behavior in a client who has OCD is due to which of the following underlying reasons?

  1. Narcissistic behavior
  2. Fear of rejection from staff
  3. Attempt to reduce anxiety
  4. Adverse effect of antidepressant medication

Answer: C

  • A. Narcissism involves seeking admiration, not anxiety-driven rituals.
  • B. Fear of rejection relates to avoidance, not compulsions.
  • C. Correct — Clients with OCD perform compulsive behaviors to reduce the anxiety produced by their obsessions.
  • D. Repetitive compulsions are a feature of OCD, not an antidepressant adverse effect.

A nurse is caring for a client who has generalized anxiety disorder and is experiencing severe anxiety. Which of the following statements should the nurse make?

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 12

Trauma- and Stressor-Related Disorders

PTSD and acute stress disorder, dissociative disorders, and nurse self-care during traumatic events.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

PTSD follows exposure to a traumatic event and persists longer than 1 month: re-experiencing (recurring nightmares, flashbacks), avoidance and reluctance to talk about the event, negative self-image, difficulty concentrating, and hyperarousal/increased startle. Acute stress disorder (ASD) begins immediately and lasts 3 days to 1 month, often with a sense of unreality/dissociation and inability to recall details. Dissociative disorders: depersonalization (feeling detached from one's body/floating), derealization (surroundings seem unreal — small/far away), dissociative amnesia, and dissociative fugue/identity disorder. Use grounding techniques (e.g., stomping feet, naming surroundings) for dissociation; avoid flooding the client with past information. Nurses in mass-casualty events prevent trauma reactions by taking breaks, hydrating, debriefing, expressing emotion, and using offered counseling.

PTSD vs ASD

  • PTSD: >1 month; nightmares, avoidance, negative self-image, hyperarousal
  • ASD: immediate onset, 3 days-1 month; dissociation/unreality

Dissociation types

  • Depersonalization — detached from one's own body
  • Derealization — surroundings feel unreal/distant
  • Fugue — grounding techniques; let client make routine decisions

Specific Disorders

Trauma- and stressor-related disorders develop after exposure to an extreme stressor (combat, interpersonal violence). Trauma-informed, survivor-centered care guides the nursing approach throughout.

Trauma- & stressor-related disorders

  • Acute stress disorder (ASD) — anxiety, detachment, and other manifestations lasting 3 days to 1 month after the event
  • Posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD) — same manifestations lasting longer than 1 month; can persist for years
  • Adjustment disorder — a stressor triggers mood changes/dysfunction; less severe than ASD or PTSD

Dissociative disorders

  • Depersonalization/derealization — depersonalization (observing one's own self/body from a distance) and/or derealization (outside events feel unreal)
  • Dissociative amnesia — inability to recall personal information tied to trauma
  • Dissociative fugue — a type of amnesia in which the client travels to a new area and cannot remember their identity
  • Dissociative identity disorder — more than one distinct personality, with a stressful event precipitating the change

Risk Factors & Prevention

Risk centers on the nature of the trauma, individual vulnerability, and the support received afterward.

Risk factors

  • Exposure to a traumatic event (motor-vehicle crash, assault, abuse); for adjustment disorder the event can be milder (relationship breakup, job loss)
  • Natural disasters, man-made events, occupational re-exposure (medical personnel, law enforcement), or vicarious trauma to a loved one
  • Severity of the trauma, individual vulnerabilities (prior coping, preexisting disorders), and insufficient treatment afterward
  • PTSD is itself a risk factor for dissociative, anxiety, depressive, and substance use disorders

Health promotion & prevention

  • Monitor for and report suspected child abuse promptly to proper authorities to prevent severe trauma reactions
  • Recognize high-incidence occupations (military, first responders) and provide support/treatment early
  • During a mass-casualty incident: ensure breaks/rest/water/nutrition, provide emotional support, debrief afterward, and encourage expression of feelings

Expected Findings

ASD and PTSD share intrusive, mood/cognitive, and behavioral manifestations.

ASD & PTSD

Intrusive & avoidance

  • Involuntary distressing memories, flashbacks (feeling the event is recurring), trauma-related dreams
  • Avoidance of people, places, or reminders; trying to avoid thinking of the event

Mood & cognitive

  • Anxiety/depression, anger/irritability, decreased interest
  • Guilt, negative self-beliefs, detachment, inability to feel positive emotions; dissociative manifestations

Behavioral

  • Aggression/irritability, hypervigilance with heightened startle, poor concentration, insomnia
  • Destructive behavior, including thoughts of self-harm or harming others

Adjustment & dissociative disorders

  • Adjustment disorder — depression, anxiety, behavior changes (arguing, erratic driving)
  • Dissociative — feeling detached from one's body/environment; memory gaps ranging from details to a lifetime; two or more distinct personalities (DID)

Diagnostic & Screening

Diagnosis combines screening tools, a mental status exam, and ruling out physical causes for dissociative findings.

Diagnostic procedures

  • ASD/PTSD/adjustment: Primary Care PTSD Screen and PTSD Checklist; anxiety/depression screening; ask about suicidal ideation; mental status exam
  • Dissociative: physical assessment, EEG, and x-rays to rule out physical trauma (TBI, epilepsy); substance screening
  • Assess recent/remote memory for gaps; use tools such as the Dissociative Experience Scale

Nursing Care

Trauma-informed care addresses the client's emotional, psychological, and physiological needs while maintaining safety.

Care & client education

  • Establish a therapeutic relationship; provide a safe, nonthreatening, routine environment
  • Assess for suicidal ideation and take precautions as needed
  • Decrease anxiety with multiple strategies (music therapy, guided imagery, massage, relaxation, breathing); for children, involve caregivers and use play/art
  • Dissociative: help with decisions during dissociative periods, encourage independence when ready, use grounding techniques (clap hands, touch an object), and avoid giving too much information about past events
  • Client education: practice anxiety-reduction strategies, verbalize feelings at own pace, avoid caffeine/alcohol

Medications & Therapies

Medications target depression/anxiety and hyperarousal; therapies focus on processing the trauma safely.

Medications (ASD/PTSD)

  • Antidepressants — SSRIs (paroxetine, sertraline), SNRI (venlafaxine), mirtazapine, TCA (amitriptyline)
  • Prazosin (centrally-acting alpha agonist) — decreases hypervigilance and insomnia
  • Propranolol (beta-blocker) — decreases elevated vital signs, anxiety, panic, hypervigilance, insomnia
  • Adjustment/dissociative disorders: medications used only if depression/anxiety findings require treatment

Therapeutic procedures

  • CBT (cognitive restructuring) — change distorted appraisals and negative thoughts
  • Prolonged exposure therapy — relaxation plus imagined or in-vivo exposure to reduce the anxiety response over time
  • EMDR — rapid eye movements during desensitization; contraindicated with active suicidal ideation, psychosis, severe dissociative disorders, detached retina/glaucoma, or unstable substance use
  • Group/family therapy, crisis intervention, hypnotherapy, and biofeedback/neurofeedback

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: System Disorder.

PTSD following combat

System Disorder · PTSD

A nurse is caring for a client who has posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD) following several months in a military combat situation.

Alteration in health — PTSD vs ASD

PTSD persists longer than 1 month after the trauma; ASD begins immediately and resolves within 1 month. Both share re-experiencing, avoidance, and arousal symptoms.

Expected findings

Subjective: recurring nightmares, intrusive memories, negative self-image. Objective: difficulty concentrating, exaggerated startle response, avoidance of reminders.

Nursing care & therapeutic procedures

Establish trust and safety; encourage expression at the client's pace. Therapeutic techniques include cognitive-behavioral therapy and exposure-based therapies (e.g., EMDR) alongside prescribed medications.

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 12.1 — PTSD Findings (Select All That Apply)

A nurse working on an acute mental health unit is caring for a client who has posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD). Which of the following findings should the nurse expect? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Difficulty concentrating on tasks
  2. Obsessive need to talk about the traumatic event
  3. Negative self-image
  4. Recurring nightmares
  5. Diminished reflexes
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, C, D

  • A. Correct — Difficulty concentrating is a manifestation of PTSD.
  • B. Incorrect — Clients with PTSD are typically reluctant to talk about the event.
  • C. Correct — A negative self-image is a manifestation of PTSD.
  • D. Correct — Recurring nightmares are a manifestation of PTSD.
  • E. Incorrect — Reflexes are increased (hyperarousal/startle), not diminished.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 12.2 — Nurse Self-Care in Mass Casualty (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is involved in a serious and prolonged mass casualty incident in the emergency department. Which of the following strategies should the nurse use to help prevent developing a trauma-related disorder? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Avoid thinking about the incident when it is over.
  2. Take breaks during the incident for food and water.
  3. Debrief with others following the incident.
  4. Avoid displays of emotion in the days following the incident.
  5. Take advantage of offered counseling.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, C, E

  • A. Incorrect — Thinking and talking about the incident afterward helps processing.
  • B. Correct — Taking breaks for food and water supports resilience during the event.
  • C. Correct — Debriefing with others after the incident is protective.
  • D. Incorrect — Expressing emotions afterward is healthy and protective.
  • E. Correct — Accepting offered counseling helps prevent a trauma-related disorder.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Crisis Intervention

Exercise 12.3 — Acute Stress Disorder

A nurse is collecting an admission history for a client who has acute stress disorder (ASD). Which of the following client behaviors should the nurse expect?

  1. The client remembers many details about the traumatic incident.
  2. The client expresses heightened elation about what is happening.
  3. The client remembers first noticing manifestations of the disorder 6 weeks after the traumatic incident occurred.
  4. The client expresses a sense of unreality about the traumatic incident.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. Clients with ASD are often unable to recall details (dissociative amnesia).
  • B. Elation is not characteristic of ASD.
  • C. ASD manifestations occur immediately (within 1 month), not 6 weeks later.
  • D. Correct — A sense of unreality (dissociation) about the event is characteristic of ASD.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 12.4 — Derealization

A nurse is caring for a client who has derealization disorder. Which of the following findings should the nurse identify as an indication of derealization?

  1. The client describes a feeling of floating above the ground.
  2. The client has suspicions of being targeted in order to be killed and robbed.
  3. The client states that the furniture in the room seems to be small and far away.
  4. The client cannot recall anything that happened during the past 2 weeks.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Feeling detached from/floating above one's body is depersonalization.
  • B. Suspicion of being targeted reflects a delusion, not derealization.
  • C. Correct — Perceiving surroundings as unreal (small/far away) indicates derealization.
  • D. Inability to recall events reflects dissociative amnesia.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Exercise 12.5 — Dissociative Fugue

A nurse in an acute mental health facility is planning care for a client who has dissociative fugue. Which of the following interventions should the nurse add to the plan of care?

  1. Teach the client to recognize how stress brings on a personality change in the client.
  2. Repeatedly present the client with information about past events.
  3. Make decisions for the client regarding routine daily activities.
  4. Work with the client on grounding techniques.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. Recognizing stress-induced personality changes applies to dissociative identity disorder.
  • B. Avoid flooding the client with information about past events.
  • C. Encourage the client to make their own decisions about routine activities.
  • D. Correct — Grounding techniques (e.g., stomping the feet, naming the surroundings) help reconnect the client to the present.

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 13

Depressive Disorders

Major depressive disorder, persistent depressive disorder, and PMDD — risk factors, phases of treatment, and the priority of safety.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Major depressive disorder (MDD): at least five clinical findings (depressed mood, anhedonia, sleep/appetite changes, fatigue, worthlessness, poor concentration) for ≥2 weeks. Safety is the priority — clients are at greatest risk for suicide during the first weeks of an episode and as energy improves before mood lifts; assess risk and use one-to-one observation when indicated. Persistent depressive disorder (dysthymia): a chronic, lower-grade depression lasting ≥2 years with decreased self-esteem. Premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD): findings in the luteal phase (before menses) with emotional lability; exercise helps. Risk factors for depression include female sex, family history, chronic medical illness, recent loss, and a history of anxiety/personality disorders. Treatment phases: acute (most effective for medication + psychotherapy), continuation (relapse prevention), and maintenance (long-term).

Safety first

  • Greatest suicide risk in the early weeks of an episode
  • Risk can rise as energy returns before mood improves
  • Assess risk; use one-to-one observation when indicated

Disorder snapshots

  • MDD — ≥5 findings, ≥2 weeks
  • Persistent depressive — ≥2 years
  • PMDD — luteal phase, emotional lability

Depressive Disorders

Depression is a mood (affective) disorder and a leading cause of disability. A client who has depression carries a potential risk for suicide — heightened by a personal/family history of attempts, comorbid anxiety or substance use, psychosis, poor self-esteem, lack of social support, or chronic medical illness.

Major depressive disorder (MDD)

  • A single or recurrent episode of unipolar depression causing significant change in functioning (social, occupational, self-care)
  • Requires ≥5 findings almost every day for ≥2 weeks, most of the day: depressed mood, sleep change, indecisiveness, decreased concentration, suicidal ideation, change in motor activity, inability to feel pleasure, weight change >5% in a month
  • Specifiers: psychotic features (hallucinations/delusions); postpartum onset (within 4 weeks of childbirth; delusions can place the newborn at risk)

Other depressive disorders

  • Seasonal affective disorder (SAD) — seasonal (usually winter); light therapy is first-line
  • Persistent depressive disorder (formerly dysthymia) — milder, early onset, lasts ≥2 years (1 year for children); can later become MDD
  • Premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD) — tied to the luteal phase; mood swings, irritability, anxiety plus physical findings; treated with exercise, diet, relaxation
  • Substance-induced depressive disorder — associated with use of or withdrawal from drugs/alcohol

Common comorbidities

  • Anxiety disorders — comorbid in ~70%; worsens prognosis and raises suicide/disability risk
  • Psychotic disorders, substance use disorders, eating disorders, personality disorders

Risk Factors

Depression occurs across all groups and can be primary or a response to another disorder.

Risk factors

  • Family and personal history of depression are the most significant risk factors
  • Twice as common in females; very common over age 65 (differentiate from early dementia — memory loss, confusion, agitation can mimic it)
  • Neurotransmitter deficiencies — serotonin (mood, sleep, hunger, pain perception) and norepinephrine (attention, behavior); imbalances of dopamine, acetylcholine, GABA, glutamate
  • Stressful life events, medical illness, postpartum period, comorbid anxiety/personality/substance disorders, early-life trauma

Expected Findings & Screening

Depression presents with low energy, loss of pleasure, and vegetative and physical changes.

Manifestations

Core & vegetative

  • Anergia (lack of energy), anhedonia (lack of pleasure), anxiety
  • Vegetative findings: appetite change (usually anorexia in MDD), constipation, sleep disturbance, decreased sexual interest; somatic reports

Physical assessment

  • Sad look with blunted affect, poor grooming/hygiene
  • Psychomotor retardation (slowed movement, slumped posture) — most common — or agitation
  • Social isolation; slowed speech; delayed responses

Standardized screening tools

  • Hamilton Depression Scale; Beck Depression Inventory
  • Geriatric Depression Scale (short form); Zung Self-Rating Depression Scale
  • Patient Health Questionnaire-9 (PHQ-9)

Phases of Client Care

Care mirrors the phase of the disease the client is experiencing.

Acute, continuation & maintenance

Acute

  • Severe findings; ~6-12 weeks; possible hospitalization; goal is symptom reduction; assess suicide risk and implement safety precautions/1:1 observation

Continuation

  • Increased ability to function; ~4-9 months; goal is relapse prevention through education, medication, psychotherapy

Maintenance

  • Remission; can last years; goal is prevention of future episodes

Nursing Care

Suicide risk assessment and a safe environment are priorities; communication is adapted to the client's slowed processing.

Care priorities

  • Suicide risk: assess regularly and implement appropriate safety precautions
  • Self-care: monitor ADLs and encourage independence as able
  • Communication: make time to be present even if the client doesn't speak; make observations rather than asking direct questions (which can cause anxiety); use simple, concrete sentences; allow time to respond
  • Counseling for problem-solving, coping, changing negative thinking, self-esteem, assertiveness, and community resources

Medications

Antidepressant therapeutic effects take several weeks; teaching applies across all classes.

Antidepressant classes

SSRIs (leading treatment)

  • Citalopram, fluoxetine, sertraline; watch for serotonin syndrome and sexual dysfunction; avoid St. John's wort

SNRIs

  • Venlafaxine, duloxetine; nausea, insomnia, weight gain, sexual dysfunction; caution with hypertension

TCAs

  • Amitriptyline; change positions slowly (orthostatic hypotension); manage anticholinergic effects

MAOIs

  • Phenelzine; avoid tyramine foods (aged cheese, cured meats, some wine) — hypertensive crisis risk; avoid drug interactions

Atypical

  • Bupropion; appetite suppression; avoid if seizure risk

Teaching for all antidepressants

  • Do not discontinue suddenly; therapeutic effects take several weeks
  • Avoid hazardous activities (driving, machinery) due to sedation; avoid alcohol
  • Notify the provider of any thoughts of suicide
  • 30 min of exercise on 3-5 days/week improves findings and helps prevent relapse (adjunct to other therapy)

Therapies & Procedures

Complementary, device-based, and psychotherapeutic options supplement medication.

Complementary & device-based

  • St. John's wort — unregulated plant product for mild depression; potentially fatal serotonin syndrome if combined with SSRIs/antidepressants; photosensitivity
  • Light therapy — first-line for SAD; inhibits nocturnal melatonin; 10,000-lux box ~30 min/day
  • ECT — for treatment-resistant depression; TMS and VNS for treatment-resistant cases

Psychotherapy

  • CBT — identify and change negative behavior and thought patterns
  • IPT — focus on personal relationships contributing to the depression
  • Group and family therapy

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: System Disorder.

Admission assessment — major depressive disorder

System Disorder · MDD

A nurse working in an acute mental health facility is performing an admission assessment for a client who has major depressive disorder (MDD).

Alteration in health

MDD is a mood disorder with persistent depressed mood and loss of interest that impairs daily functioning; safety screening for suicide risk is essential at admission.

Expected findings

Depressed mood, anhedonia, sleep disturbance, appetite/weight change, fatigue, feelings of worthlessness, and difficulty concentrating.

Nursing care — communication technique

Use unhurried, open-ended communication; sit with the client, allow silence, and convey acceptance to build trust and encourage expression of feelings.

A nurse is caring for a client who has major depressive disorder. Which of the following should the nurse identify as a risk factor for depression? (Select all that apply.)

  4. Family history of depression

  2. Assisting the client to perform ADLs

Exercise 13.3 — Premenstrual Dysphoric Disorder

A nurse is teaching a client who has a new diagnosis of premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD). Which of the following statements by the client indicates understanding of the teaching?

  1. "I can expect my problems with PMDD to be worst when I'm menstruating."
  2. "I should avoid exercising when I am feeling depressed."
  3. "I am aware that my PMDD causes me to have rapid mood swings."
  4. "I should increase my caloric intake with a nutritional supplement when my PMDD is active."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. PMDD findings are present during the luteal phase (before menses), not during menstruation.
  • B. Aerobic and other exercise are effective treatments and should not be avoided.
  • C. Correct — Emotional lability (rapid mood swings) is a clinical finding of PMDD.
  • D. PMDD increases the risk for weight gain; increasing caloric intake is not advised.

Exercise 13.4 — Phases of MDD Treatment

A charge nurse is discussing the care of a client who has major depressive disorder (MDD) with a newly licensed nurse. Which of the following statements by the newly licensed nurse indicates an understanding of the teaching?

  1. "Care during the continuation phase focuses on treating continued manifestations of MDD."
  2. "The treatment of MDD during the maintenance phase lasts for 6 to 12 weeks."
  3. "The client is at greatest risk for suicide during the first weeks of an MDD episode."
  4. "Medication and psychotherapy are most effective during the acute phase of MDD."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. The continuation phase focuses on relapse prevention, not treating continued manifestations.
  • B. The maintenance phase is long-term; the 6-to-12-week range describes the acute phase.
  • C. Correct — The client is at greatest risk for suicide during the first weeks of an episode (and as energy improves).
  • D. Medication and psychotherapy are used across all phases, not only the acute phase.

Exercise 13.5 — Persistent Depressive Disorder

A nurse is interviewing a client who has a new diagnosis of persistent depressive disorder. Which of the following findings should the nurse expect?

  3. Presence of manifestations for at least 2 years
  4. Inflated sense of self-esteem

Answer: C

  • B. A minimum of five clinical findings describes MDD.

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 14

Bipolar Disorders

Mania and bipolar spectrum disorders — milieu management, safety, therapeutic limit-setting, and relapse prevention.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Bipolar disorders cycle between mania/hypomania and depression. During a manic episode, manage the milieu with consistent limits, a firm approach, and concise explanations; monitor for escalating behavior (a priority for safety); provide a low-stimulation environment and high-calorie finger foods (the client may not sit to eat). Set boundaries therapeutically — decline gifts matter-of-factly ("I am here to provide care and cannot accept this"). ECT is appropriate for severe mania and acute episodes unresponsive to medication (pharmacotherapy remains first-line). Relapse prevention: teach that sleep disturbance can signal relapse, take medications consistently (not only when symptoms begin), avoid caffeine/stimulants, participate in psychotherapy, and recognize that anhedonia signals a depressive relapse.

Manic milieu management

  • Consistent limits + firm, concise communication
  • Monitor for escalating behavior (safety priority)
  • Low stimulation; high-calorie finger foods

Relapse prevention

  • Sleep changes can indicate relapse
  • Take medications consistently; avoid caffeine
  • Psychotherapy helps; anhedonia = depressive relapse

Behaviors & Types

Bipolar disorders are mood disorders with recurrent episodes of depression and mania, usually emerging in early adulthood. Periods of normal functioning alternate with periods of illness; clients can exhibit psychotic, paranoid, or bizarre behavior during mania.

Mood states

  • Mania — abnormally elevated, expansive, or irritable mood lasting ≥1 week; usually requires hospitalization
  • Hypomania — less severe, lasts ≥4 days with ≥3 manifestations; no hospitalization; can progress to mania
  • Rapid cycling — ≥4 episodes of hypomania/mania within 1 year; higher recurrence and treatment resistance

Types & comorbidities

Types

  • Bipolar I — ≥1 manic episode alternating with major depression
  • Bipolar II — ≥1 hypomanic episode alternating with major depressive episodes
  • Cyclothymic — ≥2 years of hypomanic + minor depressive manifestations not meeting full criteria

Comorbidities

  • Substance use, anxiety disorders, borderline PD, ODD, phobias, SAD, ADHD, migraines, metabolic syndrome

Expected Findings

Findings reflect the current episode — manic or depressive.

Manic vs. depressive characteristics

Manic

  • Labile/euphoric mood, agitation, irritability, restlessness
  • Flight of ideas, grandiosity, impulsivity (spending/giving away money)
  • Distractibility, poor judgment, attention-seeking, decreased sleep, ADL neglect
  • Possible delusions/hallucinations; denial of illness

Depressive

  • Flat/blunted affect, tearfulness, anergia, anhedonia
  • Difficulty concentrating; suicidal ideation
  • Decreased hygiene; appetite/sleep changes; psychomotor retardation or agitation

Standardized screening tool

  • Mood Disorders Questionnaire — places mood on a continuum from hypomania (euphoria) to acute mania (extreme irritability/hyperactivity) to delirious mania (out of touch with reality)

Risk Factors

Genetic and physiological factors interact with environmental stress.

Risk & relapse triggers

  • Genetics — an immediate family member with bipolar disorder
  • Physiological — neurobiologic and neuroendocrine disorders
  • Environmental — increased stress can trigger mania/depression in genetically susceptible clients
  • Relapse triggers: substances (alcohol, cocaine, caffeine), sleep disturbances, and psychological stressors

Phases of Client Care

Care mirrors the phase of the disease, with safety central during acute mania.

Acute, continuation & maintenance

  • Acute (mania): hospitalization may be required; goals are mania reduction and safety; determine risk of harm to self/others; 1:1 supervision as indicated
  • Continuation: ~4-9 months; relapse prevention through education, medication adherence, psychotherapy
  • Maintenance: generally lifelong; prevention of future manic episodes

Nursing Care

During acute mania the focus is safety, reduced stimulation, self-care, and consistent limit-setting.

Therapeutic milieu & safety

  • Provide a safe environment; assess regularly for suicidal thoughts and escalating behavior
  • Decrease stimulation without isolating when possible (noise, TV, other clients can escalate behavior); seclusion only when needed for safety per protocol
  • Follow agency protocols for protection (restraints, seclusion, 1:1) if a threat of self-injury or injury to others exists
  • Implement frequent rest periods; provide physical-activity outlets (avoid long/high-concentration tasks)
  • Protect from poor judgment and impulsive behavior (giving money away, indiscretions)

Self-care & communication

  • Self-care: monitor sleep/fluid/nutrition; provide portable, nutritious food (client may not sit to eat); supervise clothing choices; give step-by-step hygiene reminders
  • Communication: calm, matter-of-fact, concise approach; consistency and limit-setting; avoid power struggles; reinforce nonmanipulative behavior

Medications & Procedures

Mood stabilizers anchor treatment, with antipsychotics and selected antidepressants as adjuncts.

Medication classes & ECT

  • Mood stabilizers: lithium carbonate; anticonvulsants — valproate and carbamazepine treat acute mania, lamotrigine for maintenance
  • First-generation antipsychotics: chlorpromazine, loxapine
  • Second-generation antipsychotics: olanzapine, risperidone; lurasidone and quetiapine approved for bipolar depression
  • Antidepressants: SSRI fluoxetine for a major depressive episode
  • ECT — for extreme mania (especially lithium-resistant), suicidal clients, or rapid cycling

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: System Disorder.

Acute mania

System Disorder · Bipolar

A nurse in an acute mental health facility is caring for a client who is experiencing acute mania.

Alteration in health

Acute mania is a sustained period of abnormally elevated, expansive, or irritable mood with increased energy that impairs function and safety.

Expected findings

Grandiosity, pressured speech, flight of ideas, decreased need for sleep, distractibility, and impulsive/risky behavior.

Nursing care & client outcomes

Nursing actions: set consistent limits; provide a low-stimulation environment and high-calorie finger foods. Outcomes: the client remains free from injury; the client maintains adequate nutrition and rest.

Exercise 14.1 — Manic Episode Interventions (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is planning care for a client who has bipolar disorder and is experiencing a manic episode. Which of the following interventions should the nurse include in the plan of care? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Provide flexible client behavior expectations.
  2. Offer concise explanations.
  3. Establish consistent limits.
  4. Disregard client concerns.

A nurse is teaching a newly licensed nurse about the use of electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) for the treatment of bipolar disorder. Which of the following statements by the newly licensed nurse indicates understanding?

Exercise 14.4 — Therapeutic Boundary Setting

A nurse is caring for a client who has bipolar disorder. The client states, "I am very rich, and I feel I must give my money to you." Which of the following responses should the nurse make?

  1. "Why do you think you feel the need to give money away?"
  2. "I am here to provide care and cannot accept this from you."
  3. "I can request that your case manager discuss appropriate charity options with you."
  4. "You should know that giving away your money is inappropriate."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. A "why" question is nontherapeutic.
  • B. Correct — This response is matter-of-fact and concise and sets an appropriate professional boundary.
  • C. This deflects rather than directly addressing the behavior.
  • D. This is judgmental and nontherapeutic.

Exercise 14.5 — Relapse Prevention (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is discussing relapse prevention with a client who has bipolar disorder. Which of the following information should the nurse include in the teaching? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Use caffeine in moderation to prevent relapse.
  2. Difficulty sleeping can indicate a relapse.
  3. Begin taking your medications as soon as a relapse begins.
  4. Participating in psychotherapy can help prevent a relapse.
  5. Anhedonia is a clinical manifestation of a depressive relapse.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, D, E

  • A. Incorrect — Caffeine is a stimulant that can trigger relapse and should be avoided.
  • B. Correct — Difficulty sleeping can be an early indicator of relapse.
  • C. Incorrect — Medications should be taken consistently as prescribed, not started only when a relapse begins.
  • D. Correct — Participating in psychotherapy can help prevent relapse.
  • E. Correct — Anhedonia (loss of pleasure) is a manifestation of a depressive relapse.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 15

Psychotic Disorders

Schizophrenia spectrum disorders — positive vs. negative symptoms, command-hallucination safety, and therapeutic response to altered perception.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Psychotic disorders involve a loss of contact with reality. Positive symptoms are an excess/distortion of normal function — hallucinations, delusions, alterations in speech (clang/loose associations), and bizarre motor behavior. Negative symptoms are a loss of normal function — flat affect, avolition (lack of motivation), anhedonia, alogia, and social withdrawal. For hallucinations, ask directly about content, assess for command hallucinations (especially to harm self/others), focus on the client's feelings, and do not argue about whether the voices are real. Command hallucinations to harm require one-to-one observation first (safety). Antipsychotics treat positive symptoms; teach adherence at discharge.

Positive vs negative symptoms

  • Positive: hallucinations, delusions, clang associations, bizarre movements
  • Negative: flat affect, avolition, anhedonia, withdrawal

Responding to hallucinations

  • Ask directly; assess for command hallucinations
  • Focus on feelings; don't argue reality
  • Command-to-harm → one-to-one observation first

Types of Disorders

Schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorders affect thinking, behavior, emotions, and the ability to perceive reality. Schizophrenia likely results from genetic, neurobiological, and nongenetic factors (birth injury, viral infection, nutrition); typical onset is the late teens to early 20s, often after a prodromal period.

Psychotic disorders (DSM-5)

  • Schizophrenia — psychotic thinking/behavior for ≥6 months with significant functional impairment
  • Schizotypal personality disorder — personality impairment, less severe than schizophrenia
  • Delusional disorder — delusional thinking ≥1 month without marked functional impairment
  • Brief psychotic disorder — manifestations lasting 1 day to 1 month
  • Schizophreniform — schizophrenia-like, duration 1-6 months
  • Schizoaffective — meets criteria for both schizophrenia and a depressive/bipolar disorder
  • Substance-induced psychotic disorder — psychosis from intoxication/withdrawal, more severe than expected

Symptom Dimensions

Manifestations group into positive, negative, cognitive, and affective dimensions. Negative symptoms are harder to treat than positive ones.

Positive vs. negative

Positive (things added)

  • Hallucinations, delusions, alterations in speech, bizarre behavior — the most easily identified

Negative (things absent)

  • Blunted/flat affect, alogia (poverty of speech), anergia, anhedonia, avolition (lack of motivation)

Cognitive & affective

  • Cognitive: disordered thinking, indecision, poor problem-solving, concentration difficulty, short-term memory deficits, impaired abstract thinking
  • Affective: hopelessness, suicidal ideation, unstable/rapidly changing mood

Alterations in Thought & Speech

Delusions are false fixed beliefs that cannot be corrected by reasoning; speech alterations reflect disordered thinking.

Delusions

  • Ideas of reference — attaching personal significance to trivial events
  • Persecution — feeling singled out for harm; grandeur — believing oneself all-powerful
  • Somatic, jealousy, being controlled by an outside force
  • Thought broadcasting / insertion / withdrawal; religiosity; magical thinking

Speech alterations

  • Associative looseness — inability to hold one thought; can progress to flight of ideas
  • Neologisms — made-up words meaningful only to the client
  • Echolalia — repeating others' words; clang association — meaningless rhyming
  • Word salad — jumbled words with little meaning

Alterations in Perception & Behavior

Hallucinations are sensory perceptions without an external stimulus; behavioral alterations include the catatonic features.

Perception

  • Hallucinations: auditory (incl. command hallucinations directing an action), visual, olfactory, gustatory, tactile
  • Personal boundary difficulties: depersonalization (lost/unreal sense of self), derealization (environment seems changed), illusions (misinterpreting a real experience)

Behavior (catatonic features)

  • Extreme agitation, stereotyped behaviors, automatic obedience, waxy flexibility, stupor, negativism
  • Echopraxia (imitating movements), catatonia (marked decrease/increase in movement, muscular rigidity), motor retardation, posturing, boundary impairment

Nursing Care

Milieu therapy and a trusting relationship anchor care; communication around hallucinations and delusions follows specific principles, always prioritizing safety.

Communication for hallucinations & delusions

  • Ask directly about hallucinations; do not argue with or agree with the client's view — comment on the feeling ("I don't hear anything, but you seem frightened")
  • Don't argue with delusions; focus on the client's feelings; assess paranoid delusions (increased risk for violence toward others)
  • For command hallucinations, provide for safety due to increased risk of harm to self or others
  • Focus conversations on reality-based subjects; identify triggers; be genuine and empathetic

Milieu, self-care & screening

  • Milieu therapy in acute and community settings; provide a structured, safe environment; PACT (assertive community treatment) for community-living needs
  • Promote self-care; teach manifestation management (music to distract from voices, activities, walking, talking to a trusted person, telling a hallucination to stop)
  • Incorporate family; encourage social skills, group work, and medication adherence
  • Screening tools: AIMS (tardive dyskinesia) and WHODAS (global functioning)

Medications

Antipsychotics are the mainstay, with adjuncts for depression and anxiety.

Antipsychotic generations

First-generation (conventional)

  • Haloperidol, loxapine, chlorpromazine, fluphenazine; mainly positive symptoms; monitor EPS (dystonia, akathisia, pseudoparkinsonism, tardive dyskinesia)

Second-generation (atypical)

  • Risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, ziprasidone, clozapine; meds of choice; positive + negative; weight gain; clozapine requires blood tests for agranulocytosis

Third-generation

  • Aripiprazole; positive + negative + cognitive; lower EPS/TD and weight-gain risk

Adjunct medications

  • Antidepressants (paroxetine) — temporary, for depression; monitor for suicidal ideation, especially early; avoid abrupt cessation
  • Mood stabilizers / benzodiazepines (valproate, lamotrigine, lorazepam) — for anxiety and some positive/negative symptoms; use cautiously in older adults

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Risperidone at discharge

Medication · Antipsychotic

A nurse is caring for a client who has schizophrenia and is reviewing discharge instructions that include a new prescription for risperidone.

Therapeutic uses

Risperidone is an atypical (second-generation) antipsychotic used to treat the positive and negative symptoms of schizophrenia.

Client education

  • Take the medication consistently as prescribed; do not stop abruptly.
  • Report extrapyramidal symptoms (tremor, rigidity, restlessness) and signs of neuroleptic malignant syndrome (high fever, muscle rigidity).
  • Change positions slowly (orthostatic hypotension) and monitor weight/glucose (metabolic effects).

Exercise 15.1 — Responding to Auditory Hallucinations (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is caring for a client who has substance-induced psychotic disorder and is experiencing auditory hallucinations. The client states, "The voices won't leave me alone!" Which of the following statements should the nurse make? (Select all that apply.)

  1. "When did you start hearing these things?"
  2. "The voices are not real, or else we would both hear them."
  3. "It must be scary to hear voices."
  4. "Are the voices you hear telling you to hurt yourself?"
  5. "Why are the voices talking to only you?"
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, C, D

  • A. Correct — Asking directly about the hallucination is appropriate assessment.
  • B. Incorrect — Do not argue with the client's view of the situation.
  • C. Correct — Focusing on the client's feelings is therapeutic.
  • D. Correct — Assess for command hallucinations and risk for self-harm.
  • E. Incorrect — Avoid "why" questions, which are nontherapeutic.
