Exercise 15.2 — Positive Symptoms (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is completing an admission assessment for a client who has schizophrenia. Which of the following findings should the nurse document as positive symptoms? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Auditory hallucination
  2. Lack of motivation
  3. Use of clang associations
  4. Delusion of persecution
  5. Constantly waving arms
  6. Flat affect
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, C, D, E

  • A. Correct — Hallucinations are a positive symptom.
  • B. Incorrect — Lack of motivation (avolition) is a negative symptom.
  • C. Correct — Alterations in speech (clang associations) are a positive symptom.
  • D. Correct — Delusions are a positive symptom.
  • E. Correct — Bizarre motor movements are a positive symptom.
  • F. Incorrect — Flat affect is a negative symptom.

Exercise 15.3 — Depersonalization

A nurse is caring for a client who has schizoaffective disorder. Which of the following statements indicates the client is experiencing depersonalization?

  1. "I am a superhero and am immortal."
  2. "I am no one, and everyone is me."
  3. "I feel monsters pinching me all over."
  4. "I know that you are stealing my thoughts."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Reflects a grandiose delusion.
  • B. Correct — A nonexistent or merged sense of self reflects depersonalization (a disturbed sense of one's own reality).
  • C. Reflects a tactile hallucination.
  • D. Reflects a delusion (thought withdrawal).

Exercise 15.4 — Command Hallucination (Priority)

A nurse is caring for a client on an acute mental health unit. The client reports hearing voices that are stating, "kill your doctor." Which of the following actions should the nurse take first?

  1. Encourage the client to participate in group therapy on the unit.
  2. Initiate one-to-one observation of the client.
  3. Focus the client on reality.
  4. Notify the provider of the client's statement.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A, C, D. Group therapy, reorienting to reality, and notifying the provider are appropriate but follow ensuring immediate safety.
  • B. Correct — A command hallucination to harm others requires one-to-one observation first to maintain safety.

Exercise 15.5 — Client Appears to Hallucinate During Interview

A nurse is speaking with a client who has schizophrenia when the client suddenly seems to stop focusing on the nurse's questions and begins looking at the ceiling and talking to themselves. Which of the following actions should the nurse take?

  1. Stop the interview at this point, and resume later when the client is better able to concentrate.
  2. Ask the client, "Are you seeing something on the ceiling?"
  3. Tell the client, "You seem to be looking at something on the ceiling. I see something there, too."
  4. Continue the interview without comment on the client's behavior.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A, D. Stopping or ignoring the behavior fails to address the client's current experience.
  • B. Correct — Ask directly about the hallucination to assess what the client is experiencing.
  • C. Incorrect — Avoid agreeing with or validating the hallucination.

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 16

Personality Disorders

Cluster A, B, and C personality disorders — defense mechanisms, limit-setting, and the nurse's self-assessment.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Personality disorders are enduring, inflexible patterns that impair function. Common to all personality disorders: difficulty in social/professional relationships, maladaptive responses to stress (defense mechanisms when routines change), and difficulty understanding professional boundaries. Cluster A (odd/eccentric): paranoid, schizoid (avoid forcing socialization), schizotypal (magical thinking). Cluster B (dramatic/erratic): antisocial (exploits/manipulates others, blames others, fails to accept responsibility — use limit-setting), borderline (uses splitting — all-good/all-bad; self-injury risk), histrionic, narcissistic (needs admiration). Cluster C (anxious/fearful): avoidant (fears rejection/abandonment), dependent, obsessive-compulsive (perfectionism/orderliness). Use assertiveness training for dependent/avoidant clients and consistent limit-setting for manipulation.

Defense mechanism — splitting

  • Hallmark of borderline personality disorder
  • Sees people as all good or all bad
  • Staff should communicate consistently to avoid being split

Antisocial PD findings

  • Exploitation/manipulation of others
  • Failure to accept personal responsibility; blames others
  • Limit-setting is the core nursing approach

Overview & Defense Mechanisms

A personality disorder involves pathological personality traits — impairments in self-identity/self-direction and interpersonal functioning. The maladaptive behaviors are not always perceived by the client as dysfunctional, and some areas of functioning can be adequate. These disorders often co-occur with depression, anxiety, eating, and substance use disorders.

Defense mechanisms & characteristics

  • Common defenses: repression, suppression, regression, undoing, and splitting
  • Splitting — inability to integrate positive and negative aspects of self/others into a whole image; characterizes people as all-good or all-bad; commonly seen in borderline personality disorder
  • Pathological characteristics: inflexibility/maladaptive stress responses, compulsiveness, inability to emotionally connect, tendency to provoke interpersonal conflict

Risk factors

  • Often comorbid substance use disorders; possible history of crime
  • Psychosocial influences (childhood abuse/trauma, parenting); developmental factors
  • Biological influences — genetic and biochemical factors

The 10 Personality Disorders

The DSM-5 groups the ten personality disorders into three clusters by predominant trait.

Clusters A, B & C

Cluster A (odd/eccentric)

  • Paranoid — distrust/suspiciousness
  • Schizoid — emotional detachment, disinterest in relationships
  • Schizotypal — odd beliefs, magical thinking, eccentric appearance

Cluster B (dramatic/erratic)

  • Antisocial — disregard for others, exploitation, unlawful acts, no remorse
  • Borderline — instability of affect/identity/relationships, splitting, impulsivity, fear of abandonment; often self-injurious/suicidal
  • Histrionic — attention-seeking, seductive
  • Narcissistic — grandiosity, need for admiration, lack of empathy

Cluster C (anxious/fearful)

  • Avoidant — social inhibition, fear of rejection, feelings of inadequacy
  • Dependent — extreme dependency, urgent search for a replacement relationship
  • Obsessive-compulsive — perfectionism, orderliness, and control to the point of impaired function

Nursing Care & Communication

Self-assessment is vital — these clients can evoke intense emotions in the nurse; safety is always a priority given the risk for self-injury or violence.

Communication strategies

  • Use a firm yet supportive approach with consistent care; offer realistic choices to enhance the client's sense of control
  • Limit-setting and consistency are essential with manipulative clients (borderline, antisocial)
  • Dependent/histrionic clients benefit from assertiveness training and modeling; maintain professional boundaries with flirtatious (histrionic) clients
  • Respect the need for isolation in schizoid/schizotypal clients; self-assess for countertransference

Treatment

Psychotherapy is central; medications provide symptom relief.

Medications & therapies

  • Medications — psychotropics for symptom relief: antidepressant, anxiolytic, antipsychotic, or mood stabilizer
  • Psychotherapy, group therapy, and CBT are effective
  • Dialectical behavior therapy (DBT) — for self-injurious behavior; gradual change with acceptance/validation
  • Case management — for persistently/severely impaired clients (history, family reintegration, referrals, crisis resources)

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Nurse self-assessment

Basic Concept · Personality disorders

A charge nurse is discussing self-assessment with a newly licensed nurse who is caring for clients who have personality disorders.

Related content

Self-assessment is vital for nurses caring for clients with personality disorders because these clients can provoke strong emotional reactions (frustration, anger, being manipulated or split).

Underlying principles

  • Recognizing one's own feelings prevents countertransference and nontherapeutic responses.
  • Consistent, nonjudgmental responses support effective limit-setting and team cohesion.

Nursing interventions — who and when

All members of the care team should perform ongoing self-assessment, particularly when feeling manipulated, angry, or pulled into taking sides.

Exercise 16.1 — Caring for Personality Disorders

A nurse manager is discussing the care of a client who has a personality disorder with a newly licensed nurse. Which of the following statements by the newly licensed nurse indicates an understanding of the teaching?

  1. "I can promote my client's sense of control by establishing a schedule."
  2. "I should encourage clients who have a schizoid personality disorder to increase socialization."
  3. "I should practice limit-setting to help prevent client manipulation."
  4. "I should implement assertiveness training with clients who have antisocial personality disorder."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Establishing a schedule is not the central strategy for personality disorders.
  • B. Avoid forcing increased socialization on a client with schizoid personality disorder.
  • C. Correct — Consistent limit-setting helps prevent manipulation.
  • D. Assertiveness training is used for dependent/avoidant clients, not antisocial.

Exercise 16.2 — Avoidant Personality Disorder

A nurse is caring for a client who has avoidant personality disorder. Which of the following statements is expected from a client who has this type of personality disorder?

  1. "I'm scared that you're going to leave me."
  2. "I'll go to group therapy if you'll let me smoke."
  3. "I need to feel that everyone admires me."
  4. "I sometimes feel better if I cut myself."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Clients with avoidant personality disorder fear rejection and abandonment due to feelings of inadequacy.
  • B. Bargaining reflects manipulation (antisocial/borderline).
  • C. A need for admiration reflects narcissistic personality disorder.
  • D. Self-injury reflects borderline personality disorder.

Exercise 16.3 — Splitting

A nurse is caring for a client who has borderline personality disorder. The client says, "The nurse on the evening shift is always nice! You are the meanest nurse ever!" The nurse should recognize the client's statement as an example of which of the following defense mechanisms?

  1. Regression
  2. Splitting
  3. Undoing
  4. Identification
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Regression is reverting to an earlier developmental behavior.
  • B. Correct — Splitting is the inability to see people as having both good and bad qualities (all-good vs. all-bad).
  • C. Undoing attempts to reverse or "cancel out" an act.
  • D. Identification imitates the behavior of another.

Exercise 16.4 — Antisocial Personality Disorder (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is assisting with a court-ordered evaluation of a client who has antisocial personality disorder. Which of the following findings should the nurse expect? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Demonstrates extreme anxiety when placed in a social situation
  2. Often engages in magical thinking
  3. Attempts to convince other clients to relinquish their belongings
  4. Becomes agitated if personal area is not neat and orderly
  5. Blames others for personal past and current problems
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C, E

  • A. Incorrect — Extreme social anxiety reflects avoidant personality disorder.
  • B. Incorrect — Magical thinking/odd beliefs reflect schizotypal personality disorder.
  • C. Correct — Exploitation and manipulation of others is characteristic of antisocial personality disorder.
  • D. Incorrect — Perfectionism/orderliness reflects obsessive-compulsive personality disorder.
  • E. Correct — Failure to accept personal responsibility (blaming others) is characteristic.

Exercise 16.5 — Characteristics Common to All Personality Disorders (Select All That Apply)

A charge nurse is preparing a staff education session on personality disorders. Which of the following personality characteristics associated with all of the personality disorders should the charge nurse include in the teaching? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Difficulty in getting along with other members of a group
  2. Belief in the ability to become invisible during times of stress
  3. Display of defense mechanisms when routines are changed
  4. Claiming to be more important than other persons
  5. Difficulty understanding why it is inappropriate to have a personal relationship with staff
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, C, E

  • A. Correct — Difficulty with social and professional relationships is common to all personality disorders.
  • B. Incorrect — Magical thinking (becoming invisible) is specific to schizotypal personality disorder.
  • C. Correct — A maladaptive response to stress (defense mechanisms when routines change) is common to all.
  • D. Incorrect — Claiming to be more important reflects narcissistic personality disorder specifically.
  • E. Correct — Difficulty understanding professional boundaries is common to all.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 17

Neurocognitive Disorders

Delirium vs. dementia (Alzheimer's), donepezil teaching, home safety, and caregiver support.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Delirium is acute, fluctuating, and reversible — often from infection (e.g., UTI), with altered level of consciousness, hallucinations, personality changes, and restlessness. Dementia (Alzheimer's) is gradual, progressive, and irreversible with gradual memory loss. For donepezil (a cholinesterase inhibitor): take it at the end of the day/bedtime, avoid NSAIDs (GI bleeding risk), and continue despite common GI adverse effects. Communication with cognitive decline: introduce yourself, use positive statements, and redirect ("Let's walk together to your room") rather than confront or ask "why." Home safety: locks high on exit doors, mattress on the floor, lighting over stairs; remove throw rugs and lock cleaning supplies. Support caregivers with respite-care resources.

Delirium vs dementia

  • Delirium: acute, fluctuating, altered LOC, reversible
  • Dementia: gradual, progressive, irreversible memory loss

Home safety for Alzheimer's

  • Locks high on exit doors; mattress on the floor
  • Lighting over stairs; remove throw rugs
  • Lock cleaning supplies; offer respite-care resources

Types of Cognitive Disorders

Neurocognitive disorders (NCDs) disrupt thinking, memory, processing, and problem-solving. Care requires a compassionate understanding of both client and family.

DSM-5 categories

  • Delirium — tends to be short-term and reversible
  • Mild NCD — may or may not progress to a major disorder
  • Major NCD (dementia) — progressive and irreversible
  • Subtypes: NCD due to Alzheimer's disease (most common, neurodegenerative), Parkinson's, or Huntington's; depression in the older adult can mimic early Alzheimer's

Delirium vs. Neurocognitive Disorder

Distinguishing the two is essential — delirium is a reversible medical emergency, while major NCD is progressive.

Key differences

Delirium

  • Rapid onset (hours to days); fluctuates through the day; worse at night
  • Altered level of consciousness; a medical emergency
  • Four types: hyperactive, hypoactive, mixed, unclassified
  • Reversible with prompt treatment of the underlying cause

Neurocognitive disorder

  • Gradual deterioration over months/years; does not change through the day
  • Impairments in memory, judgment, aphasia, agnosia, apraxia, executive function
  • Stable LOC; sundowning; gradual personality change
  • Irreversible and progressive

Defense mechanisms

  • Denial — client/family refuse to believe changes are occurring
  • Confabulation — making up stories to fill memory gaps (unconscious attempt to save self-esteem, not lying)
  • Perseveration — avoiding questions by repeating phrases/behavior

Assessment & Screening

There is no single diagnostic test; testing rules out other treatable causes, and screening tools gauge cognition and function.

Risk factors

  • Delirium: neurologic, metabolic (hepatic/renal failure, electrolyte imbalance), cardiovascular/respiratory disease, infection, surgery, substance use/withdrawal, older age, polypharmacy, ICU stay, restraint use
  • NCD/Alzheimer's: advanced age, prior head trauma, cardiovascular disease, lifestyle, family history (strong genetic link in early-onset familial AD)

Diagnostics & screening tools

  • No specific test — definitive diagnosis only at autopsy; testing rules out other pathologies (neuroimaging, EEG, labs, B12/folate, thyroid, vision/hearing)
  • Delirium tools: Confusion Assessment Method (CAM), NEECHAM Confusion Scale
  • Dementia tools: MMSE, Functional Dementia Scale, BIMS, FAST, Global Deterioration Scale, Blessed Dementia Scale

Nursing Care

Care protects the client from injury while preserving dignity, with communication and cognitive support adapted to declining function.

Safe environment & physical needs

  • Minimize risk factors and promote early detection; perform self-assessment of personal reactions
  • Assess for falls/wandering; room near the nurses' station; low visual/auditory stimuli; well-lit, minimizing shadows
  • Monitor neurologic status, skin integrity, vital signs, comfort; promote sleep; provide glasses/hearing aids; ensure food/fluid intake

Communication & cognitive support

  • Calm, reassuring tone; positively worded phrases; do not argue with hallucinations/delusions; reinforce reality and orientation
  • Introduce self each contact; eye contact; short, simple sentences; one item at a time; encourage reminiscence; avoid confrontation; approach slowly from the front
  • Cognitive support: memory aids (clocks, photographs, familiar objects), consistent routine and caregivers, cover/remove mirrors, encourage daytime physical activity

Medications

Two drug classes target Alzheimer's; others manage associated symptoms.

Alzheimer's medications

Cholinesterase inhibitors

  • Donepezil, rivastigmine, galantamine — increase acetylcholine; help mild-to-moderate AD
  • GI effects, bradycardia/syncope; caution with asthma/COPD; NSAIDs raise GI bleeding risk; start low, taper to discontinue

Memantine

  • Blocks calcium entry to slow brain-cell death; for moderate-to-severe AD
  • Can be used with a cholinesterase inhibitor; watch dizziness, headache, confusion, constipation

Other medications & caution

  • SSRIs for depression; antianxiety agents for agitation
  • Antipsychotics reserved as a last resort for hallucinations/delusions (many adverse effects)
  • Use caution with PRN medications for agitation/anxiety; medications themselves can cause delirium

Home Safety & Caregiver Support

Discharge planning protects the client at home and sustains the caregiver.

Home safety & caregiver support

Home safety measures

  • Remove scatter rugs and clutter; install door locks that aren't easily opened
  • Lower water-heater temperature; good lighting and stair handrails; secure cords; lock cleaning supplies

Caregiver support

  • Seek legal counsel (advance directives, durable power of attorney)
  • Review home-care/community/long-term-care resources; encourage respite and support groups; "take one day at a time"

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: System Disorder.

Safe environment in severe Alzheimer's

System Disorder · Neurocognitive

A nurse is planning care to promote a safe and therapeutic environment for a client who has severe cognitive decline due to Alzheimer's disease.

Alteration in health

Alzheimer's disease is a neurodegenerative subtype of major neurocognitive disorder marked by progressive, irreversible cognitive and functional decline.

Nursing care — five actions

  • Provide a structured, consistent routine and a calm, low-stimulation environment.
  • Use simple, clear communication and reorient/redirect as needed.
  • Ensure safety: prevent wandering and falls (door locks high, adequate lighting).
  • Assist with ADLs and maintain nutrition/hydration (finger foods if needed).
  • Support and educate caregivers, including respite resources.

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 17.1 — Donepezil Teaching

A nurse is caring for a client who has early stage Alzheimer's disease and a new prescription for donepezil. The nurse should include which of the following statements when teaching the client about the medication?

  1. "You should avoid taking over-the-counter acetaminophen while on donepezil."
  2. "You should take this medication before going to bed at the end of the day."
  3. "You will be screened for underlying kidney disease prior to starting donepezil."
  4. "You should stop taking donepezil if you experience nausea or diarrhea."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Clients should avoid NSAIDs (GI bleeding risk), not acetaminophen.
  • B. Correct — Take donepezil at the end of the day/at bedtime.
  • C. Routine kidney-disease screening is not required before starting donepezil.
  • D. GI adverse effects are common; the client should not stop the medication without consulting the provider.

Exercise 17.2 — Redirecting a Wandering Client

A nurse in a long-term care facility is caring for a client who has major neurocognitive disorder and attempts to wander out of the building. The client states, "I have to get home." Which of the following statements should the nurse make?

  1. "You have forgotten that this is your home."
  2. "You cannot go outside without a staff member."
  3. "Why would you want to leave? Aren't you happy with your care?"
  4. "I am your nurse. Let's walk together to your room."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. Avoid statements that emphasize the client's deficits.
  • B. Use positive rather than negative statements.
  • C. A "why" question can promote defensiveness.
  • D. Correct — Introduce oneself and redirect the client calmly to a safe activity.

Exercise 17.3 — Home Safety (Select All That Apply)

A home health nurse is making a visit to a client who has Alzheimer's disease to assess the home for safety. Which of the following suggestions should the nurse make to decrease the client's risk for injury? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Install extra locks at the top of exit doors.
  2. Place rugs over electrical cords.
  3. Put cleaning supplies on the top of a shelf.
  4. Place the client's mattress on the floor.
  5. Install light fixtures above stairs.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, D, E

  • A. Correct — Locks placed high on exit doors reduce wandering/elopement.
  • B. Incorrect — Rugs create a fall hazard and should be removed.
  • C. Incorrect — Cleaning supplies should be in locked cupboards, not just out of reach.
  • D. Correct — Placing the mattress on the floor reduces injury from falls out of bed.
  • E. Correct — Adequate lighting over stairs reduces fall risk.

Exercise 17.4 — Caregiver Support

A nurse is making a home visit to a client who is in the late stage of Alzheimer's disease. The client's partner, who is the primary caregiver, wishes to discuss concerns about the client's nutrition and the stress of providing care. Which of the following actions should the nurse take?

  1. Verify that a current power of attorney document is on file.
  2. Instruct the client's partner to offer finger foods to increase oral intake.
  3. Provide information on resources for respite care.
  4. Schedule the client for placement of an enteral feeding tube.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. A power of attorney document does not address the nutrition or caregiver-stress concerns raised.
  • B. Finger foods help nutrition but do not address the partner's caregiver stress.
  • C. Correct — Providing respite-care resources addresses both the caregiver's stress and supports ongoing care.
  • D. An enteral feeding tube is appropriate only in specific circumstances and is premature here.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Support Systems

Exercise 17.5 — Delirium Findings (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is performing an admission assessment for a client who has delirium related to an acute urinary tract infection. Which of the following findings should the nurse expect? (Select all that apply.)

  1. History of gradual memory loss
  2. Family report of personality changes
  3. Hallucinations
  4. Unaltered level of consciousness
  5. Restlessness
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, C, E

  • A. Incorrect — Gradual memory loss is characteristic of dementia, not delirium.
  • B. Correct — Personality changes can occur with delirium.
  • C. Correct — Hallucinations can occur with delirium.
  • D. Incorrect — Delirium causes an altered (fluctuating) level of consciousness.
  • E. Correct — Restlessness is a common finding in delirium.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 18

Substance Use and Addictive Disorders

Assessment of intoxication and withdrawal, safety priorities, maintenance of abstinence, and family-centered recovery support.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Substance use disorders are treated with a focus on safety, assessment, and recovery. Alcohol withdrawal findings include fine hand tremors, vomiting, restlessness, tachycardia, and hypertension; benzodiazepines (e.g., chlordiazepoxide) manage acute withdrawal. Benzodiazepine withdrawal can cause life-threatening seizures — seizure precautions are the priority. After withdrawal resolves, disulfiram supports alcohol abstinence (aversion). Older adults are at increased risk following retirement and other life-change stressors and need lower (not higher) doses. Recovery is family-centered: the primary goal is abstinence; eliminating codependent behaviors and acknowledging feelings support recovery; Al-Anon supports the family (the client attends a recovery group such as AA).

Withdrawal safety priorities

  • Alcohol: tremors, vomiting, restlessness, ↑HR/↑BP
  • Benzodiazepine: seizure precautions are the priority
  • Disulfiram supports abstinence after withdrawal resolves

Family-centered recovery

  • Primary goal: abstinence
  • Eliminate codependent behaviors; acknowledge feelings
  • Al-Anon supports the family; AA supports the client

Overview & Risk Factors

A substance use disorder involves repeated use of chemical substances leading to clinically significant impairment over a 12-month period. Disorders are marked by loss of control, continued use despite associated problems, and a tendency to relapse. Denial is the defense mechanism most commonly used and often prevents clients from getting help.

Scope & process addictions

  • Substances: alcohol, caffeine, cannabis, hallucinogens, inhalants, opioids, sedatives/hypnotics/anxiolytics, stimulants, tobacco
  • Non-substance (process) addictions: gambling, sexual activity, shopping, social media, internet gaming

Risk factors & populations

  • Genetics (family history), chronic stress, history of trauma (abuse, combat), low self-esteem, low frustration/pain tolerance, poor relationships, risk-taking
  • Sociocultural — some cultures have high rates of alcohol use disorder, others low; peer pressure increases likelihood
  • Use is highest in ages 18-25; earlier onset raises risk; older adults are prone to falls, memory loss, and effects at lower doses (polypharmacy interactions)

Screening Tools

Standardized tools screen for use and gauge withdrawal severity.

Standardized screening tools

  • MAST (Michigan Alcohol Screening Test); CAGE Questionnaire; AUDIT (Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test)
  • DAST / DAST-A (Drug Abuse Screening Test, adolescent version)
  • CIWA-Ar (alcohol withdrawal) and the Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale

CNS Depressants

CNS depressants can cause physiological and psychological dependence, with cross-tolerance and additive effects. Alcohol withdrawal in particular can become a medical emergency.

Alcohol (ethanol)

  • Intended effect: relaxation, decreased social anxiety
  • Intoxication: slurred speech, nystagmus, memory impairment, decreased motor skills/LOC; large doses can cause respiratory arrest and death; chronic use causes liver damage (cirrhosis), gastritis, pancreatitis
  • Withdrawal: tremors, restlessness, increased vital signs, transient hallucinations, and possible tonic-clonic seizures
  • Alcohol withdrawal delirium can occur 2-3 days after cessation — a medical emergency with severe disorientation, hallucinations, and cardiac instability that can progress to death

Sedatives / hypnotics / anxiolytics

  • Benzodiazepines, barbiturates, and club drugs; intended effect is decreased anxiety/sedation
  • Intoxication: drowsiness, slurred speech, respiratory depression, and decreased LOC (can be fatal)
  • Antidote: flumazenil for benzodiazepine toxicity; no antidote for barbiturate toxicity
  • Withdrawal: anxiety, insomnia, hypertension, tremors, possible seizures and psychotic reactions

Opioids & CNS Stimulants

Opioid toxicity is reversible with naloxone; stimulant toxicity can be life-threatening, while stimulant withdrawal carries a suicide risk.

Opioids

  • Intended effect: euphoria, pain relief
  • Intoxication: slurred speech, decreased respirations and LOC (can cause death), impaired judgment
  • Antidote: naloxone (IV) to relieve toxicity
  • Withdrawal: abstinence syndrome (sweating, rhinorrhea, piloerection, tremors, diarrhea, pain) — very unpleasant but not life-threatening

CNS stimulants

Cocaine & amphetamines

  • Euphoria, energy; severe intoxication causes seizures, fever, and possible cardiovascular collapse/death
  • Withdrawal: depression, fatigue, craving — not life-threatening but possible suicidal ideation

Caffeine

  • Increased alertness; intoxication causes tachycardia, anxiety, insomnia
  • Withdrawal (within ~24 hr): headache, nausea, irritability, drowsiness

Cannabis, Inhalants, Hallucinogens & Tobacco

These substances vary widely in their withdrawal profiles — some have none, while others produce lasting perceptual effects.

Cannabis & inhalants

  • Cannabis — euphoria/sedation; high doses cause paranoia (delusions/hallucinations); impairs motor skills for hours; withdrawal includes irritability, anxiety, insomnia
  • Inhalants (often used by children/adolescents) — euphoria; intoxication causes dizziness, uncoordinated movement, respiratory depression, possible death; no withdrawal manifestations

Hallucinogens & tobacco

  • Hallucinogens (LSD, mescaline, PCP) — altered perceptions; intoxication causes anxiety, paranoia, tachycardia; hallucinogen persisting perception disorder (flashbacks) can recur for years
  • Tobacco (nicotine) — relaxation; long-term cardiovascular/respiratory disease and cancer; withdrawal abstinence syndrome with irritability, craving, anxiety, increased appetite

Nursing Care

A nonjudgmental approach is imperative; safety is the primary focus during acute intoxication or withdrawal.

Care during intoxication/withdrawal

  • Self-assess personal feelings — they can be transferred through body language and word choice; use an objective, nonjudgmental approach
  • Maintain a safe, low-stimulation environment; implement seizure precautions; provide close observation (possibly 1:1); restraint is a last resort
  • Orient the client; maintain nutrition/fluid balance; monitor for covert substance use during detox
  • Educate about codependency and abstinence as the initial goal; help develop an emergency plan; encourage self-help groups

Medications & Interprofessional Care

Pharmacologic options support withdrawal and abstinence; dual diagnosis requires treating both disorders together.

Medications

  • Alcohol withdrawal: diazepam, chlordiazepoxide, carbamazepine, clonidine, phenobarbital
  • Alcohol abstinence: disulfiram (avoid all alcohol), naltrexone, acamprosate
  • Opioid withdrawal: methadone substitution, buprenorphine, clonidine, naltrexone
  • Nicotine: bupropion, nicotine replacement therapy, varenicline

Interprofessional care

  • Dual diagnosis (a mental health disorder plus a substance use disorder) — both must be treated simultaneously with a team approach
  • Psychotherapies: CBT, acceptance and commitment therapy (ACT), and relapse prevention therapy
  • Group therapy with clients who have similar disorders

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: System Disorder.

Severe stimulant intoxication

System Disorder · Substance use

A nurse is caring for a client who has a stimulant (cocaine) use disorder and is experiencing severe effects of intoxication.

Alteration in health & expected findings

Severe stimulant intoxication can produce tachycardia, hypertension, hyperthermia, agitation, and risk of seizures or cardiac events.

Nursing care

  • Prioritize safety: monitor vital signs, provide a calm low-stimulation environment, and institute seizure/cardiac precautions.
  • Reorient the client and protect from injury.

Interprofessional care & client outcomes

Nonpharmacological therapy: individual/group counseling and 12-step recovery programs. Outcomes: the client remains free from injury; the client engages in a recovery plan.

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key.

Exercise 18.1 — Substance Use in Older Adults

A nurse is planning a staff education program on substance use in older adults. Which of the following information should the nurse include in the presentation?

  1. Older adults require higher doses of a substance to achieve a desired effect.
  2. Older adults commonly use rationalization to cope with a substance use disorder.
  3. Older adults are at an increased risk for substance use following retirement.
  4. Older adults develop substance use to mask manifestations of dementia.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Older adults generally require lower (not higher) doses to achieve an effect.
  • B. Denial, rather than rationalization, is the commonly described defense mechanism.
  • C. Correct — Retirement and other life-change stressors increase the risk for substance use in older adults.
  • D. Substance use can mimic or worsen cognitive changes but is not developed to "mask" dementia.

Exercise 18.5 — Family Teaching (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is providing teaching to the family of a client who has a substance use disorder. Which of the following statements by a family member indicates an understanding of the teaching? (Select all that apply.)

  1. "We need to understand that our sibling is responsible for their disorder."
  2. "Eliminating codependent behavior will promote recovery."
  3. "Our sibling should participate in an Al-Anon group to assist with recovery."

  5. "Our sibling needs to discuss personal feelings about substance use to help with recovery."

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 19

Eating Disorders

Anorexia nervosa, bulimia nervosa, and binge-eating disorder — assessment, cognitive distortions, medical complications, and the therapeutic relationship.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Eating disorders are serious psychiatric conditions with significant medical risk; nursing care centers on safety, assessment, structure, and a therapeutic relationship rather than on the food itself. Anorexia nervosa features restriction, distorted body image, and intense fear of weight gain; findings can include amenorrhea, lanugo, and skin changes. Bulimia nervosa features binge-and-purge cycles; clients often maintain a near-normal body weight, and purging can cause hypokalemia and other electrolyte disturbances. Watch for cognitive distortions (catastrophizing, overgeneralization, distorted body image). Use a highly structured milieu, a positive (non-punitive) approach, and close observation around meals; acknowledge the client's feelings and redirect toward strengths. Assessment focuses on family/interpersonal relationships, eating patterns, and feelings about appearance — avoid "why" questions and statements that reinforce distortions.

Anorexia vs bulimia

  • Anorexia: restriction; amenorrhea, lanugo, skin changes
  • Bulimia: binge-purge; near-normal weight; hypokalemia from purging

Therapeutic approach

  • Structured milieu + positive, non-punitive approach
  • Close observation around meals
  • Acknowledge feelings; redirect to strengths

Eating Disorders

Eating disorders carry a high mortality rate, and suicide is also a risk. Treatment focuses on normalizing eating patterns and addressing the underlying issues. Comorbidities include depression, personality disorders, substance use disorder, and anxiety.

Anorexia nervosa

  • Persistent restriction of energy intake leading to significantly low body weight for age/sex/health, with intense fear of gaining weight and a disturbance in self-perceived weight or shape
  • Preoccupation with food and eating rituals alongside voluntary refusal to eat; most often female, adolescence to young adulthood; onset can follow a stressful life event
  • Types: restricting type (restricts intake, no binge/purge) and binge-eating/purging type (higher impulsivity)

Bulimia & binge eating disorder

Bulimia nervosa

  • Recurrent binge eating with a sense of lack of control, followed by inappropriate compensatory behaviors (purging type) or other means such as excessive exercise (nonpurging type)
  • Weight is usually within or slightly above the normal range

Binge eating disorder

  • Recurrent binge eating without compensatory behaviors, with distress afterward
  • Affects all ages; associated weight gain raises risk for type 2 diabetes, hypertension, and cancer

Risk Factors

Risk reflects a mix of biological, psychological, social, and environmental influences.

Risk factors

  • Occupations/activities that emphasize thinness (modeling, elite athletics, weight-class sports); history of obesity or childhood "picky" eating
  • Family genetics (family history of eating disorders); biological factors (serotonin pathway disturbances)
  • Interpersonal pressure to succeed; psychological factors (rigidity, ritualism, ineffectiveness, distorted body image, possible abuse history); media/societal pressure; childhood anxiety/obsessional traits

Expected Findings

Findings span distorted cognition and multisystem physical effects, particularly in clients who purge.

Mental status & cognitive distortions

  • Overgeneralization, all-or-nothing thinking, catastrophizing, personalization, emotional reasoning
  • High interest in preparing food but not eating; terror of gaining weight; perceives self as overweight
  • Low self-esteem, impulsivity, guilt/shame after bingeing; obsessive-compulsive features (collecting recipes, eating in public)

Physical findings by system

Integumentary & HEENT

  • Anorexia: fine downy hair (lanugo), yellowed skin, poor turgor
  • Purging: calluses/scars on hand (Russell's sign), parotid enlargement, dental erosion

Cardiovascular & fluid

  • Dysrhythmias, heart failure, cardiomyopathy, peripheral edema, acrocyanosis
  • Dehydration, electrolyte imbalances, acidosis or alkalosis

GI, MSK & reproductive

  • Constipation/diarrhea, abdominal pain, esophageal tears (bulimia)
  • Muscle weakness, loss of bone density; amenorrhea/menstrual irregularities

Standardized screening tools

  • Eating Disorder Inventory; Body Attitude Test; Diagnostic Survey for Eating Disorders; Eating Attitudes Test

Nursing Care

Care combines a structured milieu, a trusting relationship, and behavioral strategies, with close monitoring around meals.

Nursing care

  • Self-assess feelings (frustration, belief the disorder is self-imposed); provide a highly structured milieu for intensive therapy
  • Build a trusting relationship through consistency; use a positive approach to support self-esteem; encourage decision-making and participation in the plan of care to provide a sense of control
  • Promote cognitive-behavioral therapies (cognitive reframing, relaxation, journaling, desensitization); use behavioral contracts and reward positive behaviors
  • Closely monitor during and after meals to prevent purging (may include accompanying the client); monitor for appropriate exercise; collaborate with a dietitian for nutrition education and meal structure; involve family

Treatment

Medication and layered therapy support recovery.

Medications & therapy

  • SSRI (fluoxetine) — initial response can take a few weeks, maximal response longer; avoid hazardous activities until effects are known; report intolerable sexual dysfunction
  • Arrange individual, group, and family therapy to resolve the personal issues contributing to the disorder

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Therapeutic Procedure.

Desensitization for mealtime anxiety

Therapeutic Procedure · Anorexia (restricting type)

A nurse is caring for a client who has anorexia of the restricting type. The client exhibits severe anxiety when food is offered, and the nurse plans to use desensitization as a behavioral therapy.

Description of procedure & indications

Desensitization gradually exposes the client to the anxiety-producing situation (mealtimes) while pairing it with relaxation, so anxiety decreases over time. It is indicated when intense anxiety interferes with treatment.

Outcomes / evaluation

The client demonstrates reduced anxiety at mealtimes and participates in the structured eating plan.

Nursing interventions

  • Provide a calm, supportive presence and coach relaxation techniques during exposure.
  • Maintain a consistent, structured routine and a positive therapeutic relationship.

Answer: A

Exercise 19.4 — Plan of Care

A nurse is planning care for a client who has anorexia nervosa with binge-eating and purging behavior. Which of the following actions should the nurse include in the client's plan of care?

  1. Allow the client to select preferred meal times.
  2. Establish consequences for purging behavior.
  3. Provide the client with a high-fat diet at the start of treatment.
  4. Implement one-to-one observation during meal times.

Answer: D

  • A. Provide a highly structured milieu rather than client-selected meal times.
  • B. Use a positive, non-punitive approach rather than consequences for purging.
  • C. Limit high-fat and gas-producing foods.

Exercise 19.5 — Therapeutic Response

A nurse is caring for a client who has bulimia nervosa and has stopped purging behavior. The client tells the nurse about fears of gaining weight. Which of the following responses should the nurse make?

  1. "Many clients are concerned about their weight. However, the dietitian will ensure that you don't get too many calories in your diet."
  2. "Instead of worrying about your weight, try to focus on other problems at this time."
  3. "I understand you have concerns about your weight, but first, let's talk about your recent accomplishments."
  4. "You are not overweight, and the staff will ensure that you do not gain weight while you are in the hospital. We know that is important to you."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Minimizes and generalizes the client's concern.
  • B. Minimizes and dismisses the client's concern.
  • C. Correct — Acknowledges the client's feelings and redirects toward strengths/accomplishments.
  • D. Minimizes the concern and offers false reassurance.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Unit 3 · Psychobiologic Disorders · Chapter 20

Somatic Symptom and Related Disorders

Somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety, conversion disorder, and factitious disorders — risk factors and the focus on coping.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Somatic symptom and related disorders involve physical symptoms or health preoccupations not fully explained by a medical condition. Somatic symptom disorder: distressing physical symptoms with excessive thoughts/anxiety; risk factors include age 16-25, anxiety disorders, and childhood trauma. Illness anxiety disorder: preoccupation with having a serious illness — obsessive thoughts about disease, possible care avoidance, often with depression and a history of childhood abuse. Conversion disorder: neurological-type symptoms (e.g., sudden blindness/deafness/paralysis) following a recent psychological stressor (such as the death of a child). Factitious disorder: intentionally falsifying illness in oneself, or imposed on another (making a dependent sick). Nursing care focuses on alternative coping strategies, setting reasonable limits on symptom discussion, and encouraging communication.

Distinguishing the disorders

  • Illness anxiety — preoccupation with having a disease
  • Conversion — neuro symptoms after a stressor
  • Factitious (on another) — making a dependent sick
  • Malingering — faking for external gain (not a disorder)

Nursing focus

  • Teach alternative coping strategies
  • Set time limits on symptom-focused discussion
  • Encourage communication; monitor safety as indicated

The Disorders

In somatic symptom and related disorders, psychological stress is expressed through physical manifestations. Clients are usually seen first in primary/medical care, often reject a psychological cause, and seek care from multiple providers.

Somatic symptom & illness anxiety

  • Somatic symptom disorder — physical manifestations (unexplained by pathology) cause distress and dominate the client's life; anxiety and depression are common comorbidities
  • Illness anxiety disorder (formerly hypochondriasis) — misinterprets bodily sensations as a serious disease; manifestations may be minimal/absent; health-seeking type (frequent care) vs. care-avoidant type

Conversion, factitious & psychological factors

  • Conversion disorder (functional neurologic) — neurologic deficits (paralysis, blindness, seizures) without a neurologic diagnosis; some show la belle indifference; ~95% remit, often after an acute stressor
  • Factitious disorder (formerly Munchausen) — conscious falsification of manifestations for an emotional need (attention), without external gain; differs from malingering (conscious, for personal gain, not a mental illness); factitious imposed on another causes injury/illness to a vulnerable person
  • Psychological factors affecting other medical conditions — psychological/behavioral factors contribute to or worsen a confirmed medical diagnosis

Findings & Risk Factors

Manifestations are usually chronic with remissions and exacerbations; tests rule out underlying pathology.

Findings & risk factors

  • Findings: somatic manifestations disrupting daily life, excessive preoccupation/anxiety about them (often >6 months), high health-service use, possible overmedication with analgesics/anxiolytics
  • Risk factors: first-degree relative with the disorder, decreased serotonin/endorphins, comorbid depressive/anxiety/personality disorder, childhood trauma/abuse/neglect, learned helplessness
  • PHQ-15 identifies the 15 most commonly reported somatic manifestations

Nursing Care

Care accepts the client's manifestations as real while gently redirecting toward psychological links and coping.

Nursing care

  • Accept somatic manifestations as real to the client; assess for suicidal ideation/self-harm; identify secondary gains (attention, distraction from obligations)
  • Report new physical manifestations to the provider; limit the time allowed to discuss somatic manifestations; encourage independence in self-care
  • Encourage verbalization of feelings; educate on alternative coping and assertiveness; for factitious disorder, communicate suspicions openly with the team to reduce unnecessary treatment

Reattribution treatment (4 stages)

  • 1. Feeling understood — therapeutic communication, active listening, empathy, brief physical assessment
  • 2. Broadening the agenda — acknowledge concerns and give feedback on findings
  • 3. Making the link — acknowledge the lack of a physical cause while preserving self-esteem
  • 4. Negotiating further treatment — develop a plan with regular follow-up visits

Treatment

Medications and scheduled follow-up reduce reliance on unscheduled care.

Medications & education

  • Medications: antidepressants, anxiolytics, and analgesics as prescribed
  • Participate in individual and group therapy; attend community support groups
  • Collaborate with a case manager for brief, regularly scheduled provider visits — this decreases the need for unscheduled care and reduces costs

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: System Disorder.

Psychological factors affecting medical conditions

System Disorder · Somatic

A nurse is caring for a client who has psychological factors affecting other medical conditions.

Expected findings & risk factors

Findings: psychological/behavioral factors (stress, maladaptive coping) that adversely affect a medical condition. Risk factors: anxiety, depression, and a history of trauma or chronic stress.

Nursing care — three interventions

  • Establish a trusting therapeutic relationship and assess coping patterns.
  • Teach and reinforce adaptive coping and stress-management strategies.
  • Coordinate interprofessional care to address both the medical condition and psychological factors.

Exercise 20.4 — Plan of Care for Conversion Disorder

A nurse is developing a plan of care for a client who has conversion disorder. Which of the following actions should the nurse include?

  1. Encourage the client to spend time alone in their room.
  2. Monitor the client for self-harm once per day.
  3. Allow the client unlimited time to discuss physical manifestations.
  4. Discuss alternative coping strategies with the client.

Answer: D

  • A. Encourage communication with others rather than isolation.
  • B. If at risk, monitor for self-harm continuously, not once per day.
  • C. Establish a time limit for discussion of physical manifestations.
  • D. Correct — Discussing alternative coping strategies addresses the underlying psychological needs.

Exercise 20.5 — Factitious Disorder Imposed on Another

A nurse is counseling several clients. Which of the following client statements should the nurse identify as expected for factitious disorder imposed on another?

  1. "I had to pretend I was injured in order to get disability benefits."
  2. "I know that my abdominal pain is caused by a malignant tumor."
  3. "I needed to make my child sick so that someone else would take care of them for a while."
  4. "I became deaf when I heard that my partner was having an affair with my best friend."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Faking injury for an external reward (benefits) is malingering.
  • B. Conviction of having a serious illness reflects illness anxiety/somatic symptom disorder.
  • C. Correct — Intentionally making a dependent ill to gain attention/care reflects factitious disorder imposed on another.
  • D. Sudden sensory loss after a stressor reflects conversion disorder.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Mental Health Concepts

Unit 4 · Psychopharmacological Therapies · Chapter 21

Medications for Anxiety and Trauma- and Stressor-Related Disorders

Benzodiazepines, buspirone, and SSRIs/SNRIs for anxiety and trauma — therapeutic use, toxicity, and adverse-effect management.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Benzodiazepines (e.g., alprazolam) act quickly for short-term anxiety but carry dependence risk; report confusion as a sign of toxicity, avoid alcohol (additive CNS depression), and reverse overdose with flumazenil (after assessing the client per the nursing process). Buspirone is non-sedating and non-dependence-forming but requires 3-6 weeks for full effect. SSRIs (e.g., fluoxetine, paroxetine) are first-line for many anxiety/trauma disorders: take in the morning, taper to discontinue, watch for weight gain and hyponatremia. Serotonin syndrome (hallucinations, agitation, diaphoresis, fever, tremors) is an emergency. SSRI bruxism can be managed with a mouth guard, low-dose buspirone, or changing antianxiety medication class.

Drug class snapshots

  • Benzodiazepines — fast, dependence risk; flumazenil reverses
  • Buspirone — non-sedating; 3-6 weeks to work
  • SSRIs — first-line; taper to stop; AM dosing

Serotonin syndrome

  • Hallucinations, agitation, diaphoresis
  • Fever, muscle tremors/rigidity
  • A medical emergency — report immediately

Benzodiazepines

Benzodiazepines (alprazolam, lorazepam, diazepam, clonazepam) enhance the inhibitory effects of GABA in the CNS, relieving anxiety rapidly. Because of dependence risk, they are generally used short-term.

Uses & complications

  • Uses: short-term GAD and panic disorder; also seizures, insomnia, muscle spasm, alcohol withdrawal, anesthesia induction
  • CNS depression (sedation, ataxia) — avoid alcohol/other CNS depressants and hazardous activities
  • Anterograde amnesia, paradoxical response (insomnia, excitation, rage), and withdrawal (anxiety, insomnia, possible seizures)

Toxicity, contraindications & interactions

  • Toxicity: oral (drowsiness, confusion); IV (respiratory depression, severe hypotension, cardiac arrest) — antidote is flumazenil
  • Pregnancy Risk Category D; Schedule IV; contraindicated in sleep apnea, respiratory depression, glaucoma; assess fall risk in older adults
  • Taper over weeks to discontinue; keep secure due to misuse potential

Buspirone

Buspirone is an atypical anxiolytic that binds serotonin and dopamine receptors, with less dependency potential than benzodiazepines and no sedation or risk of misuse.

Buspirone

  • Use: generalized anxiety disorder; taken on a scheduled basis, not PRN
  • Effects develop slowly — initial response ~1 week, full effect up to 4 weeks
  • Contraindicated with MAOIs (hypertensive crisis); avoid erythromycin, ketoconazole, St. John's wort, and grapefruit juice (increase effects)
  • No tolerance, dependence, or withdrawal

SSRIs

SSRIs (paroxetine, sertraline, fluoxetine, citalopram, escitalopram, fluvoxamine) selectively inhibit serotonin reuptake and are first-line for anxiety, OCD, panic, and trauma-/stressor-related disorders. Full effect takes up to 4 weeks.

Complications

  • Early: nausea, diaphoresis, tremor, drowsiness (subside); later: sexual dysfunction, weight changes
  • Serotonin syndrome (can begin 2-72 hr; potentially lethal): confusion, agitation, hallucinations, hyperreflexia, fever, diaphoresis — withhold medication and notify provider
  • GI bleeding, hyponatremia (older adults on diuretics), bruxism, withdrawal syndrome (taper to discontinue)

Contraindications & interactions

  • Paroxetine is Pregnancy Risk Category D; use cautiously in bipolar disorder (mania risk)
  • Concurrent TCAs, MAOIs, or St. John's wort → serotonin syndrome (discontinue MAOIs 14 days before; fluoxetine 5 weeks before an MAOI)
  • Warfarin (increased levels), NSAIDs/anticoagulants (bleeding risk)

SNRIs

SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine, desvenlafaxine) inhibit serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake.

SNRIs

  • Uses: major depression, panic disorder, GAD
  • Headache, nausea, hyponatremia, hypertension, sexual dysfunction; monitor blood pressure
  • Contraindicated with MAOIs; duloxetine avoided in hepatic disease/heavy alcohol use; avoid abrupt cessation

Other Agents

Several non-antidepressant classes target specific anxiety and trauma manifestations.

Adjunct medications

  • Beta blockers (propranolol) — performance anxiety; reduce autonomic symptoms
  • Centrally-acting alpha blockers (prazosin) — decrease PTSD hypervigilance and insomnia
  • Alpha-2 agonists (clonidine), anticonvulsants (gabapentin, pregabalin), antihistamines (hydroxyzine), mirtazapine, trazodone, TCAs, and MAOIs

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Buspirone teaching

Medication · Antianxiety

A nurse is providing teaching for a client who has a new prescription for buspirone.

Therapeutic use & complications

Use: long-term management of generalized anxiety disorder (non-sedating, non-dependence-forming). Adverse effects: dizziness, nausea, headache, lightheadedness.

Interactions & client education

  • Interactions: avoid MAOIs and erythromycin; avoid grapefruit juice (raises drug levels).
  • Education: take consistently; full effect takes 3-6 weeks, so do not expect immediate relief; avoid grapefruit and report interacting medications.

Exercise 21.1 — Alprazolam Teaching

A nurse is teaching a client who has a new prescription for alprazolam for generalized anxiety disorder. Which of the following information should the nurse provide?

  1. Three to six weeks of treatment is required to achieve therapeutic benefit.
  2. Combining alcohol with alprazolam will produce a paradoxical response.
  3. Alprazolam has a lower risk for dependence than other antianxiety medications.

Answer: D

  • A. Buspirone (not alprazolam) requires 3 to 6 weeks for full benefit.
  • B. Alcohol with alprazolam causes additive CNS/respiratory depression, not a paradoxical response.
  • C. Alprazolam carries a higher dependence risk and is used short-term.

Exercise 21.5 — Managing SSRI Bruxism (Select All That Apply)

Unit 4 · Psychopharmacological Therapies · Chapter 22

Medications for Depressive Disorders

TCAs, MAOIs, SSRIs, and atypical antidepressants (bupropion, mirtazapine) — adverse effects, interactions, and safety priorities.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Tricyclic antidepressants (TCAs) (e.g., amitriptyline, imipramine) cause sedation, anticholinergic effects (dry mouth, urinary retention, blurred vision, constipation), and orthostatic hypotension — minimize anticholinergic effects by voiding before doses, wearing sunglasses, and chewing sugarless gum. MAOIs (e.g., phenelzine) cause orthostatic hypotension and risk a hypertensive crisis with tyramine-containing foods (aged cheese, cured meats like pepperoni); can precipitate hypomania. Bupropion lowers the seizure threshold — a head-injury or seizure history is the priority to report; it aids smoking cessation and is contraindicated in eating disorders. Mirtazapine increases serotonin and norepinephrine release, commonly causes drowsiness (and weight gain), but has low sexual dysfunction. All antidepressants: watch for emerging suicidal ideation and a switch to hypomania/mania.

Class-specific cautions

  • TCAs — sedation + anticholinergic effects
  • MAOIs — tyramine/hypertensive crisis; orthostasis
  • Bupropion — seizure threshold ↓; no use in eating disorders

Minimizing TCA anticholinergic effects

  • Void just before each dose (urinary retention)
  • Sunglasses outside (photophobia); sugarless gum (dry mouth)
  • Change positions slowly (orthostatic hypotension)

Tricyclic Antidepressants (TCAs)

TCAs (amitriptyline, imipramine, nortriptyline, others) block reuptake of norepinephrine and serotonin. Onset is slow — 10-14 days to begin, up to several weeks for maximum effect.

Complications & toxicity

  • Anticholinergic effects, orthostatic hypotension, sedation; decreased seizure threshold; increased appetite
  • Toxicity causes cholinergic blockade and cardiac toxicity (dysrhythmias, confusion → seizures, coma, death) — lethal in overdose, so dispense limited supply to acutely ill clients; obtain baseline ECG
  • Contraindicated with MAOIs (severe hypertension) and seizure disorders; can increase suicide risk

SSRIs

SSRIs (fluoxetine, sertraline, citalopram, escitalopram, paroxetine) are the first-line treatment for depression, intensifying serotonin's effects.

SSRIs in depression

  • Uses: major depression, OCD, bulimia, PMDD, panic, PTSD, GAD, social anxiety
  • Sexual dysfunction (manage with dose reduction, drug holiday, or switching to bupropion), GI bleeding, bruxism, hyponatremia
  • Take in the morning (minimize sleep disturbance) with food; serotonin-syndrome risk with MAOIs/TCAs/St. John's wort

SNRIs & Atypical Antidepressants

SNRIs and several atypical agents offer alternatives with different effect profiles.

SNRIs, mirtazapine & trazodone

  • SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine, desvenlafaxine) — monitor blood pressure, hyponatremia, weight loss; taper to discontinue
  • Mirtazapine — faster effect with less sexual dysfunction; causes sleepiness, increased appetite/weight, elevated cholesterol
  • Trazodone — sedating (good for SSRI-induced insomnia, taken at bedtime); priapism is a serious effect requiring immediate care

Bupropion & vilazodone

  • Bupropion — inhibits dopamine uptake; used for depression, an alternative when SSRI sexual dysfunction is a problem, and to aid smoking cessation; suppresses appetite; contraindicated with seizure disorder and eating disorders
  • Vilazodone — blocks serotonin reuptake and is a partial serotonin agonist; risk of serotonin syndrome; take with food

Monoamine Oxidase Inhibitors (MAOIs)

MAOIs (phenelzine, isocarboxazid, tranylcypromine, selegiline patch) block MAO in the brain, raising norepinephrine, dopamine, and serotonin. They are a first-line option for atypical depression but require strict dietary control.

Hypertensive crisis & precautions

  • Hypertensive crisis from dietary tyramine (aged cheese, cured/smoked meats, avocados, figs, some beer/wine) — headache, severe hypertension; treat with IV phentolamine
  • Provide written lists of foods/beverages to avoid; avoid OTC decongestants (sympathomimetics)
  • Contraindicated with SSRIs (serotonin syndrome) and TCAs; stop 10-14 days before surgery; avoid meperidine (hyperpyrexia)

Suicide Risk & Evaluation

Antidepressants can increase suicide risk early in treatment, particularly in clients under age 25.

Safety & effectiveness

  • For an acutely ill client, prescribe only a 1-week supply, then 1 month at a time — especially with TCAs (high lethality in overdose); assess suicide risk regularly
  • Therapeutic effect is not immediate (can take 1-3 weeks, full effect 2-3 months); do not discontinue suddenly
  • Effectiveness: improved mood, sleep, and appetite; ability to perform ADLs; increased peer interaction

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Sertraline teaching

Medication · SSRI

A nurse is providing teaching for a client who has a new prescription for sertraline for the treatment of depression.

Complications — adverse effects

Nausea/GI upset, headache, insomnia or drowsiness, sexual dysfunction, weight changes, and (rarely) serotonin syndrome and hyponatremia.

Interactions & nursing interventions

  • Interactions: MAOIs (risk of serotonin syndrome — avoid concurrent use); other serotonergic drugs and St. John's Wort.
  • Administration: give in the morning to reduce insomnia; teach that full effect takes several weeks and to taper rather than stop abruptly.

Unit 4 · Psychopharmacological Therapies · Chapter 23

Medications for Bipolar Disorders

Lithium and mood-stabilizing anticonvulsants — therapeutic range, toxicity, interactions, and monitoring.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Lithium is a first-line mood stabilizer with a narrow therapeutic range (acute mania around 1.0-1.4 mEq/L; maintenance lower). Early toxicity: polyuria, muscle weakness, diarrhea, fine tremor; severe toxicity: tinnitus, confusion, ataxia. Avoid NSAIDs (ibuprofen raises lithium levels — use aspirin or acetaminophen instead) and diuretics (furosemide raises levels — a priority to report). Maintain consistent sodium/fluid intake. Mood-stabilizing anticonvulsants: valproate requires monitoring of liver function (AST/ALT/LDH); carbamazepine treats manic/depressive episodes and prevents relapse (monitor CBC for blood dyscrasias). During an acute manic episode, a lithium level around 1.2 mEq/L is expected — administer the scheduled dose.

Lithium toxicity stages

  • Early: polyuria, muscle weakness, diarrhea, tremor
  • Severe: tinnitus, confusion, ataxia
  • Avoid NSAIDs and diuretics (raise levels)

Anticonvulsant monitoring

  • Valproate — liver function (AST/ALT/LDH)
  • Carbamazepine — CBC for blood dyscrasias
  • Both stabilize mood and help prevent relapse

Lithium

Lithium carbonate is the primary mood stabilizer for bipolar disorder. It controls acute mania, prevents recurrence of mania/depression, and decreases the incidence of suicide. It has a narrow therapeutic range, so blood levels are monitored closely.

Common adverse effects & monitoring

  • GI distress (take with food), fine hand tremors (treat with propranolol), polyuria/thirst, weight gain
  • Renal toxicity (monitor I&O, BUN, creatinine) and, long-term, goiter/hypothyroidism (monitor thyroid; may need levothyroxine)
  • Maintenance level ~0.6-1.2 mEq/L (higher, 1-1.5, during acute mania); draw level in the morning ~10-12 hr after the last dose; maintain adequate fluid and sodium intake

Lithium toxicity (by level)

Early (< 2.0 mEq/L)

  • Diarrhea, nausea/vomiting, thirst, polyuria, muscle weakness, coarse tremors, slurred speech, mental confusion — withhold and notify provider

Advanced (2.0-2.5)

  • Extreme polyuria, tinnitus, blurred vision, ataxia, seizures, severe hypotension, stupor → coma

Severe (> 2.5)

  • Rapid progression to coma and death; hemodialysis may be warranted

Contraindications & interactions

  • Pregnancy Risk Category D (teratogenic); contraindicated in severe renal/cardiac disease, hypovolemia
  • Diuretics and NSAIDs decrease lithium excretion → toxicity (use aspirin instead of other NSAIDs)
  • Withhold and seek care if experiencing diarrhea, vomiting, or excessive sweating (volume loss raises levels)

Mood-Stabilizing Antiepileptics

Carbamazepine, valproate, and lamotrigine stabilize mood by modulating sodium/calcium channels, GABA, and glutamate. They are especially useful in mixed mania and rapid cycling.

Key adverse effects by agent

Carbamazepine

  • CNS effects (nystagmus, diplopia, ataxia); blood dyscrasias (monitor CBC/platelets); decreases effect of oral contraceptives/warfarin

Valproate

  • GI distress; hepatotoxicity (monitor LFTs) and pancreatitis; thrombocytopenia

Lamotrigine

  • Serious skin rashes incl. Stevens-Johnson syndrome — start low and titrate slowly; withhold and notify provider for rash

Other agents for bipolar disorder

  • Antipsychotics (e.g., lurasidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, aripiprazole) for acute mania; lurasidone and quetiapine for bipolar depression
  • Anxiolytics (clonazepam, lorazepam) for acute mania/agitation
  • Antidepressants for the depressive phase — given with a mood stabilizer to prevent rebound mania

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Carbamazepine for rapid-cycling bipolar disorder

Medication · Mood stabilizer

A nurse is providing teaching to a client who has rapid-cycling bipolar disorder and a new prescription for carbamazepine.

Therapeutic uses

Carbamazepine treats manic and depressive episodes and helps prevent relapse, and is useful for rapid-cycling presentations.

Complications

CNS effects (dizziness, drowsiness), blood dyscrasias (leukopenia, anemia), hyponatremia, and rash (including rare severe skin reactions).

Nursing interventions / education

  • Monitor CBC and sodium; report fever, sore throat, bruising, or rash.
  • Teach consistent dosing, avoid abrupt discontinuation, and use sun protection.

Exercise 23.1 — Lithium and Ibuprofen

A nurse is caring for a client who is prescribed lithium therapy. The client tells of the plan to take ibuprofen for osteoarthritis pain relief. Which of the following statements should the nurse make?

  1. "That is a good choice. Ibuprofen does not interact with lithium."
  2. "Regular aspirin would be a better choice than ibuprofen."
  3. "Lithium decreases the effectiveness of ibuprofen."
  4. "The ibuprofen will make your lithium level fall too low."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Ibuprofen is not recommended for clients taking lithium.
  • B. Correct — Aspirin is recommended as a mild analgesic for clients taking lithium (NSAIDs like ibuprofen increase lithium levels).
  • C. Lithium does not decrease ibuprofen's effectiveness.
  • D. Ibuprofen increases (not lowers) the lithium level, raising toxicity risk.

Exercise 23.4 — Lithium Level During Mania

A nurse is caring for a client who is experiencing extreme mania due to bipolar disorder. Prior to administration of lithium carbonate, the client's lithium blood level is 1.2 mEq/L. Which of the following actions should the nurse take?

  1. Administer the next dose of lithium carbonate as scheduled.

  3. Notify the provider for a possible increase in the dosage of lithium carbonate.
  4. Request a stat repeat of the client's lithium blood level.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • B. Aminophylline is used to treat lithium toxicity, which is not present here.
  • C. A dosage increase would risk toxicity.
  • D. A repeat level is unnecessary because 1.2 mEq/L is an expected finding.

Exercise 23.5 — Priority History to Report

A nurse is admitting a client who has a new diagnosis of bipolar disorder and is scheduled to begin lithium therapy. When collecting a medical history from the client's caregiver, which of the following statements is the priority to report to the provider?

  1. "Current medical conditions include diabetes that is controlled by diet."
  2. "Recent medications include a course of prednisone for acute bronchitis."
  3. "Current vaccinations include a flu vaccine last month."
  4. "Current medications include furosemide for congestive heart failure."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A, B, C. All findings should be reported, but they are not the greatest risk.

Unit 4 · Psychopharmacological Therapies · Chapter 24

Medications for Psychotic Disorders

First- vs. second-generation antipsychotics — symptom targeting, EPS, formulations, and adherence teaching.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

First-generation (typical) antipsychotics (e.g., chlorpromazine, haloperidol, thiothixene, perphenazine) primarily treat positive symptoms (hallucinations, delusions, agitation) but cause extrapyramidal symptoms (EPS) — drooling, involuntary movements, akathisia (continual pacing), dystonia, and tardive dyskinesia. Second-generation (atypical) antipsychotics (e.g., risperidone, olanzapine, clozapine, aripiprazole) treat both positive and negative symptoms (lack of grooming, flat affect) with fewer EPS but more metabolic effects (weight gain, hyperglycemia). For clients who cannot swallow or refuse injections, several atypicals offer orally disintegrating or sublingual forms. Antipsychotics are long-term; stopping abruptly causes relapse — teach adherence.

Symptom targeting

  • 1st-gen: positive symptoms; high EPS risk
  • 2nd-gen: positive + negative symptoms; metabolic effects
  • EPS: drooling, involuntary movements, akathisia

Adherence teaching

  • Long-term treatment; do not stop when feeling better
  • Manage weight/metabolic effects with atypicals
  • ODT/sublingual forms if swallowing is difficult

First-Generation (Conventional) Antipsychotics

First-generation antipsychotics (chlorpromazine, haloperidol, fluphenazine) block dopamine (D2), acetylcholine, histamine, and norepinephrine receptors. They mainly control positive symptoms and are classified low-, medium-, or high-potency by their EPS, sedation, and anticholinergic profiles.

Potency & uses

  • Low potency (chlorpromazine): low EPS, high sedation/anticholinergic; high potency (haloperidol, fluphenazine): high EPS, low sedation/anticholinergic
  • Uses: acute/chronic psychosis, schizophrenia, bipolar mania, Tourette disorder, agitation, and as an antiemetic
  • Contraindicated in coma, Parkinson's disease, and older adults with dementia

Extrapyramidal Symptoms (EPS)

EPS are the hallmark adverse effects of first-generation antipsychotics. The AIMS scale screens for them; anticholinergics, beta-blockers, and benzodiazepines manage early EPS.

The four EPS

Acute dystonia (1-5 days)

  • Severe spasm of tongue, neck, face, back — a crisis; treat with benztropine or IM/IV diphenhydramine; monitor airway

Pseudoparkinsonism (5-30 days)

  • Bradykinesia, rigidity, shuffling gait, tremors, drooling; treat with benztropine or trihexyphenidyl

Akathisia (5-60 days)

  • Inability to sit still, pacing; manage with antiparkinsonian agents, beta blockers, or a benzodiazepine; monitor suicide risk

Tardive dyskinesia (months-years)

  • Involuntary tongue/face movements (lip smacking); evaluate after 12 months then every 3 months; usually irreversible, no reliable treatment

Other Complications

Beyond EPS, antipsychotics carry a life-threatening emergency and several system effects.

Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS)

  • A life-threatening emergency: sudden high fever, blood-pressure fluctuations, diaphoresis, muscle rigidity, decreased LOC, coma
  • Stop the antipsychotic; apply cooling blankets and antipyretics; give dantrolene or bromocriptine; transfer to ICU; wait ~2 weeks before resuming (consider an atypical agent)

Other effects

  • Agranulocytosis (discontinue if WBC < 3,000/mm³ — watch for fever/sore throat), anticholinergic effects, orthostatic hypotension, sedation, seizures
  • Neuroendocrine (gynecomastia, galactorrhea, menstrual irregularities), severe dysrhythmias, photosensitivity, liver impairment

Second- & Third-Generation (Atypical) Antipsychotics

Atypicals (risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, ziprasidone, clozapine, aripiprazole) block serotonin more than dopamine and are first-line — relieving both positive and negative symptoms with fewer EPS.

Class effects

  • Metabolic syndrome — new-onset diabetes, dyslipidemia, weight gain; monitor fasting glucose, lipids, and weight
  • Elevated prolactin, sexual dysfunction, mild EPS, orthostatic hypotension
  • Contraindicated in dementia (increased death risk); third-generation agents (aripiprazole) have the lowest EPS/metabolic risk

Agent-specific cautions

  • Clozapine — risk of fatal agranulocytosis, requiring regular WBC monitoring (weekly → biweekly → monthly)
  • Olanzapine — high diabetes/weight/dyslipidemia risk; ziprasidone — QT prolongation (torsades risk), take with food
  • Lurasidone — low metabolic risk, take with food; asenapine — sublingual, avoid eating/drinking 10 min after

Nursing Administration

Screening, adherence, and depot options shape antipsychotic management.

Administration

  • Use the AIMS scale to screen for EPS; differentiate EPS from worsening psychosis
  • Consider depot preparations (haloperidol/fluphenazine decanoate, IM every 2-4 weeks) for clients with adherence difficulty
  • Antipsychotics rarely cause dependence; some effect within days, but full effect can take 2-4 weeks or longer

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Clozapine teaching

Medication · Atypical antipsychotic

A nurse is providing teaching to a client who has a prescription for clozapine.

Therapeutic uses

Treats schizophrenia (including treatment-resistant cases) and reduces both positive and negative symptoms.

Complications

Agranulocytosis (requires routine WBC/ANC monitoring), seizures, metabolic effects (weight gain, hyperglycemia), sedation, and orthostatic hypotension.

Evaluation of effectiveness

Reduced hallucinations/delusions, improved social engagement and self-care, and no signs of infection (intact WBC counts).
