Unit 4 · Psychopharmacological Therapies · Chapter 25

Medications for Children and Adolescents Who Have Mental Health Issues

TCAs, SSRIs, stimulants, and atomoxetine in pediatric clients — toxicity, hepatotoxicity, and administration teaching.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

TCAs (imipramine, clomipramine) in children: monitor for toxicity (seizures, agitation, irregular pulse/dysrhythmia) — distinct from the expected anticholinergic effects (dry mouth, photophobia, constipation; manage with a high-fiber diet); give at bedtime due to sedation. Atomoxetine (ADHD): monitor for hepatotoxicity (yellowing skin, fever, malaise); expect insomnia and decreased appetite/weight loss. Stimulant transdermal patches (methylphenidate): apply once daily to a clean, dry hip in the morning and remove after about 9 hr; dispose of safely. SSRIs (fluoxetine): give in the morning, expect weight loss and possible CNS stimulation; works by blocking serotonin reuptake; initial effects in 1-3 weeks (full effect takes longer). Monitor all pediatric clients for emerging suicidal ideation.

Toxicity vs side effects

  • TCA toxicity: seizures, agitation, dysrhythmia
  • TCA anticholinergic: dry mouth, photophobia, constipation
  • Atomoxetine: hepatotoxicity — yellow skin, fever, malaise

Administration teaching

  • Methylphenidate patch: clean dry hip, remove after ~9 hr
  • Fluoxetine: morning dosing; serotonin reuptake blockade
  • Monitor for suicidal ideation across agents

CNS Stimulants

CNS stimulants (methylphenidate, amphetamine mixtures, lisdexamfetamine) raise norepinephrine and dopamine levels and are first-line for ADHD. Pharmacological management works best alongside behavior-modification techniques.

Complications & administration

  • CNS stimulation (insomnia, restlessness) — give the last dose before 4 p.m.; avoid caffeine
  • Growth/appetite suppression, weight loss — monitor height/weight against baseline; administer during/after meals; consider a drug "holiday"
  • Cardiovascular effects (dysrhythmias, hypertension) — can increase sudden-death risk with cardiac abnormalities; monitor vital signs/ECG
  • High potential for a substance use disorder in adolescents; special federal handling/refill rules; ADHD is not cured by medication

Contraindications & interactions

  • Contraindicated with a history of substance use disorder, cardiovascular disorders, severe anxiety, or psychosis; Pregnancy Risk Category C
  • MAOIs → hypertensive crisis (separate by 14 days); avoid OTC cold/decongestant medications
  • Full response can take up to 6 weeks; do not chew/crush sustained-release tablets

Atomoxetine & TCAs

Non-stimulant options treat ADHD and related disorders in children, with distinct safety monitoring.

Atomoxetine (selective NRI) & TCAs

  • Atomoxetine — a non-stimulant for ADHD; appetite/growth suppression, GI effects, hepatotoxicity; monitor for suicidal ideation in children/adolescents
  • TCAs (desipramine, imipramine, clomipramine) — for depression, autism spectrum, ADHD, anxiety/OCD; anticholinergic effects, orthostatic hypotension, sedation; cardiac toxicity in overdose, so dispense limited supply (high suicide potential)

Alpha-2 Adrenergic Agonists

Guanfacine and clonidine activate presynaptic alpha-2 receptors in the brain.

Guanfacine & clonidine

  • Uses: ADHD, tic disorders, conduct/oppositional defiant disorders
  • CNS effects (sedation), cardiovascular effects (hypotension, bradycardia); abrupt discontinuation causes rebound hypertension — taper
  • Safety not established under age 6; monitor blood pressure and pulse at baseline and each dose change

SSRIs & Atypical Antipsychotics

SSRIs and atypical antipsychotics address mood, anxiety, and behavioral disorders in younger clients.

SSRIs & atypical antipsychotics in youth

  • SSRIs (fluoxetine, sertraline, fluvoxamine) — for OCD, anxiety, depression, intermittent explosive disorder, autism; may increase suicidal ideation in children/adolescents — monitor closely and taper to discontinue
  • Atypical antipsychotics (risperidone, olanzapine, aripiprazole, quetiapine) — for autism spectrum, conduct disorder, OCD; monitor for diabetes, weight gain (metabolic effects), and EPS; start low and titrate

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Olanzapine in a pediatric client

Medication · Atypical antipsychotic

A nurse working in a pediatric mental health clinic is caring for a client who has a new prescription for olanzapine.

Complications

Weight gain and metabolic effects (hyperglycemia, dyslipidemia), sedation, orthostatic hypotension, and extrapyramidal symptoms.

Nursing interventions to minimize adverse effects

  • Monitor weight, blood glucose, and lipids; promote nutrition and activity.
  • Teach slow position changes for orthostatic hypotension.
  • Assess for EPS and sedation; administer at bedtime if sedating.
  • Reinforce adherence and follow-up monitoring.

Unit 4 · Psychopharmacological Therapies · Chapter 26

Medications for Substance Use Disorders

Withdrawal management and long-term abstinence agents for alcohol, opioid, and tobacco use disorders.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Alcohol withdrawal: benzodiazepines (lorazepam, diazepam) for short-term safe withdrawal; carbamazepine helps prevent withdrawal seizures; clonidine/propranolol manage autonomic symptoms. Long-term alcohol abstinence: disulfiram (aversion — reaction with alcohol), naltrexone (suppresses craving/reward), and acamprosate (decreases unpleasant abstinence effects). Opioid use disorder: clonidine eases withdrawal symptoms (causes dry mouth — keep sugarless gum; reduces, not causes, diarrhea); buprenorphine (sublingual) and methadone are opioid substitution agents — methadone is an oral opioid that prevents withdrawal, treats withdrawal and maintains abstinence, and is dispensed only by approved treatment centers. Tobacco use: nicotine gum (avoid eating/drinking ~15 min before; chew intermittently) and varenicline.

Alcohol — withdrawal vs abstinence

  • Withdrawal: benzodiazepines; carbamazepine prevents seizures
  • Abstinence: disulfiram, naltrexone, acamprosate

Opioid & tobacco agents

  • Clonidine — eases opioid withdrawal; causes dry mouth
  • Methadone — oral opioid; approved-center dispensing
  • Nicotine gum / varenicline — tobacco cessation

Withdrawal & Alcohol Withdrawal Management

Abstinence syndrome occurs when a physically dependent client abruptly withdraws. Alcohol withdrawal in particular can be life-threatening; benzodiazepines are the mainstay of management.

Withdrawal manifestations

Alcohol

  • Begins ~4-12 hr after the last intake: tremors, restlessness, increased vital signs, diaphoresis, possible tonic-clonic seizures; alcohol withdrawal delirium (2-3 days after cessation) is a medical emergency

Opioids

  • Agitation, insomnia, flu-like manifestations, rhinorrhea, sweating, diarrhea — not life-threatening, but suicidal ideation can occur

Alcohol withdrawal medications

  • Benzodiazepines (chlordiazepoxide, diazepam, lorazepam) — substitution therapy that decreases seizures and withdrawal intensity; implement seizure precautions
  • Adjuncts: carbamazepine (seizures); clonidine, propranolol, atenolol (decrease autonomic response/craving) — check heart rate before propranolol and withhold if below 60/min

Alcohol Abstinence Maintenance

Following withdrawal, three medications support continued abstinence.

Disulfiram, naltrexone & acamprosate

  • Disulfiram — aversion therapy; concurrent alcohol causes acetaldehyde syndrome (nausea, vomiting, hypotension, possibly fatal); avoid all alcohol-containing products (mouthwash, aftershave, hand sanitizer); effects persist ~2 weeks after stopping
  • Naltrexone — opioid antagonist that suppresses alcohol craving/pleasure (also used for opioids); monthly IM depot available; assess for opioid dependence first
  • Acamprosate — reduces unpleasant abstinence effects (dysphoria, anxiety); can cause diarrhea

Opioid Withdrawal & Antidotes

Opioid agonists substitute for the dependent substance, and antidotes reverse toxicity.

Opioid medications

  • Methadone substitution — oral opioid agonist for withdrawal and long-term maintenance; transfers dependence from the illegal opioid; dispensed only at an approved treatment center; taper to detoxify
  • Buprenorphine — agonist-antagonist for withdrawal/maintenance; decreases craving; can be prescribed by a primary care provider; given sublingually (some products contain naloxone)
  • Clonidine — eases autonomic withdrawal effects but does not reduce craving

Antidotes

  • Naloxone — opioid antagonist (IM/SQ/IV/inhalation) reversing respiratory depression, coma, and other opioid toxicity
  • Flumazenil — benzodiazepine receptor antagonist (IV) reversing sedative effects and toxicity

Nicotine Cessation

Bupropion, nicotine replacement therapy, and varenicline support tobacco cessation; nicotine products are avoided in pregnancy/breastfeeding.

Cessation medications

  • Bupropion — decreases nicotine craving and withdrawal; avoid caffeine/CNS stimulants to control insomnia
  • Nicotine replacement therapy (gum, patch, nasal spray, lozenge, inhaler) — nearly doubles cessation rates; remove the patch before an MRI; avoid other nicotine products while using
  • Varenicline — nicotinic receptor agonist that reduces craving and blocks nicotine's effects; can cause neuropsychiatric effects (mood changes, suicidal thoughts) — take after a meal and report mood changes

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Varenicline for tobacco use disorder

Medication · Smoking cessation

A nurse working in an outpatient clinic is teaching a client who has tobacco use disorder about the use of varenicline.

Expected pharmacological action & therapeutic use

Varenicline is a partial nicotinic receptor agonist that reduces nicotine cravings and the reinforcing effects of smoking; it is used as an aid to smoking cessation.

Nursing interventions

  • Teach the client to set a quit date and start the medication before it as prescribed.
  • Monitor for and report mood changes, depression, or suicidal ideation.
  • Take with food and a full glass of water to reduce nausea; report sleep disturbances/vivid dreams.

Exercise 26.1 — Carbamazepine in Alcohol Withdrawal

A nurse is providing teaching to a client who has alcohol use disorder and a new prescription for carbamazepine. Which of the following information should the nurse include in the teaching?

  1. "This medication will help prevent seizures during alcohol withdrawal."
  2. "Taking this medication will decrease your cravings for alcohol."
  3. "This medication maintains your blood pressure at a normal level during alcohol withdrawal."
  4. "Taking this medication will improve your ability to maintain abstinence from alcohol."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Carbamazepine is used during withdrawal to promote safe withdrawal and help prevent seizures.
  • B. Carbamazepine does not decrease alcohol cravings.
  • C. Clonidine or propranolol is used to manage autonomic symptoms such as blood pressure.
  • D. Maintaining abstinence is the role of disulfiram, naltrexone, or acamprosate.

Exercise 26.2 — Long-Term Abstinence Agents (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is assisting in the discharge planning for a client following alcohol detoxification. The nurse should expect prescriptions for which of the following medications to promote long-term abstinence from alcohol? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Lorazepam
  2. Diazepam
  3. Disulfiram
  4. Naltrexone
  5. Acamprosate
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C, D, E

  • A, B. Lorazepam and diazepam are used for short-term withdrawal, not long-term abstinence.
  • C. Correct — Disulfiram promotes abstinence through an aversive reaction with alcohol.

  1. "Taking this medication will help reduce my craving for heroin."
  2. "While taking this medication, I should keep a pack of sugarless gum."
  3. "I can expect some diarrhea from taking this medicine."
  4. "Each dose of this medication should be placed under my tongue to dissolve."

Exercise 26.4 — Nicotine Gum

A nurse is teaching a client who has tobacco use disorder about the use of nicotine gum. Which of the following information should the nurse include in the teaching?

  1. Chew the gum for no more than 10 min.
  2. Rinse out the mouth immediately before chewing the gum.
  3. Avoid eating 15 min prior to chewing the gum.
  4. Use of the gum is limited to 90 days.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Chew the gum slowly and intermittently rather than limiting to 10 min.
  • B. Avoid food and beverages (rather than rinsing) before chewing.
  • C. Correct — Avoid eating or drinking about 15 min before chewing, because acidic intake reduces nicotine absorption.

Exercise 26.5 — Methadone (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is discussing the use of methadone with a newly licensed nurse. Which of the following statements by the newly licensed nurse indicates an understanding of the teaching? (Select all that apply.)

  1. "Methadone is a replacement for physical dependence to opioids."
  2. "Methadone reduces the unpleasant effects associated with abstinence syndrome."
  3. "Methadone can be used during opioid withdrawal and to maintain abstinence."
  4. "Methadone increases the risk for acetaldehyde syndrome."
  5. "Methadone must be prescribed and dispensed by an approved treatment center."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, B, C, E

  • A. Correct — Methadone is an oral opioid used as a replacement to manage physical dependence.
  • B. Correct — Methadone prevents/reduces the unpleasant effects of abstinence syndrome.
  • C. Correct — Methadone is used both during withdrawal and to maintain abstinence.
  • D. Incorrect — Disulfiram (with alcohol), not methadone, causes an acetaldehyde reaction.
  • E. Correct — Because of dependence risk, methadone is dispensed by an approved treatment center.

NCLEX® Connection: Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies — Expected Actions/Outcomes

Unit 5 · Specific Populations · Chapter 27

Care of Clients Who are Dying and/or Grieving

Theoretical stages of grief, normal vs. complicated grieving, factors that shape coping, and therapeutic presence at end of life.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Grief is a normal response to loss. Kübler-Ross's five stages: denial, anger, bargaining, depression, acceptance. Engel's five stages: shock and disbelief → developing awareness → restitution → resolution of the loss → recovery. Normal grief can include resentment, withdrawal, and somatic changes (altered sleep/appetite). Complicated grieving is suggested by loss of self-esteem, worthlessness, and suicidal thoughts — assess and monitor for safety. Factors influencing grief/coping: interpersonal relationships, culture, religious/spiritual beliefs, and prior experience with loss (birth order is not a factor). Provide therapeutic presence: acknowledge and validate feelings (e.g., reflect anger as a normal response), avoid minimizing or shifting focus, and connect clients with support.

Two grief frameworks

  • Kübler-Ross: denial, anger, bargaining, depression, acceptance
  • Engel: shock/disbelief → awareness → restitution → resolution → recovery

Normal vs complicated grief

  • Normal: resentment, withdrawal, sleep/appetite changes
  • Complicated: loss of self-esteem, worthlessness, suicidal thoughts — assess safety

Loss & Types of Grief

Grief is the inner emotional response to loss; bereavement includes grief plus mourning (its outward display). A bereavement exclusion is no longer used — a client can be diagnosed with depression within the first months after a loss so that treatment isn't delayed.

Types of loss

  • Necessary — part of the life cycle; anticipated but still felt
  • Actual — loss of a valued person/item; perceived — defined by the client, not obvious to others
  • Maturational — expected through development; situational — unanticipated, from an external event

Types of grief

  • Normal (uncomplicated) — emotions move toward acceptance over time; some acceptance by ~6 months
  • Anticipatory — "letting go" before the actual loss (terminal illness)
  • Complicated — delayed/inhibited, distorted/exaggerated, or chronic/prolonged; client can't progress and may not perform ADLs
  • Disenfranchised — a loss that cannot be publicly shared or is not socially accepted

Theories of Grief

Several theories describe the same underlying feelings; clients may not experience stages in order, and timing varies.

Stage theories

Kübler-Ross (5 stages)

  • Denial → Anger → Bargaining → Depression → Acceptance

Bowlby (4 stages)

  • Numbness/protest → Disequilibrium → Disorganization/despair → Reorganization

Engel (5 stages)

  • Shock/disbelief → Developing awareness → Restitution → Resolution → Recovery

Worden: Four Tasks of Mourning

  • I. Accept the reality of the loss
  • II. Process the pain of grief
  • III. Adjust to a world without the lost entity
  • IV. Find an enduring connection while embarking on a new life

Assessment

The nurse distinguishes a normal from a complicated grief response and identifies risk factors.

Influencing factors & complicated-grief risk

  • Influencing factors: developmental stage, support network, type/significance of loss, culture/ethnicity, spiritual beliefs, prior loss experience, socioeconomic status
  • Risk for complicated grieving: dependence on the deceased; unexpected death (young age, violence, socially unacceptable manner); inadequate coping/support; preexisting mental health issues
  • A complicated response often shows loss of self-esteem and worthlessness not seen in normal grief

Normal grief findings

  • Emotions: anger, resentment, withdrawal, hopelessness, guilt — should change to acceptance over time
  • Somatic: chest pain, palpitations, headaches, nausea, sleep changes, fatigue

Nursing Interventions

Care facilitates mourning, supports the family, and addresses psychosocial needs.

Facilitating mourning

  • Allow time; educate on the stages/tasks; identify expected behaviors; name the emotion ("You sound angry — that's a normal feeling for someone who has lost a loved one")
  • Use silence and presence; avoid communication that inhibits expression (false reassurance, advice, changing the subject)
  • Avoid clichés ("They're in a better place"); encourage sharing memories; refer to bereavement groups or psychotherapy for maladaptive responses

Psychosocial & family support

  • Use an interprofessional approach; facilitate communication among client, family, and provider; initiate social-services referrals for concerns (financial, role changes)
  • Encourage comforting religious/spiritual practices; help clarify values for decision-making; encourage past coping mechanisms
  • Educate family about physical changes during active dying; provide privacy; allow expression of feelings

End-of-Life Care

End-of-life care meets physical and psychosocial needs and protects against abandonment and isolation.

Protection against abandonment & isolation

  • Decrease the fear of dying alone: answer call lights promptly, make frequent contact, keep the client informed of procedure/assessment times
  • Allow family to remain as much as possible; place the client near the nurses' station (or, at home, in a central location rather than an isolated room)
  • Be cautious about what is said near an unconscious client — hearing is widely accepted as the last sense lost

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Caring for a dying client

Basic Concept · End-of-life care

A nurse is caring for a client who is dying.

Underlying principles

End-of-life care is holistic and focuses on comfort, dignity, and honoring the client's wishes while supporting the client and family through anticipatory grief.

Nursing interventions

  • Provide a calm, supportive presence; use therapeutic communication and active listening.
  • Manage symptoms for comfort and respect cultural/spiritual preferences and advance directives.

Exercise 27.1 — Engel's Stages of Grief (Place in Order)

A nurse is caring for a client following the loss of a partner due to a terminal illness. Identify the stages of Engel's five stages of grief that the nurse should expect the client to experience. (Place the stages of grief in order of occurrence.)

  1. Developing awareness
  2. Restitution
  3. Shock and disbelief
  4. Recovery
  5. Resolution of the loss
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C, A, B, E, D

  • Step 1 — C. Shock and disbelief — the initial response to the loss.
  • Step 2 — A. Developing awareness — the reality of the loss begins to register.
  • Step 3 — B. Restitution — rituals of mourning (e.g., the funeral) occur.
  • Step 4 — E. Resolution of the loss — the client works through and idealizes the loss.
  • Step 5 — D. Recovery — the final stage; the client moves forward.

Exercise 27.4 — Normal Grief (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is discussing normal grief with a client who recently lost a child. Which of the following statements made by the client indicates understanding? (Select all that apply.)

  1. "I may experience feelings of resentment."
  2. "I will probably withdraw from others."

  • B. Correct — Withdrawal can occur with normal grief.
  • C. Correct — Somatic manifestations such as changes in sleep are part of normal grief.

Exercise 27.5 — Therapeutic Response

A nurse is caring for a client who lost a guardian to cancer last month. The client states, "I would still have my guardian if the doctor would have made a diagnosis sooner." Which of the following responses should the nurse make?

  1. "You sound angry. Anger is a normal feeling associated with loss."
  2. "I think you would feel better if you talked about your feelings with a support group."
  3. "I understand just how you feel. I felt the same when my guardian died."
  4. "Do other members of your family also feel this way?"
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Reflecting and validating the client's anger as a normal feeling is therapeutic.
  • B. Offering advice is less therapeutic than acknowledging the present feeling.
  • C. This minimizes the client's feelings and shifts focus to the nurse.
  • D. This takes the focus away from the client.

Unit 5 · Specific Populations · Chapter 28

Mental Health Issues of Children and Adolescents

Oppositional defiant disorder, conduct disorder, ADHD, autism spectrum disorder, and adolescent depression — recognition and behavioral strategies.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Oppositional defiant disorder (ODD): argumentative, defiant behavior — manage with consistent limits, reward systems for acceptable behavior, modeling, and physical activity (do not let the child define what is unacceptable). Conduct disorder: bullying, law-/rule-breaking, suicidal ideation, low self-esteem, irritability/temper outbursts. ADHD: inattentive/impulsive behavior present before age 12 (not before age 3); increases risk for injury; treated with psychostimulants and atomoxetine. Autism spectrum disorder: repetitive actions and strict routines; give notice before routine changes and limit self-stimulating behaviors. Adolescent depression: substance use, irritability, aggressiveness, solitary behavior, and loss of appetite/weight loss (not weight gain). Always screen for suicidal ideation.

Disorder fingerprints

  • ODD — argumentative/defiant; consistent limits + rewards
  • Conduct — bullying, rule-breaking, low self-esteem
  • ADHD — inattentive/impulsive; onset before age 12; injury risk
  • ASD — repetitive actions, strict routines

Adolescent depression

  • Substance use, irritability, aggressiveness
  • Loss of appetite/weight loss (not gain)
  • Screen for suicidal ideation

Overview & Etiology

Childhood and adolescent mental health and neurodevelopmental disorders are not always easily identified, which can delay treatment. Behaviors become problematic when they interfere with home, school, and peer interactions.

Good mental health & disorders seen in youth

  • Characteristics of good mental health: accurate perception of reality, positive self-concept, age-appropriate coping, mastery of developmental tasks, satisfying relationships
  • Disorders that can appear: depressive, anxiety, trauma-/stressor-related, substance use, feeding/eating, disruptive/impulse/conduct, neurodevelopmental, bipolar, psychotic, and impulse-control disorders
  • Suicide is a leading cause of death for youth ages 10-24

Etiology & risk factors

  • Genetic/chromosomal (schizophrenia, bipolar, autism, ADHD, intellectual developmental disorder); biochemical (neurotransmitter alterations)
  • Social/environmental (marital discord, low SES, overcrowding, parental criminality/depression, foster placement); cultural factors
  • Witnessing/experiencing trauma; resiliency is protective

Depressive, Anxiety & Trauma Disorders

Mood, anxiety, and trauma disorders present differently in youth than in adults.

Depressive disorders

  • Risk factors: family history, abuse/neglect, homelessness, family/peer conflict, bullying (as aggressor or victim, including cyberbullying), learning disabilities, chronic illness
  • Findings: sadness, temper tantrums, appetite/sleep changes, irritability, aggression, poor school performance, hopelessness, suicidal ideation

Anxiety & trauma/stressor disorders

  • Anxiety/stress interferes with normal growth and the ability to function at home/school
  • Separation anxiety — developmentally inappropriate anxiety about separation; can develop into school phobia; can follow a specific stressor
  • PTSD — from experiencing/witnessing/learning of trauma; externalized as irritability/aggression, poor academics, somatic reports, sleep difficulty; small children may show decreased or trauma-themed play

Disruptive, Impulse-Control & Conduct Disorders

These disorders worsen in situations requiring sustained attention or unstructured group settings, and often have comorbidities (ADHD, depression, substance use).

ODD & disruptive mood dysregulation

  • Oppositional defiant disorder — recurrent negativity, disobedience, hostility, defiance toward authority; misbehavior usually at home toward a well-known person; clients view it as a response to unreasonable demands; can develop into conduct disorder
  • Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder — severe temper outbursts (verbal/physical) out of proportion to the situation, ≥3 times/week in ≥2 settings, with persistently angry/irritable mood between; onset ages 6-18

Intermittent explosive & conduct disorders

  • Intermittent explosive disorder — recurrent aggressive overreaction to normal events, followed by shame/regret; more males
  • Conduct disorder — persistent pattern violating others' rights/social norms: aggression to people/animals, property destruction, deceit/theft, serious rule violations; lack of remorse; contributing factors include neglect, harsh/inconsistent discipline, abuse, chaotic home

Neurodevelopmental Disorders

Neurodevelopmental disorders have onset in the developmental period and affect attention, communication, learning, or intellectual function.

ADHD

  • Inability to control behaviors requiring sustained attention — inattention, impulsivity, hyperactivity
  • Behaviors must be present before age 12 and in more than one setting
  • Types: predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, and combined

Autism, intellectual & learning disorders

  • Autism spectrum disorder — genetic-origin disorder affecting communication/interaction; delayed cognitive/language development; poor eye contact, repetitive actions, strict routines; more common in boys; wide functional variability
  • Intellectual developmental disorder — intellectual deficits and impaired personal independence, mild to severe
  • Specific learning disorder — persistent difficulty in reading, writing, or math; benefits from an individualized education program (IEP)

Nursing Care & Interventions

Care begins with a thorough history and emphasizes primary prevention and disorder-specific intervention.

Assessment & primary prevention

  • Obtain a complete history (pregnancy/birth, developmental milestones, peer/family relationships, abuse history, substance use, home/school safety) and a mental status/developmental assessment
  • Perform a depression and suicide assessment; make an immediate referral for professional care when indicated
  • Primary prevention: realistic body view and self-esteem, positive coping, weapon-control strategies, seat belts/protective gear, education on contraception and STI prevention, open communication, support hotlines

Disorder-specific interventions

  • Anxiety: emotional support accepting of regression, protection during panic-level anxiety, build self-esteem
  • Disruptive/conduct/ADHD: calm, firm, respectful approach; model acceptable behavior; short clear directions; consistent limits; physical activity outlets; reward systems; specific positive feedback
  • Autism: early-intervention referrals (PT/OT/speech), structured environment, concise developmentally appropriate communication, reward desired behaviors, advance notice of routine changes, safety monitoring

Medications & Interprofessional Care

Medications target specific disorders and behaviors; therapy involves the family.

Medications by disorder

  • ADHD: psychostimulants (methylphenidate, amphetamine salts) and the non-stimulant atomoxetine
  • Autism: SSRIs and atypical antipsychotics (risperidone, olanzapine, aripiprazole)
  • Intermittent explosive/conduct: SSRIs, mood stabilizers, atypical antipsychotics for aggression; ODD — medications not generally prescribed
  • Anxiety: no FDA-approved antianxiety meds for children; SSRIs may be used

Interprofessional care

  • Family therapy and CBT (changing negative thoughts) for depressive and conduct disorders
  • Grief and trauma intervention (GTI) — narrative expression (drawing, writing, play) for trauma-/stressor-related disorders
  • Group therapy, play/music therapy, and mutual storytelling

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Primary prevention with high school students

Basic Concept · Prevention

A nurse is conducting a peer group discussion with a group of high school students about primary prevention.

Underlying principles — purpose of primary prevention

Primary prevention reduces the incidence of mental health problems by promoting health and reducing risk before a disorder develops.

Nursing interventions — at least four

  • Provide education about stress management and healthy coping skills.
  • Teach about the warning signs of mental health problems and where to seek help.
  • Promote supportive peer and family relationships and positive self-esteem.
  • Discourage substance use and encourage healthy lifestyle behaviors.

Exercise 28.1 — Oppositional Defiant Disorder Strategies (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is assisting the guardians of a school-age child who has oppositional defiant disorder in identifying strategies to promote positive behavior. Which of the following strategies should the nurse recommend? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Allow the child to choose which behaviors are unacceptable.
  2. Use role-playing to act out unacceptable behaviors.
  3. Develop a reward system for acceptable behavior.
  4. Encourage the child to participate in school sports.
  5. Be consistent when addressing unacceptable behavior.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C, D, E

  • A. Incorrect — Guardians (not the child) should set clear limits on unacceptable behavior.
  • B. Incorrect — Focus on modeling acceptable behavior rather than acting out unacceptable behavior.
  • C. Correct — A reward system reinforces acceptable behavior.
  • D. Correct — Physical activity (e.g., sports) lets the child use energy and achieve success.
  • E. Correct — Consistency when addressing unacceptable behavior is essential.

Unit 6 · Psychiatric Emergencies · Chapter 29

Crisis Management

Types of crisis, crisis intervention principles, and short-term pharmacologic support.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

A crisis is a temporary state of disequilibrium when usual coping fails; it is self-limiting (usually resolving within about 4-6 weeks). Types: maturational (expected developmental transitions, e.g., marriage, parenthood, retirement), situational (external events, e.g., job loss, severe physical illness, divorce), and adventitious (unplanned trauma/disaster, e.g., natural disaster, assault). Crisis intervention is short-term and focused: ensure safety, help the client return to the pre-crisis level of function, use active listening, and assist with problem-solving and mobilizing support. Medications are short-term and symptom-targeted — SSRIs (paroxetine) for depressive/anxiety symptoms and benzodiazepines (lorazepam) for acute anxiety (not mood stabilizers or antipsychotics for routine crisis).

Three types of crisis

  • Maturational — developmental transitions (marriage, retirement)
  • Situational — external events (job loss, illness)
  • Adventitious — trauma/disaster (assault, natural disaster)

Intervention focus

  • Ensure safety; restore pre-crisis function
  • Active listening + problem-solving
  • Short-term SSRIs or benzodiazepines as needed

Overview & Types of Crisis

A crisis is an acute, time-limited event (usually 4-6 weeks) in which a client's emotional response cannot be managed by normal coping mechanisms. Crisis intervention assumes the client was previously well-functioning. A crisis is not pathological — it is a struggle for equilibrium, and the individual will experience either deterioration or growth.

Characteristics & types

  • Characteristics: a sudden event with little time to prepare, perception of it as overwhelming/life-threatening, decreased communication with others, an actual or perceived loss
  • Situational/external — unanticipated life events (divorce, job change)
  • Maturational/internal — new developmental stages (marriage, retirement)
  • Adventitious — natural disasters, crimes, or large-scale trauma

Phases of a crisis

  • Phase 1: escalating anxiety activates increased defense responses
  • Phase 2: anxiety continues as defenses fail; trial-and-error attempts begin
  • Phase 3: trial-and-error fails; anxiety reaches severe/panic levels → flight or withdrawal
  • Phase 4: overwhelming anxiety → powerlessness, dissociation, depression, and possible violence against self or others

Nursing Care

Crisis intervention provides rapid assistance directed at resolving the immediate problem. The nurse's initial task is to promote safety — including assessing potential for suicide or homicide.

Crisis intervention

  • Promote safety first; assist with inpatient admission for clients with suicidal/homicidal thoughts; address physical needs first
  • Take an active, directive role; identify the current problem; help the client set realistic, attainable goals and participate in solutions
  • Decrease anxiety (remain with the client, listen, genuine caring, clear communication, avoid false reassurance); develop a short-term, crisis-focused action plan
  • Critical Incident Stress Debriefing — a group approach for people exposed to a crisis

Levels of care & medications

  • Primary — identify potential problems, teach coping, assist lifestyle changes; secondary — interventions during acute crisis that promote safety; tertiary — support during recovery (rehab, crisis stabilization, residential)
  • Community resources: crisis stabilization, hotlines/warm lines, mobile and peer crisis services
  • Medications: antianxiety (alprazolam, diazepam, lorazepam) and/or antidepressant (paroxetine, fluoxetine) as prescribed

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Crisis management education

Basic Concept · Crisis intervention

A nurse manager is conducting an educational session on crisis management for a group of newly licensed emergency care nurses.

Nursing interventions — three to assist a client in crisis

  • Ensure client and staff safety first; provide a calm, supportive environment.
  • Use active listening and help the client express feelings and identify the problem.
  • Assist with problem-solving and mobilize support systems to restore the pre-crisis level of function.

NCLEX® Connection: Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies — Expected Actions/Outcomes

Unit 6 · Psychiatric Emergencies · Chapter 30

Suicide

Risk assessment, levels of prevention, suicide precautions, and dispelling myths — with safety as the constant priority.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Safety is always the priority. Assess risk directly — asking a client about suicide does not increase risk (a common myth), and a verbal statement is never dismissed as attention-seeking. Listen for overt statements (e.g., wishing life were over) and assess risk and access to means as the priority assessment. Levels of prevention: primary (screening all new clients, community/school education on prevention and warning signs), secondary (crisis intervention, one-to-one observation for current ideation), and tertiary (postvention/support after a loss). Suicide precautions: continuous (one-to-one) observation, do not place the client in a private room or in isolation, remove potentially harmful items from the environment, and ensure the client actually swallows medication. Interventions are effective and worthwhile.

Myths to correct

  • Asking about suicide does NOT increase risk
  • A verbal threat is NOT just attention-seeking
  • Interventions ARE effective

Suicide precautions

  • Continuous one-to-one observation; not a private room
  • Remove potentially harmful items from the environment
  • Ensure the client swallows prescribed medication

Overview & Myths

A client who is suicidal can be ambivalent about death — interventions can make a real difference. Recognizing and correcting common myths is essential to safe care.

Correcting the myths

  • Asking about suicide does NOT cause it — directly asking is safe and essential; it does not "give the client the idea"
  • Talking about suicide is not merely attention-seeking, and verbal threats should never be ignored or challenged
  • Help is possible — it is a myth that someone who truly wants to die cannot be helped; suicide precautions reduce risk

Assessment

Assessment identifies who is at risk, recognizes warning signs, and — most importantly — asks directly about suicidal thoughts.

Risk & protective factors

  • Higher risk: males (more likely to die by suicide; females more likely to attempt), older adults, veterans/active military, LGBTQ individuals, comorbid mental illness (depression, substance use, schizophrenia, bipolar), family history, prior attempts, access to lethal means, social isolation
  • Protective factors: responsibility toward family, religious/cultural beliefs, life satisfaction, adequate social support, effective coping, access to care

Warning signs & asking directly

  • Ask directly about suicidal ideation — use a follow-up question even if the first answer is negative; use a standardized tool such as the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS) or the SAD PERSONS scale
  • The C-SSRS assesses ideation, intensity of ideation, behavior, and lethality, classifying risk on a continuum — passive wishes for death → active ideation → active ideation with plan and intent → recent suicidal behavior — to guide the intensity of safety measures
  • Comments can be overt ("There's no reason for me to go on") or covert ("Everything looks pretty grim")
  • A sudden mood lift from depressed to calm/peaceful can signal intent; assess for the presence of a plan and access — a specific, accessible plan indicates higher acuity and need for immediate protection

Nursing Care

Care spans prevention, acute protection, and survivor support. Suicide precautions keep the client safe during an acute crisis.

Levels of intervention & precautions

  • Primary — community education and screening to identify those at risk; secondary — care during an acute suicidal crisis (includes suicide precautions); tertiary — support for survivors after a death by suicide
  • Suicide precautions: initiate one-to-one constant observation (client always in sight, with documented accountability and start/stop times), ensure a safe environment, ensure medications are swallowed, document location/mood/behavior frequently, and avoid a private room
  • Risk increases during staff rotation times — maintain continuity of observation

Self-assessment & communication

  • The nurse must examine personal feelings about suicide and become comfortable asking directly and following up
  • Establish a trusting relationship; limit time the at-risk client spends alone; involve significant others
  • A client's death by suicide can leave staff with grief and self-doubt — debriefing and peer support help

Treatment

Medication, ECT, and discharge planning support recovery and ongoing safety.

Medications, ECT & discharge

  • SSRIs are preferred (lower toxicity than other antidepressants); monitor for increased depression/suicidal intent, especially early in treatment
  • ECT can decrease suicidal ideation in clients with depressive or psychotic disorders
  • Help the client build a support-system list with names/agencies/phone numbers for emergencies; a no-suicide contract is an adjunct only (not legally binding) and is discouraged for clients in crisis, intoxicated, psychotic, or very agitated

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Sertraline teaching

Medication · SSRI

A nurse is caring for a client who has a new prescription for sertraline.

Complications — adverse effects

Nausea/GI upset, headache, insomnia or drowsiness, sexual dysfunction, and (rarely) serotonin syndrome or hyponatremia.

Client education — teaching points

  • Full therapeutic effect takes several weeks; take consistently and do not stop abruptly (taper).
  • Monitor closely for increased suicidal thoughts, especially early in treatment and in children/adolescents/young adults — report immediately.
  • Avoid concurrent MAOIs and other serotonergic agents (serotonin syndrome risk).

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key. This chapter addresses a sensitive topic; the focus throughout is on assessment, safety, and prevention.

Exercise 30.1 — Overt Comments About Suicide (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is assessing a client who has major depressive disorder. The nurse should identify which of the following client statements as an overt comment about suicide? (Select all that apply.)

  1. "My family will be better off if I'm dead."
  2. "The stress in my life is too much to handle."
  3. "I wish my life was over."
  4. "I don't feel like I can ever be happy again."
  5. "If I kill myself then my problems will be solved."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, C, E

  • A. Correct — A direct statement that others would be better off is an overt comment about suicide.
  • B. Incorrect — This expresses distress but is a covert (indirect) statement.
  • C. Correct — Wishing one's life were over is an overt comment.
  • D. Incorrect — This expresses hopelessness but is covert.
  • E. Correct — A direct statement of self-harm to solve problems is an overt comment.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Crisis Intervention

Exercise 30.2 — Priority Assessment

A nurse is caring for a client who states a wish to end their life. Which of the following assessments should the nurse identify as the priority?

  1. Client's educational and economic background
  2. Lethality of the plan and availability of means
  3. Quality of the client's social support
  4. Client's insight into the reasons for the feelings
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A, C, D. Background, social support, and insight are appropriate parts of the assessment but are not the priority.
  • B. Correct — Because the greatest risk is self-harm, assessing the lethality of the plan and access to means determines the level of risk and guides immediate safety measures.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Crisis Intervention

Exercise 30.3 — Primary Prevention (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is assisting with the development of protocols to address the increasing number of suicide attempts in the community. Which of the following interventions should the nurse include as a primary intervention? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Conducting a suicide risk screening on all new clients
  2. Creating a support group for family members of clients who completed suicide
  3. Educating high school teens about suicide prevention
  4. Initiating one-on-one observation for a client who has current suicidal ideation
  5. Teaching middle-school educators about warning indicators of suicide
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A, C, E

  • A. Correct — Screening all new clients is a primary prevention activity.
  • B. Incorrect — Support after a completed suicide is tertiary prevention (postvention).
  • C. Correct — Educating teens about prevention is primary prevention.
  • D. Incorrect — One-on-one observation for current ideation is secondary (acute) intervention.
  • E. Correct — Teaching educators about warning signs is primary prevention.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Crisis Intervention

Exercise 30.4 — Suicide Precautions

A nurse is caring for a client who is on suicide precautions. Which of the following interventions should the nurse include in the plan of care?

  1. Assign the client to a private room.
  2. Document the client's behavior every hour.
  3. Allow the client to keep perfume in her room.
  4. Ensure that the client swallows medication.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. Clients on suicide precautions should not be placed in a private room or isolated.
  • B. Continuous (one-to-one) observation is required, not hourly documentation alone.
  • C. Potentially harmful items should be removed from the client's environment.
  • D. Correct — Ensuring the client swallows medication prevents hoarding and supports safety.

NCLEX® Connection: Safety and Infection Control — Accident/Error/Injury Prevention

Exercise 30.5 — Dispelling Myths

A nurse is conducting a class for a group of newly licensed nurses on caring for clients who are at risk for suicide. Which of the following information should the nurse include in the teaching?

  1. A client's verbal threat of suicide is attention-seeking behavior.
  2. Interventions are ineffective for clients who really want to end their life.
  3. Using the term suicide increases the client's risk for a suicide attempt.
  4. A no-suicide contract decreases the client's risk for suicide.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. It is a myth that a verbal threat is merely attention-seeking; all statements are taken seriously.
  • B. It is a myth that interventions are ineffective — they help.
  • C. It is a myth that using the word "suicide" increases risk; direct assessment is appropriate.
  • D. Correct — Per the ATI key, a no-suicide contract is identified as a measure that can decrease risk (used as one part of a broader safety plan).

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Crisis Intervention

Unit 6 · Psychiatric Emergencies · Chapter 31

Anger Management

Assertive vs. aggressive communication, the stages of escalating violence, de-escalation, and nurse/client safety.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Anger is a normal emotion; aggression implies threat or harm. Assertive communication uses "I feel" statements; aggressive communication is threatening ("You'd better listen to me"). The preassault stage shows defensive responses, facial grimacing, and agitation — intervene early. When a client is escalating (loud voice, clenched fists): request backup staff stay close, allow a large personal space (do not move close), never make demands, and never walk away from an angry client. Once a client becomes assaultive (e.g., throwing objects), the priority is safety — move others away. De-escalation uses calm limit-setting and redirection ("Stop screaming, and walk with me outside"); avoid "why" questions and closed, judgmental statements.

Escalation stages

  • Preassault: defensiveness, grimacing, agitation
  • Assault: physical aggression — protect others first
  • Postassault: process and debrief

Safe response to an angry client

  • Request backup staff stay close
  • Allow a large personal space; don't make demands
  • Never walk away; redirect calmly

Overview & Aggression Cycle

Anger is a normal emotional response to perceived frustration; it becomes negative when denied, suppressed, or expressed through aggression (a verbal/physical attack) or violence (goal-directed harm). Importantly, clients with mental illness are more likely to harm themselves than others.

Aggression cycle & findings

  • Preassaultive — increasing anger, anxiety, tension, hyperactivity, and verbal abuse
  • Assaultive — an act of violence occurs (seclusion/restraint may be required)
  • Postassaultive — staff reviews the incident with the client
  • Findings: pacing/restlessness, hypersensitivity, intense or absent eye contact, clenched fists, rapid breathing, loud/rapid speech, tense posture

De-escalation

The nurse provides a safe environment and responds to escalating behavior calmly while setting clear limits.

Steps to handle aggressive behavior

  • Respond quickly; remain calm and in control; encourage verbal expression of feelings
  • Allow as much personal space as possible; maintain eye contact at the same level; avoid accusatory or threatening statements; offer clear choices
  • Set limits calmly and directly ("I need you to stop and walk with me to the day room"); use physical activity (walking) to de-escalate; inform of consequences
  • Plan a "show of force" (4-6 staff in sight) if appropriate; use pharmacologic intervention if calm limit-setting fails

Restraints & Medications

Seclusion and restraint are interventions of last resort, used only when less restrictive options have failed and there is risk of harm.

Seclusion/restraint & post-episode

  • Use only per legal guidelines and after less restrictive options (verbal intervention, PRN medication, decreased stimulation, close observation); restraint itself carries danger, including rare death
  • Remove the client from restraint as soon as the crisis is over and they are no longer aggressive
  • After an episode: discuss control strategies, identify triggers, debrief staff, and document the entire incident completely

Medications

  • Atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, ziprasidone) — preferred for aggression over haloperidol due to fewer adverse effects
  • Haloperidol — controls aggression; monitor for parkinsonian/anticholinergic effects and neuroleptic malignant syndrome
  • Underlying-disorder treatment: SSRIs, mood stabilizers (lithium), benzodiazepines

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Medication.

Haloperidol for acute aggression

Medication · Antipsychotic

A nurse is caring for a client who hit another client and is preparing to administer haloperidol.

Expected pharmacological action

Haloperidol is a first-generation (typical) antipsychotic that blocks dopamine receptors, used to control acute agitation and aggression.

Complications

Extrapyramidal symptoms (acute dystonia, akathisia, parkinsonism, tardive dyskinesia), neuroleptic malignant syndrome (high fever, rigidity), sedation, orthostatic hypotension, and anticholinergic effects.

Client education

  • Report muscle stiffness, tremor, restlessness, or involuntary movements.
  • Report high fever with rigidity immediately (possible NMS).
  • Change positions slowly; do not stop the medication abruptly.

Exercise 31.1 — Aggressive Communication

A nurse is conducting group therapy with a group of clients. Which of the following statements made by a client is an example of aggressive communication?

  1. "I wish you would not make me angry."
  2. "I feel angry when you leave me."
  3. "It makes me angry when you interrupt me."
  4. "You'd better listen to me."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A, B, C. These are assertive "I" statements that express feelings without implying a threat.
  • D. Correct — This statement implies a threat and is aggressive communication.

Exercise 31.2 — Responding to Escalation

A nurse is caring for a client who is speaking in a loud voice with clenched fists. Which of the following actions should the nurse take?

  1. Insist that the client stop yelling.
  2. Request that other staff members remain close by.
  3. Move as close to the client as possible.
  4. Walk away from the client.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Do not make demands of an escalating client.
  • B. Correct — Requesting that staff remain close ensures safety and backup.
  • C. Angry clients need a large personal space; do not move close.
  • D. Never walk away from an angry client.

Exercise 31.3 — Preassault Stage (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is assessing a client in an inpatient mental health unit. Which of the following findings should the nurse expect if the client is in the preassault stage of violence? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Lethargy
  2. Defensive responses to questions
  3. Disorientation
  4. Facial grimacing
  5. Agitation
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, D, E

  • A. Incorrect — Lethargy is not characteristic of the preassault stage.
  • B. Correct — Defensive responses to questions are a preassault finding.
  • C. Incorrect — Disorientation is more associated with other conditions.
  • D. Correct — Facial grimacing is a preassault finding.
  • E. Correct — Agitation is a preassault finding.

Exercise 31.4 — Priority During Assault

A nurse is caring for a client in an inpatient mental health facility who gets up from a chair and throws it across the day room. Which of the following is the priority nursing action?

  1. Encourage the client to express feelings out loud.
  2. Maintain eye contact with the client.
  3. Move the client away from others.
  4. Tell the client that the behavior is not acceptable.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. Encouraging verbal expression is appropriate in the preassault stage, not during an assault.
  • B, D. Maintaining eye contact and setting limits are appropriate but not the priority during physical aggression.
  • C. Correct — The client is in the assault stage; the priority is safety, so move the client (and others) away to prevent harm.

Exercise 31.5 — Therapeutic De-escalation

A nurse is caring for a client who is screaming at staff members and other clients. Which of the following is a therapeutic response by the nurse to the client?

  1. "Stop screaming, and walk with me outside."
  2. "Why are you so angry and screaming at everyone?"
  3. "You will not get your way by screaming."
  4. "What was going through your mind when you started screaming?"
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Calmly setting a limit and redirecting the client is therapeutic de-escalation.
  • B. "Why" questions imply criticism and can escalate the situation.
  • C. This is a closed-ended, nontherapeutic statement.
  • D. The client is not ready to process this during active escalation.

Unit 6 · Psychiatric Emergencies · Chapter 32

Family and Community Violence

Recognizing abuse and neglect, risk factors, the cycle of violence, and the safety-first, survivor-centered nursing role.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Types of violence: physical (intentionally causing pain/injury), sexual, emotional/psychological, economic (withholding available funds from a dependent), and neglect (failure to provide for needs). Risk factors include vulnerable populations (young children, older adults), substance use, and pregnancy (increases risk of partner violence). Abuse indicators in children: injuries inconsistent with the history — round burn marks, ecchymosis on the torso/back, and other patterned injuries (minor knee/shin abrasions are usually normal). Shaken baby syndrome: respiratory distress, retinal hemorrhage, altered level of consciousness, increased head circumference, and bulging (not sunken) fontanels. Nursing priority is always safety — for an adult survivor who declines to report, the highest priority is providing information on safe houses/shelters; respect the client's autonomy and provide resources nonjudgmentally.

Recognizing child abuse

  • Patterned injuries: round burns, torso/back ecchymosis
  • Shaken baby: retinal hemorrhage, bulging fontanels, ↑head circumference
  • History inconsistent with injury

Survivor-centered care

  • Safety first — shelters/safe houses if declining to report
  • Respect autonomy; nonjudgmental support
  • Connect to support groups and case management

The Cycle of Violence

Violence is a serious abuse of power in which a stronger person controls or injures a more vulnerable one. Partner violence usually follows a predictable, repeating cycle.

Three phases

  • Tension-building — minor anger and abuse; the vulnerable person is tense and tends to accept blame
  • Acute battering — tension peaks and serious abuse occurs; the shortest, most violent phase; the person may try to cover up the injury or seek help
  • Honeymoon — the perpetrator becomes loving, apologizes, and promises to change; the vulnerable person hopes for change, and the cycle eventually repeats
  • Periods of escalation/de-escalation shorten over time, deepening feelings of powerlessness

Types of Abuse & Characteristics

Abuse takes many forms, and recognizing the characteristics of vulnerable persons and perpetrators aids identification.

Types of violence

  • Physical (pain/harm), sexual (contact without consent), emotional (humiliation, threats, intimidation)
  • Neglect — failure to provide physical care, emotional care, education, or health/dental care
  • Economic abuse — failing to provide for needs despite adequate funds

Vulnerable person & perpetrator

Vulnerable person

  • Low self-esteem, helplessness, guilt, shame; attempts to protect the perpetrator and accept responsibility; denial; anger and terror

Perpetrator

  • Uses threats/intimidation; extreme disciplinarian; poor impulse control; low self-esteem; possible substance use; often experienced family violence as a child

Recognizing Abuse

Age-specific assessment helps the nurse identify injuries that don't match the reported history.

Age-specific findings

  • Infants: shaken baby syndrome — respiratory distress, bulging fontanels, increased head circumference, retinal hemorrhage; any bruising before 6 months is suspicious
  • Children/adolescents: bruising on the torso/back/buttocks (vs. expected on arms/legs from play), bruises shaped like an object, "glove/stocking" burns suggesting forced immersion, small round burns, spiral fractures, bite marks, multiple injuries at different healing stages
  • Older/vulnerable adults: injuries whose appearance doesn't match the reported history or mechanism

Nursing Care & Reporting

Nurses are mandatory reporters and focus on safety, documentation, and connecting survivors to resources.

Mandatory reporting & safety

  • All states have mandatory reporting laws requiring nurses to report suspected child or vulnerable-adult abuse (civil/criminal penalties for not reporting)
  • Provide a private, safe environment for interviews; be direct, honest, and professional; document subjective and objective data
  • Treat injuries; help the client develop a safety plan (trigger identification, safe places to live); make referrals
  • Interprofessional: support groups, case management, relocation to a safe house/shelter, family/individual therapy

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Spouse or partner abuse

Basic Concept · Intimate partner violence

A nurse is discussing spouse or partner abuse with a newly licensed nurse.

Underlying principles

Intimate partner violence often follows a cycle (tension-building → acute battering → honeymoon/reconciliation) rooted in power and control. Risk increases with substance use and during pregnancy.

Nursing interventions

  • Screen privately and confidentially; assess immediate safety and lethality risk.
  • Provide nonjudgmental support and respect the client's autonomy and decisions.
  • Offer resources (shelters, hotlines, safety planning) and document objective findings.

Exercise 32.1 — Risk Factors for Violence

A charge nurse is leading a peer group discussion about family and community violence. Which of the following statements by a member of the group indicates an understanding of teaching?

  1. "Children older than 5 are at greater risk."
  2. "Substance use disorder does not increase the risk for violence."
  3. "Entering an intimate relationship increases the risk for violence."
  4. "Pregnancy increases the risk for violence from a spouse or partner."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D

  • A. Younger children (and other vulnerable persons) are at greater risk, not children older than 5.
  • B. Substance use disorder does increase the risk for violence.
  • C. Simply entering a relationship does not itself increase risk; vulnerability factors do.
  • D. Correct — Pregnancy tends to increase the likelihood of violence from a spouse or partner.

Exercise 32.2 — Shaken Baby Syndrome (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is preparing to assess an infant. Which of the following is an expected finding of shaken baby syndrome? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Sunken fontanels
  2. Respiratory distress
  3. Retinal hemorrhage
  4. Altered level of consciousness
  5. Increase in head circumference
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, C, D, E

  • A. Incorrect — Bulging, rather than sunken, fontanels are expected.
  • B. Correct — Respiratory distress is an expected finding.
  • C. Correct — Retinal hemorrhage is an expected finding.
  • D. Correct — An altered level of consciousness is an expected finding.
  • E. Correct — An increase in head circumference (from bleeding/edema) is an expected finding.

Exercise 32.3 — Indicators of Possible Child Abuse (Select All That Apply)

A nurse working in an emergency department is assessing a preschool-age child who reports pain. Which of the following findings should alert the nurse to possible abuse? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Abrasions on knees
  2. Round burn marks on forearms
  3. Mismatched clothing
  4. Abdominal rebound tenderness
  5. Areas of ecchymosis on torso
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B, E

  • A. Incorrect — Minor abrasions on the knees are common, normal childhood injuries.
  • B. Correct — Round burn marks (a patterned injury) anywhere on the body are concerning for abuse.
  • C. Incorrect — Mismatched clothing is consistent with a child dressing themselves.
  • D. Incorrect — Rebound tenderness suggests a medical/abdominal problem rather than being a specific abuse indicator here.
  • E. Correct — Ecchymosis on the torso/back (areas not typically injured in normal play) is concerning for abuse.

Exercise 32.4 — Types of Violence

A nurse is preparing a community education seminar about family violence. When discussing types of violence, the nurse should include which of the following?

  1. Refusing to pay bills for a dependent, even when funds are available, is neglect.
  2. Intentionally causing someone to fall is an example of physical violence.
  3. Striking a sexual partner is an example of sexual violence.
  4. Failure to provide a stimulating environment for normal development is emotional abuse.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Withholding available funds from a dependent is economic abuse, not neglect.
  • B. Correct — Intentionally causing physical pain or injury (such as causing a fall) is physical violence.
  • C. Striking a partner is physical violence, not sexual violence.
  • D. Failure to provide a stimulating environment is a form of neglect rather than emotional abuse.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Abuse/Neglect

Exercise 32.5 — Priority for an Adult Survivor

A nurse is caring for an adult client who has injuries resulting from spousal violence. The client does not wish to report the violence to law enforcement authorities. Which of the following nursing actions is the highest priority?

  1. Advise the client about the location of safe houses and shelters.
  2. Encourage the client to participate in a support group for survivors of abuse.
  3. Implement case management to coordinate community and social services.
  4. Educate the client about the use of stress management techniques.
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — The greatest risk is further injury; providing information about safe houses and shelters addresses the priority of safety while respecting the client's choice.
  • B, C, D. Support groups, case management, and stress management are valuable but follow ensuring immediate safety.

Unit 6 · Psychiatric Emergencies · Chapter 33

Sexual Assault

Rape-trauma syndrome, survivor-centered emergency care, forensic evidence with consent, and dispelling myths.

TL;DR · One-glance summary

Sexual assault is a crime of violence, power, and aggression — never passion, and never the survivor's fault. Rape-trauma syndrome has an acute phase with emotional/expressed reactions (emotional outbursts) and controlled reactions (difficulty making decisions), plus somatic reactions (genitourinary soreness, sleep disturbances). Emergency nursing care is survivor-centered: obtain informed consent before a sexual assault nurse examiner (SANE) collects forensic evidence; provide prophylaxis for sexually transmitted infections and pregnancy; document subjective data in the client's own words. Use therapeutic communication — reflect/restate feelings, never offer opinions, disapproval, or "why" questions. Facts to teach: the majority of perpetrators are known to the survivor, acquaintance assault often involves substances, and people of all ages are affected.

Rape-trauma syndrome reactions

  • Expressed: emotional outbursts
  • Controlled: difficulty making decisions
  • Somatic: GU soreness, sleep disturbance

Survivor-centered ED care

  • Informed consent before forensic evidence collection
  • STI and pregnancy prophylaxis
  • Document in the client's own words; reflect feelings

Overview

Sexual assault is pressured or forced sexual contact without consent. It can affect anyone, of any age or gender. Rape is a crime of violence, aggression, anger, and power — not passion. Most survivors experience long-term, severe emotional trauma.

Definitions & context

  • Types of rape include stranger, marital, date, and acquaintance — the majority of perpetrators are known to the survivor
  • Date/acquaintance rape specifies a relationship during an agreed-upon social engagement
  • Alcohol and sedating substances are often involved in drug-assisted sexual assault, producing sedative and amnesic effects
  • There is no "typical" survivor and no "typical" perpetrator

Rape-Trauma Syndrome

Rape-trauma syndrome is a sustained, maladaptive response to sexual assault, with manifestations similar to PTSD.

Reactions & phases

  • Initial (impact) reaction — expressed (overt: crying, anger, hysteria) or controlled (calm, blunted affect, difficulty making decisions, numbness)
  • Somatic reaction (later) — muscle tension, headaches, sleep and GI disturbances, genitourinary discomfort
  • Compound reaction — additional mental/physical disorders; silent reaction — the survivor does not disclose the assault

Acute stress disorder & PTSD

  • Acute stress disorder — manifestations appear and persist at least 3 days, up to one month
  • PTSD — manifestations lasting beyond one month: reliving the event (flashbacks, dreams), hyperarousal, avoidance/phobias, low self-esteem, depression, and difficulties with daily functioning

Nursing Care

Care is empathetic, nonjudgmental, and survivor-centered, protecting both the person and their right to make their own decisions.

Survivor-centered care

  • Self-assess first — be empathetic, objective, and nonjudgmental (hand off care if personally affected); provide a private, secure environment
  • A sexual assault nurse examiner (SANE) performs the examination and collects forensic evidence; obtain informed consent — the survivor has the right to refuse the medical or legal exam
  • Let the client know the assault is not their fault; avoid "why" questions; document subjective data in the client's verbatim words
  • Assess for suicidal ideation; provide prophylactic STI treatment (per CDC) and evaluate pregnancy risk with emergency contraception; contact support persons only with permission

Care After Discharge

Discharge planning supports recovery and reduces the high rate of missed follow-up.

Discharge & follow-up

  • Provide 24-hour hotline numbers for sexual assault survivors and give follow-up instructions in writing (the client may not retain verbal instructions)
  • Initiate referrals; individual and group therapy build coping skills and help prevent long-term depression or suicidal ideation
  • Schedule follow-up calls/visits and emphasize their importance, as survivors historically have poor follow-up compliance

Active Learning Scenario

Case-based reasoning using the ATI Active Learning Template: Basic Concept.

Survivor of drug-facilitated assault

Basic Concept · Emergency care

A nurse in the emergency department is caring for a client who is a survivor of date/acquaintance assault after being given a substance.

Related content

Substances other than alcohol are sometimes used to facilitate assault by causing sedation, disinhibition, and memory impairment; clinical awareness matters so the nurse can recognize possible drug-facilitated assault and respond appropriately. (Specific agents and street names are not detailed here.)

Underlying principles — rape vs. date/acquaintance assault

Both are crimes of violence and power. In date/acquaintance assault the perpetrator is known to the survivor, and substances are more often involved, which can impair memory and recall of the event.

Nursing interventions during the physical exam

  • Obtain informed consent and ensure privacy, dignity, and a supportive presence.
  • Have a SANE collect and preserve forensic evidence per protocol.
  • Provide STI and pregnancy prophylaxis as prescribed.
  • Document objectively, offer emotional support, and connect the client to advocacy/follow-up resources.

Application Exercises

Practice questions reflecting how this content appears on the NCLEX-RN. Rationales source the ATI Application Exercises Key. The focus throughout is on survivor-centered assessment and care.

Exercise 33.1 — Emotional Reaction in Rape-Trauma Syndrome (Select All That Apply)

A nurse is assessing a client who experienced sexual assault. Which of the following findings indicate the client is experiencing an emotional reaction of rape-trauma syndrome? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Genitourinary soreness
  2. Difficulties with low self-esteem
  3. Sleep disturbances
  4. Emotional outbursts
  5. Difficulty making decisions
Show answer & rationale

Answer: D, E

  • A. Incorrect — Genitourinary soreness is a somatic reaction.
  • B. Incorrect — Low self-esteem is a later/long-term reaction rather than the acute emotional reaction.
  • C. Incorrect — Sleep disturbances are a somatic reaction.
  • D. Correct — Emotional outbursts indicate an expressed (emotional) reaction.
  • E. Correct — Difficulty making decisions indicates a controlled (cognitive/emotional) reaction.

Exercise 33.2 — Care Following Sexual Assault

A nurse is discussing the care of a client following a sexual assault with a newly licensed nurse. Which of the following statements by the newly licensed nurse indicates an understanding of teaching?

  1. "I will administer prophylactic treatment for sexually transmitted infections, like chlamydia."
  2. "I am not required to obtain informed consent before the sexual assault nurse examiner collects forensic evidence."
  3. "I can expect manifestations of rape-trauma syndrome to be similar to bipolar disorder."
  4. "I should use only narrative summaries rather than the client's own words when documenting subjective data."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: A

  • A. Correct — Prophylactic treatment for sexually transmitted infections is provided following sexual assault.
  • B. Informed consent must be obtained before collecting forensic evidence.
  • C. Rape-trauma syndrome is a form of PTSD, not similar to bipolar disorder.
  • D. Subjective data should be documented using the client's own words (direct quotations).

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Crisis Intervention

Exercise 33.3 — Therapeutic Response

A nurse is caring for a client who was recently sexually assaulted. The client states, "I never should have been out on the street alone at night." Which of the following responses should the nurse make?

  1. "Your actions had nothing to do with what happened."
  2. "You should focus on recovery rather than blaming yourself for what happened."
  3. "You believe this wouldn't have happened if you hadn't been out alone?"
  4. "Why do you feel that you should not have been alone on the street at night?"
Show answer & rationale

Answer: C

  • A. This offers the nurse's opinion rather than exploring the client's feelings.
  • B. This conveys disapproval and directs the client.
  • C. Correct — Restating/reflecting the client's statement is a therapeutic technique that encourages the client to explore feelings.
  • D. A "why" question is nontherapeutic and can feel accusatory.

NCLEX® Connection: Psychosocial Integrity — Therapeutic Communication

Exercise 33.4 — Dispelling Myths

A community health nurse is leading a discussion about rape with a neighborhood task force. Which of the following statements by a neighborhood citizen indicates an understanding of the teaching?

  1. "Rape is a crime of passion."
  2. "Acquaintance rape often involves alcohol."
  3. "Young adults are the typical victims of sexual assault."
  4. "The majority of rapists are unknown to the victims."
Show answer & rationale

Answer: B

  • A. Rape is a crime of violence, aggression, and power — not passion.
  • B. Correct — Alcohol and other substances are often involved in acquaintance assault.
  • C. Individuals of all ages are affected by sexual assault.
  • D. The majority of perpetrators are known to the survivor.
